We replace future RGB and geometry with learned mask latents and train a lightweight adapter to turn the retrospective tracking representation into a forward predictor, while freezing the large video and tracking components. Using just 40k human videos and no auxiliary inputs such as language, MotionForesight generalizes across diverse out-of-distribution objects, environments, viewpoints, and interactions. It also outperforms substantially larger models that use over a million training videos. These results show that we can efficiently re-purpose video priors into explicit geometric forecasts for embodied intelligence. https://motionforesight.github.io/

</details>


### [76] [Knowing the Self, Understanding the World: A Dual-Cognition Benchmark for UAV Spatio-temporal Reasoning with MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16193)
*Like Liu,Zhengzheng Xu,Haitao He,Hongzhe Li,Shuchang Zhang,Dian Shao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UAV-DualCog introduces a benchmark for aerial multimodal LLMs to evaluate dual cognition—self-state and environment-state reasoning—in multiview spatio-temporal contexts, with image/video tasks and an automated semantic point-cloud data pipeline; results show current MLLMs struggle with self-state, viewpoint transformation, spatial grounding, and temporal localization, though the dataset is scalable and useful for supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a gap in UAV-focused MLLMs evaluation: existing benchmarks emphasize scene understanding or navigation but not joint self-state and environment-state reasoning in 3D, multiview, and temporal frames. A scalable, automated dataset is needed to drive progress in dual-cognition UAV agents.

Method: Construct UAV-DualCog with image and video tasks requiring self-state and environment-state reasoning and grounding beyond discrete answers. Develop an automated pipeline to generate data from scene-level semantic point clouds, creating a scalable benchmark across diverse scenes, landmarks, and QA samples. Create UAV-DualCog-Train from disjoint scenes to provide structured supervision and probe lightweight optimization for useful training signals.

Result: Current MLLMs lag in UAV dual cognition. Self-state reasoning, viewpoint transformation, precise spatial grounding, and temporal interval localization are persistent bottlenecks. Human baselines indicate the task is understandable but challenging for models. The training variant UAV-DualCog-Train offers structured supervision and shows potential as a data resource.

Conclusion: UAV-DualCog is a valid evaluation benchmark and data resource to advance MLLM-based UAV agents, highlighting key bottlenecks and guiding future research in dual-cognition, 3D grounding, and spatio-temporal reasoning. The project website provides access to data and materials.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models have achieved strong performance across diverse vision-language tasks, yet their capabilities in UAV scenarios remain insufficiently explored. Recent UAV-oriented benchmarks have begun to evaluate MLLMs in aerial scenarios, but they typically focus on scene understanding, event recognition, or navigation completion, rather than jointly assessing the dual-cognition capability required for UAV agents: reasoning about both the UAV's own state and the external environment in multiview spatio-temporal contexts. To address this gap, we present UAV-DualCog, a benchmark for aerial multiview spatio-temporal reasoning built on this dual-cognition perspective. UAV-DualCog includes both image and video tasks to jointly evaluate self-state and environment-state reasoning, while requiring spatial or temporal grounding beyond discrete answer prediction. We also develop an automated pipeline that constructs data from scene-level semantic point clouds, yielding a scalable benchmark with diverse scenes, hundreds of landmarks, and thousands of QA samples. Extensive evaluations show that current MLLMs remain far from reliable in UAV dual cognition. Self-state reasoning, viewpoint transformation, precise spatial grounding, and temporal interval localization are persistent bottlenecks, and additional validation with thinking/frontier models and a human baseline confirms that the benchmark is understandable to humans but challenging for existing models. We further construct UAV-DualCog-Train from disjoint scenes and show through a lightweight optimization probe that it provides useful structured supervision, suggesting its value not only as an evaluation benchmark but also as a data resource for advancing MLLM-based UAV agents. Project website and supplementary materials: https://uav-dualcog.lozumi.com

</details>


<div id='cs.CG'></div>

# cs.CG [[Back]](#toc)

### [77] [trueform: Fast And Robust Mesh CSG Via Topological Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15905)
*Žiga Sajovic,Dejan Knez*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: An exact-topology approach for Mesh CSG that stays in integer land, reconstructs topology rather than geometry, and uses topological aggregation to resolve disagreements. It yields valid, watertight outputs, with large speedups and browser interactivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Floating-point CSG emits coordinates after materialisation that can misrepresent orderings/crossings; exact topology is necessary for robust, conflict-free domain partitioning and reusable, queryable outputs across multiple stages.

Method: Compute the arrangement using integer-exact predicates with five canonical pairwise intersection types (VV, VE, VF, EE, EF). Each cut face is placed in its own plane; a two-level identity ensures cross-face consistency. Build a per-domain partition (boolean tests per domain and open surfaces as oriented sheets) and query arbitrarily. For decisions spanning faces, recover the intended answer by majority voting over disagreeing geometric observations within a topological unit. Implemented in the header-only trueform library (C++ with Python/TypeScript bindings).

Result: Compared with prior art, the approach delivers valid, watertight outputs and runs up to two orders of magnitude faster, while remaining interactive in the browser.

Conclusion: A topology-first, exact-integer approach to 3D CSG arrangements enables robust, efficient, and reusable results. It preserves topology across operations, avoids global exact constructions, and supports real-time exploration and rendering.

Abstract: Mesh CSG output is consumed in floating point: however exact the computation, every emitted coordinate is materialised -- rounded to a representable position -- and the next stage can observe crossings and orderings the exact result never had. Only index-based topology survives materialisation. We keep it exact: within the build, the arrangement's radial structure is ordered by exact predicates on the original input planes -- exact without exact constructions -- and where a decision spans faces, the intended answer is recovered by topological aggregation: a majority vote over the disagreeing geometric observations within their topological unit.
  We compute the arrangement locally with integer-exact predicates, every stage a graph problem on graphs it never explicitly constructs. Pairwise intersections are classified into five canonical types (VV, VE, VF, EE, EF), each cut face is arranged in its own plane, and a two-level identity keeps the result consistent across faces with no global structure. The arrangement and its domain partition are built once and queried arbitrarily often: a boolean of any arity is a per-domain bit test, volumetric regions read straight off the partition, and open surfaces -- declared as oriented sheets -- cut volumes through the same algebra. The method is implemented in the header-only trueform library, in C++ with Python and TypeScript bindings. Compared to prior art, it produces valid, watertight output while running up to two orders of magnitude faster, and stays interactive in the browser.

</details>


### [78] [On the Stability of Minimum-Weight Perfect Matching on the Line](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16137)
*Mark de Berg,Ulrike Schmidt-Kraepelin,Andree-Ovidiu Stef*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: In 1D, dynamic minimum-weight perfect matching under insertions/deletions: a trade-off between stability (number of edges changed per update) and approximation. An O(sqrt(n))-stable algorithm achieves a 2-approximation, and this is optimal among sublinear-stability algorithms. Any o(log n)-stable algorithm has unbounded (poor) approximation. Lower bounds hold for insertion-only and apply to bipartite versions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maintain a high-quality match efficiently in a dynamic setting where the input set changes, aiming to change only a small number of matched edges per update while keeping the matching close to optimal.

Method: Construct a dynamic 1D data-structure or algorithm that updates the matching with at most O(sqrt(n)) edge changes per update and proves a 2-approximation. Prove lower bounds: any algorithm with o(log n) stability must incur unbounded approximation. Extend results to insertion-only dynamic updates and to the bipartite variant.

Result: An O(sqrt(n))-stable algorithm with a 2-approximation in the fully dynamic 1D setting; proved optimal for all sublinear stability levels. Any o(log n)-stable algorithm cannot guarantee a bounded approximation. Lower bounds hold in insertion-only case and for bipartite versions.

Conclusion: In 1D dynamic matching, there is a clear stability–quality frontier: sublinear stability (o(log n)) is insufficient for a bounded approximation; achieving 2-approximation requires at least Θ(sqrt(n)) stability, and the bounds extend to insertion-only and bipartite cases.

Abstract: Computing a minimum-weight perfect matching for a point set $P$ in Euclidean space is a classic geometric optimization problem. We consider the problem in a dynamic setting, where pairs of points may be added to or removed from the set $P$. Our focus is on maintaining an approximately optimal solution without making too many changes to the solution. More precisely, we are interested in $k$-stable algorithms, which change at most $k$ edges in the matching after each update to the set $P$. In other words, we consider an online setting (with insertions and deletions) with bounded recourse.
  We study trade-offs between the stability of the algorithm and the approximation ratio of the maintained solution for point sets in $\mathbb{R}^1$. First, we present an $O(\sqrt{n})$-stable algorithm that maintains a $2$-approximation, which we show to be optimal among all algorithms with sublinear stability. Second, we prove that any $o(\log n)$-stable algorithm has unbounded approximation ratio. Our lower bounds hold even in the insertion-only case, while our algorithm works in the fully dynamic case. Moreover, our lower bounds also hold for the bipartite variant of the problem.

</details>


### [79] [Updating zigzag representatives efficiently](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16153)
*Tamal K. Dey,Tao Hou,Dmitriy Morozov*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Efficiently updating zigzag persistence representatives in quadratic time via a DV-based extraction from a constructed non-zigzag.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To close the efficiency gap in zigzag persistence by improving the update step for zigzag representatives after filtration changes.

Method: Propose update algorithms that reuse a recent method for extracting zigzag representatives from a R = D V decomposition of a constructed non-zigzag; address adjacency changes during filtration elongation/shortening; achieve quadratic-time updates.

Result: Despite adjacency changes, the update procedures run in quadratic time, demonstrating practical efficiency and aligning with non-zigzag complexity.

Conclusion: The proposed updates mitigate the main inefficiency barrier in zigzag persistence, enabling effective maintenance of zigzag representatives; potential for broader applicability.

Abstract: Computation of zigzag persistence has progressed in recent years, with results showing that complexities of many problems closely align with those in the non-zigzag setting. The major efficiency gap now lies in the updating of zigzag representatives. In this paper, we propose efficient algorithms for updating zigzag representatives based on a recent algorithm for extracting zigzag representatives from a $R=DV$ decomposition of a constructed non-zigzag. The main difficulty for designing our update algorithms lies in the adjacency change occurring in two operations that elongate or shorten a filtration. Despite the adjacency change, we find that the update can still be done efficiently in quadratic time.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [80] [Structure of the Circular-Dyadic Convolution Error](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15293)
*Ben Fauber,Alireza Moradzadeh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Substituting the DFT with the Hadamard transform in dyadic/circular convolution yields structured, predictable error. There are two input and two output positions that are universally error-free; the error operator is nearly full rank with a logarithmic-dimensional nullspace; the expected error is governed by a single alignment scalar, with a closed form when averaging over random filters; overall, the substitution error typically doubles the output energy except within a universal zero-error subspace.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify and understand the algebraic substitution error incurred when using the real-valued Hadamard transform as a substitute for the DFT in fast convolution, enabling predictable performance and informed design choices.

Method: Analytical characterization of the substitution error: (i) identify exact cancellation locations in input/output; (ii) analyze the error operator’s spectrum to establish near-full rank with a logarithmic nullspace; (iii) derive the expected error via averaging over random filters, yielding a closed-form alignment scalar.

Result: Three complementary findings: (1) exact error cancellation occurs at two input and two output positions, invariant to reordering; (2) the error operator is nearly full rank with a nullspace of logarithmic dimension; (3) the expected error is governed by a single alignment scalar, with a closed-form expression after averaging over random filters. Consequently, substitution error generally doubles the output energy, except for the universal zero-error subspace where no error occurs.

Conclusion: The substitution error is structured and predictable, dominated by a single alignment parameter. This enables principled prediction of the Hadamard-based convolution error and informs strategies to exploit or mitigate it by exploiting the universal zero-error subspace or alignment-aware design.

Abstract: Dyadic and circular convolution can both be computed in $O(N\log N)$ time using the Hadamard transform and the FFT-computed discrete Fourier transform (DFT), respectively. The Hadamard transform is preferable for its real-valued sign flips, yet its substitution for the DFT introduces algebraic error. We present three complementary results that characterize this error. First, we identify exact error cancellation: two input and two output positions are universally error-free, and no reordering of the output can eliminate this error. Second, the error operator is nearly full rank, while its null space has only logarithmic dimension. Third, the expected error is governed by a single alignment scalar, with a closed-form expression obtained by averaging over random filters. In general, the substitution error asymptotically doubles the output energy, except for filters in the universal zero-error subspace, which incur no error. Collectively, these results show that the substitution error is structured, predictable, and governed by alignment.

</details>


### [81] [Position: Quantum Program Generation Must Prioritize Validity Over Probabilistic Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15313)
*Junhao Song,Yu Zhou,William Knottenbelt,Yudong Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Scaling alone cannot ensure valid quantum circuit synthesis; a verifier-centric, constraint-aware generation approach is needed, incorporating hierarchical constraints, topological masks, and symbolic proxies to respect Hilbert-space semantics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the risk of learning invalid quantum programs from unverified data and the exponential decay of valid circuit designs as qubits increase.

Method: Propose verifier-centric agents and embed hierarchical constraints, topological masks, and symbolic proxies directly into generation, shifting from imitation to constraint-driven generation.

Result: Scale cannot bridge the validity gap; verification-aware architectures offer a viable path for modular quantum program generation.

Conclusion: Generation methods should encode task-specific quantum information rules rather than rely on imitation, emphasizing verification-driven design.

Abstract: The scaling hypothesis assumes that increasing model parameters yields emergent reasoning capabilities. This position paper argues that applying this probabilistic paradigm to generic quantum circuit synthesis is a directional error. Unlike natural languages, quantum circuits require strict adherence to mathematical constraints that manifest a significant syntax-semantics gap. Training on unverified quantum programs means that models learn syntax but fail to capture the physical semantics of the Hilbert space. Since the valid subset of circuit designs decays exponentially with the number of qubits, post-hoc filtering is mathematically intractable. We propose a pivot from human-centric copilots to verifier-centric agents. We integrate hierarchical constraints, topological masks, and symbolic proxies directly into generation. Our analysis suggests that scale alone cannot bridge the validity gap. Verification-aware architectures offer a viable path for modular quantum program generation. These considerations point toward generation methods that encode task-specific rules of quantum information, rather than relying on imitation alone.

</details>


### [82] [A Transportable Threshold-Based Framework for Interpretable Classification of Medical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15394)
*Antony Garcia,Adrian Noriega,Gabrielle Britton,Xinming Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Interpretable Bernoulli NB with chi-square binarization yields competitive AUC and calibrated risk across three medical datasets, plus a reproducible worked example.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tackle interpretability, reproducibility, and trust in AI for medicine by replacing opaque models with a transparent probabilistic classifier.

Method: Apply chi-square guided supervised binarization to continuous features to create thresholds that maximize association with outcomes; feed binarized features into Bernoulli Naive Bayes; evaluate on Pima Indians Diabetes, Wisconsin Breast Cancer, and Heart Failure Prediction datasets; assess discrimination via AUC and calibration via Brier score, calibration intercept/slope, and post-hoc beta calibration; include leakage-safe cross-validated calibration and a fully reproducible worked example.

Result: AUCs of 0.800 (Pima), 0.984 (Wisconsin Breast Cancer), and 0.919 (Heart Failure); improved probability calibration across datasets; demonstration that inference can be reproduced with a reference table and basic arithmetic; results suggest comparable performance to complex models with explicit decision rules and calibrated risk estimates.

Conclusion: A statistically interpretable framework can match performance of more complex models while providing transparent decision rules and calibrated risk, supporting trustworthy and generalizable AI in real-world healthcare; includes a concrete worked example to demonstrate reproducibility.

Abstract: Black-box models limit the adoption of artificial intelligence in medicine due to their lack of interpretability and reproducibility. We introduce a statistically grounded framework that provides fully interpretable, rule-based clinical classification using the Bernoulli Naïve Bayes (BNB) model. The method applies supervised $χ^2$-guided statistical binarization to continuous variables, identifying thresholds that maximize association with clinical outcomes within the training data. This transformation allows BNB to operate effectively on continuous medical data without sacrificing its inherent transparency. The approach was evaluated on three benchmark datasets, Pima Indians Diabetes, Wisconsin Breast Cancer, and Heart Failure Prediction, achieving area-under-the-curve (AUC) scores of 0.800 for the Pima analysis, 0.984 for Wisconsin Breast Cancer, and 0.919 for Heart Failure Prediction. In addition to discrimination, probabilistic reliability was assessed using leakage-safe cross-validated calibration analysis including Brier score, calibration intercept/slope, and post-hoc beta calibration, which improved probability calibration across datasets. These results suggest that a statistically interpretable framework can achieve performance comparable to more complex models while providing explicit, clinically meaningful decision rules and calibrated risk estimates. To illustrate this transparency concretely, a complete worked example demonstrates that model inference can be reproduced using only a reference table and basic arithmetic, without access to software or proprietary tools. This work offers a practical approach to supporting trustworthy and generalizable AI in real-world healthcare settings.

</details>


### [83] [Regularity-Aware Stochastic MGDA with Adaptive Conflict-Avoidant Update Direction Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15412)
*Chentong Huang,Lisha Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes MoRe, a regularity-aware stochastic MGDA method for multi-objective learning that leverages CA direction regularity to accelerate convergence; achieves a faster rate and empirical improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In stochastic MGDA, gradient noise biases the update direction, and the CA direction continuity limits convergence. Exploiting regularity of the CA direction can yield faster optimization in multi-objective learning.

Method: Introduce MoRe: a stochastic multi-objective regularity-aware method that switches between CA-direction updates (when gradient conflict is large) and fixed linear scalarization (when conflict is small). It relies on the CA direction being 1/2-Hölder continuous in general (with optimal exponent) and Lipschitz under extra regularity; provides per-iteration conflict-avoidance guarantees.

Result: Theoretical improvement of nonconvex SMG convergence rate from ~O(T^-1/4) to ~O(T^-1/2); per-iteration conflict-avoidance guarantees; empirical results show improved multi-task performance and convergence consistent with theory.

Conclusion: MoRe delivers a principled, regularity-aware update rule for stochastic multi-objective optimization, achieving faster convergence under regularity while maintaining robust conflict avoidance; broadly applicable to multi-task learning scenarios.

Abstract: Multi-objective learning (MOL) aims to optimize multiple objectives simultaneously. The multi-gradient descent algorithm (MGDA) is a workhorse that iteratively updates along a common descent or conflict-avoidant (CA) direction across objectives. In stochastic settings, however, the vanilla stochastic MGDA method, SMG, lacks a fast convergence rate because mini-batch sampling introduces noise in the gradients. This causes bias in the update direction, which is controlled by the CA direction continuity. In this paper, we show that the CA direction is $1/2$-Holder continuous with respect to the Jacobian matrix, and the exponent $1/2$ cannot be improved in the worst case. This leads to a suboptimal convergence rate for vanilla stochastic MGDA in prior works. Nevertheless, under additional regularity conditions, we show this can be improved to Lipschitz continuity. Based on this insight, we propose a stochastic multi-objective regularity-aware (MoRe) method that exploits the Lipschitz continuity of the CA direction when the subproblem is regular, and switches to a fixed scalarization weight otherwise. Intuitively, the proposed algorithm employs CA direction update when the gradient conflict is large, and linear scalarization update otherwise. Theoretically, our method improves the convergence rate of SMG in the nonconvex setting from $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(T^{-1/4})$ to $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(T^{-1/2})$, where $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(\cdot)$ hides logarithmic factors. Meanwhile, we also establish the per-iterate conflict-avoidance guarantees. Empirically, experiments demonstrate its effectiveness in multi-task performance and verify convergence behavior consistent with the established theoretical rate.

</details>


### [84] [AI Trading: Evaluating Large Language Models for Technical Market Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15414)
*Geofrey Ntale*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematic, cross-model evaluation of five LLMs (GPT-4 Turbo, Claude 3 Opus, Gemini 1.5 Pro, Llama 3 70B, FinGPT) on technical market analysis across four tasks (candlestick pattern recognition, directional signals, backtesting via simulated execution, financial report comprehension) using rigorous metrics (Sharpe, max drawdown, Sortino, information coefficient, F1, BLEU). GPT-4 Turbo tops general-purpose models in annualized return and Sharpe; FinGPT shows competitive risk-adjusted performance due to domain fine-tuning; both outperform a passive S&P 500 benchmark in tests. Common failure modes include numerical hallucination, context-window limitations, and poor performance in sideways markets. Conclusion: LLMs are promising for AI trading systems but require task decomposition, robust backtesting, and domain-aware fine-tuning for robust deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically compare the practical utility and limitations of both general-purpose and domain-specialized LLMs for technical market analysis, across multiple decision-making and evaluation tasks in financial markets.

Method: Four structured tasks with quantitative metrics: candlestick pattern recognition from OHLCV data; directional signal generation (BUY/SELL/HOLD); backtesting of signal quality via a simulated execution pipeline; and financial report comprehension. Evaluation metrics include Sharpe ratio, maximum drawdown, Sortino ratio, information coefficient (IC), F1-score, and BLEU score. Comparative analysis across GPT-4 Turbo, Claude 3 Opus, Gemini 1.5 Pro, Llama 3 70B, and FinGPT.

Result: GPT-4 Turbo achieves the highest annualized return and Sharpe ratio among the general-purpose models; FinGPT provides competitive risk-adjusted performance due to domain-specific fine-tuning; both models beat a passive S&P 500 benchmark under the tested conditions. Across all models, persistent failure modes include numerical hallucination, context-window limitations, and inconsistent performance in sideways markets.

Conclusion: LLMs hold genuine promise for AI-driven trading systems, but robust deployment requires careful task decomposition, rigorous backtesting protocols, and domain-aware fine-tuning to mitigate failure modes and achieve reliable performance.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for processing the heterogeneous information environments of modern financial markets. This paper presents a systematic, comparative evaluation of five prominent LLMs: GPT-4 Turbo, Claude 3 Opus, Gemini 1.5 Pro, Llama 3 70B, and the domain-specialized FinGPT, with respect to their capacity for technical market analysis. The evaluation spans four structured tasks: candlestick pattern recognition from OHLCV data, directional signal generation (BUY/SELL/HOLD), backtesting of signal quality through a simulated execution pipeline, and financial report comprehension. Our experimental framework employs rigorous quantitative metrics, including Sharpe ratio, maximum drawdown, Sortino ratio, information coefficient, F1-score, and BLEU score. Findings from simulated backtesting indicate that GPT-4 Turbo achieves the highest annualized return and Sharpe ratio among general-purpose models, while FinGPT demonstrates competitive risk-adjusted performance due to domain-specific fine-tuning. Both models outperform a passive S&P 500 benchmark under the tested conditions. The study identifies persistent failure modes across all evaluated models, including numerical hallucination, context-window limitations, and inconsistent performance in sideways market regimes. We conclude that while LLMs hold genuine promise within AI trading systems, robust deployment requires careful task decomposition, rigorous backtesting protocols, and domain-aware fine-tuning strategies.

</details>


### [85] [qZACH-ViT: Quantization-Aware Intrinsic Explanations with Recursive Attribution-Stabilized Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15421)
*Athanasios Angelakis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: qZACH-ViT quantizes a zero-token, position-free ViT backbone for efficient, intrinsically explainable classification; with Recursive Attribution-Stabilized Optimization (RASO) to align and stabilize gradients for attribution; evaluated on MedMNIST with strong deployment metrics and speedups from ONNX INT8, achieving high prediction fidelity and robust XAI signals; not all metrics dominated, but overall deployable with improved stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve compact, efficient medical-image classifiers that provide interpretable evidence without sacrificing accuracy, addressing the common trade-off between efficiency and interpretability.

Method: Extend ZACH-ViT to a quantization-aware, CLS-token-free variant (qZACH-ViT); introduce Recursive Attribution-Stabilized Optimization (RASO) to norm-match classification and attribution gradients and remove conflicting attribution components; train and evaluate under four controlled conditions on seven MedMNIST datasets using 50 images per class and 10 seeds; convert 210 qZACH-ViT checkpoints to executable mixed-precision ONNX INT8 graphs; deploy INT8 inference with Adam optimizer.

Result: Mixed-precision INT8 qZACH-ViT with Adam improves the FP32 ZACH-ViT baseline across all seven datasets, averaging 0.0313 gain in the primary metric; qZACH-ViT with RASO yields 0.0368 mean gain. Prediction agreement between source and INT8 runs is 99.9751%; mean absolute primary-metric change 0.000133; max 0.004386. Intrinsic maps: mean cosine similarity 0.999955, rank correlation 0.9944, top-10% overlap 0.9692. ONNX artifacts are 70% smaller; end-to-end CPU speedups of 1.41x (one thread) and 2.39x (four threads). RASO reduces sufficiency error and improves input-noise stability but does not dominate every metric; overall, qZACH-ViT is deployable and RASO offers targeted stability improvements.

Conclusion: qZACH-ViT enables a compact, intrinsically explainable model suitable for deployment; RASO provides a stability-oriented optimization that improves attribution robustness without sacrificing accuracy in most cases.

Abstract: Compact medical-image classifiers need efficiency and interpretable evidence, yet these goals are often addressed separately. We introduce qZACH-ViT, a quantization-aware extension of the zero-token (CLS-token-free), position-free ZACH-ViT backbone with recursive intrinsic patch-level class evidence. We also introduce Recursive Attribution-Stabilized Optimization (RASO), which norm-matches classification and attribution gradients and removes attribution components that conflict with classification. We evaluate four controlled conditions on seven MedMNIST datasets using 50 training images per class and ten fixed seeds, completing 280 runs. All 210 qZACH-ViT checkpoints are converted to executable mixed-precision ONNX INT8 graphs containing 16 signed INT8 MatMulInteger projections with INT32 accumulation. Deployed mixed-precision INT8 qZACH-ViT with Adam improves the FP32 ZACH-ViT baseline mean on all seven datasets, with a mean paired gain of 0.0313 in the dataset-specific primary metric; qZACH-ViT with RASO yields a mean gain of 0.0368. Across 964,920 source-to-INT8 test comparisons, prediction agreement is 99.9751\%, with a mean absolute primary-metric change of 0.000133 and a maximum of 0.004386. Across 3,600 matched intrinsic maps, mean cosine similarity is 0.999955, mean rank correlation is 0.9944, and mean top-10\% overlap is 0.9692. ONNX artifacts are 70.0\% smaller than source checkpoints and provide $1.41\times$ and $2.39\times$ end-to-end CPU speedups with one and four threads. RASO significantly reduces sufficiency error and improves input-noise stability over Adam with the same attribution loss, but does not dominate every predictive or explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) metric. These results establish qZACH-ViT as a deployable compact intrinsically explainable model and RASO as a targeted stability-oriented optimization procedure.

</details>


### [86] [From hyperplanes to hyperellipsoids: characterizing the inherent interpretability of linear and single-qubit mixed-state binary classification models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15433)
*Kaitlin Gili*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A single-qubit mixed state model for binary classification acts as the ellipsoid counterpart to a standard linear classifier, replacing a hyperplane with a hyperellipsoid and exposing distinct geometric and feature-importance inductive biases; the work emphasizes interpretability and pedagogy to introduce quantum ML concepts in undergraduate ML.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify how quantum-inspired models differ in interpretability and inductive biases from standard linear models, to provide an accessible bridge for teaching quantum ML concepts using familiar linear classification.

Method: Side-by-side analytical comparison of a standard linear model and a single-qubit mixed state model for binary classification, focusing on decision boundaries (hyperplane vs hyperellipsoid) and associated inductive biases and feature importances.

Result: Demonstrates that the single-qubit mixed state model yields a hyperellipsoid decision boundary, equivalently the ellipsoidal version of linear classification; reveals distinct geometric inductive biases and feature-importance biases between the two models.

Conclusion: Offers an accessible, pedagogy-friendly characterization that helps readers understand quantum ML ideas via familiar linear-classification concepts and supports introducing quantum ML in undergraduate courses.

Abstract: We characterize and compare the inherent interpretability offerings of a standard linear model with a single qubit mixed state model for the task of supervised binary classification. A side by side comparison reveals that a single qubit mixed state model for binary classification is just the ``ellipsoid version" of standard linear model classification. More precisely, rather than learning a hyperplane to classify data, we learn a hyperellipsoid. We discuss the consequences of the geometric inductive biases of both models, as well as how each model contains a different feature importance inductive bias. This short characterization offers an accessible route to quantum machine learning (ML) ideas for readers who have zero background in quantum and are only familiar with linear classification in ML. In support of ML pedagogy, we encourage instructors to utilize this piece to smoothly introduce quantum ML ideas into the undergraduate ML classroom.

</details>


### [87] [Stochastic Reset Pathfinding: Path-Level Regret for Cascading Bandits over Graph Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15440)
*Guni Sharon,Wei Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A stochastic reset pathfinding framework on known directed graphs with unknown edge reliability, introducing path-level algorithms PathUCB and PathTS within a combinatorial cascading bandit (CCB) setting. It yields a path-level regret bound and demonstrates empirical performance advantages and a caveat: adversarial instances can break Thompson sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To optimize source-to-goal routing under stochastic edge successes with reset on failure, modeling real-world networks (quantum repeater, Lightning, unreliable mesh). Existing edge-centric analyses miss structured path-level dynamics; SRP’s global reset makes optimal policies open-loop, motivating a path-centric bandit approach.

Method: Define Stochastic Reset Pathfinding (SRP) on a known graph with unknown edge success probabilities. Propose Log-Dijkstra meta-algorithm with two instantiations: PathUCB (UCB) and PathTS (Thompson Sampling). Prove a path-level regret bound by decomposing regret over suboptimal paths using a per-path complexity C(pi) that combines edge prefix/suffix reliability. Place SRP in CC B (combinatorial cascading bandits).

Result: The path-level regret bound complements existing edge-level CCB bounds and is informative on graphs with polynomially many s–t paths. Empirical results across quantum-network, layered-DAG, grid-world, and Erdos-Renyi domains show PathTS often performs best; an adversarial instance demonstrates PathTS can fail to converge, aligning with known exponential obstructions for combinatorial Thompson Sampling in multiplicative-reward settings.

Conclusion: PathTS is a practical default with caveats; PathUCB provides solid theoretical guarantees; the work advances path-level bandit analysis in SRP, particularly for structured graphs, while highlighting limitations in adversarial environments.

Abstract: We introduce Stochastic Reset Pathfinding (SRP), an episodic learning problem on a known directed graph with unknown stationary edge success probabilities. In each episode, the agent commits to a source-to-goal path, and any edge failure during execution resets it to the source. SRP captures settings such as entanglement distribution in quantum repeater networks, payment routing on the Lightning Network, and delivery in unreliable mesh networks. We show that the global-reset structure makes the optimal policy open-loop, placing SRP within the combinatorial cascading bandit (CCB) framework. We propose a Log-Dijkstra meta-algorithm with UCB (PathUCB) and Thompson Sampling (PathTS) instantiations. Our main technical result is a path-level regret bound for PathUCB that decomposes regret over suboptimal paths via a per-path complexity C(pi) combining each edge's prefix and suffix reliability. The bound is complementary to the edge-level CCB bound and more informative on structured graphs with polynomially many source-to-goal paths. Experiments on quantum-network, layered-DAG, grid-world, and Erdos-Renyi domains support the theory and show that PathTS typically achieves the best empirical performance among the algorithms tested. We then exhibit an adversarial instance on which PathTS fails to converge, consistent with a known exponential obstruction for combinatorial Thompson Sampling on multiplicative-reward problems. We recommend PathTS as the practical default while cautioning that adversarial instances exist.

</details>


### [88] [Who Became Financially Vulnerable After COVID-19? A Population-Level Machine Learning Analysis Using MEPS Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15446)
*Alexey Kresin,Zien Cheng,Ammar Ahad,Ebiyomare Kelvin,Manish Sivaratri,Prabhjeet Singh,Omar Aljawfi,Olabisi Ojo,Nawar Shara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MEPS-based pre/post-COVID analysis finds persistent financial vulnerability (out-of-pocket health expenditures >10% of family income) linked to poverty, insurance, and prescription spending; logistic regression and ML show stable predictors with only modest drop in predictive performance when generalized across the pandemic divide.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess the trajectory of healthcare financial vulnerability during the COVID-19 period using nationally representative data, evaluate time-stability of predictors, and examine the value of combining interpretable statistics with machine learning for population health surveillance and policy planning.

Method: Data: MEPS 2019 and 2021 (nationally representative, survey-weighted). Outcome: high financial burden defined as OOP expenditures >10% of family income. Analyses: descriptive statistics; survey-weighted; logistic regression for adjusted odds ratios; machine learning (random forest, gradient boosting) for predictive performance; temporal generalization by training on pre-pandemic data and testing on post-pandemic observations.

Result: Financial vulnerability is strongly associated with poverty status, insurance coverage, and prescription drug spending. Subgroup analyses show persistent disparities with some evidence of increased burden among vulnerable populations in 2021. Models trained on pre-pandemic data showed only modest reductions in predictive performance when applied to post-pandemic data, indicating that principal predictors remained relatively stable over time.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates the value of integrating interpretable regression with machine learning for population health research, providing a framework for surveillance and risk stratification to inform policy aimed at reducing financial barriers to care, with predictors largely robust across the COVID-19 period.

Abstract: The cost of healthcare remains a concern in the United States and may have been influenced by disruptions associated with the COVID-19 pandemic. This study examines healthcare financial vulnerability before and after the pandemic using Medical Expenditure Panel Survey (MEPS) data from 2019 and 2021. High financial burden was defined as out-of-pocket healthcare expenditures exceeding 10% of family income. Survey-weighted subgroup analyses were performed to obtain nationally representative estimates across demographic and socioeconomic groups.
  Descriptive analyses were complemented by interpretable logistic regression and machine learning models. Logistic regression was used to estimate adjusted odds ratios, while random forest and gradient boosting models were used to evaluate predictive performance. Temporal generalization assessed whether models trained on pre-pandemic data remained predictive when applied to post-pandemic observations.
  Financial vulnerability was strongly associated with poverty status, insurance coverage, and prescription drug spending. Subgroup analyses indicated persistent disparities across population groups, with some evidence of increased burden among vulnerable populations in 2021. Despite these differences, models trained on pre-pandemic data exhibited only modest reductions in predictive performance when evaluated on post-pandemic data, suggesting that the principal predictors of healthcare financial vulnerability remained relatively stable over time.
  These findings provide a population-level assessment of healthcare financial vulnerability during the COVID-19 period and demonstrate the value of combining interpretable statistical modeling with machine learning for population health research. The results may support future population health surveillance, risk stratification, and healthcare policy research aimed at reducing financial barriers to care.

</details>


### [89] [LLM4EHR: Aligning Clinical Time Series with Medical Event Sequences via Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15447)
*Jingteng Li,Alexander Capstick,Louise Rigny,Iona Biggart,Neil J Sebire,Payam Barnaghi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces LLM4EHR, a clinical foundation model for ICU EHR data that aligns EHR events with time-series using a domain-adapted LLM and TS encoder, trained via a regularised contrastive objective; shows improved downstream task performance and k-shot transferability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To exploit shared temporal structures between discrete EHR events and continuous time-series data in ICU, addressing limitations where temporal alignment is underutilized in existing clinical foundation models.

Method: Pre-train a hybrid model combining a domain-adapted LLM for EHR event embeddings with a transformer-based TS encoder; train with a regularised contrastive objective that aligns EHR and TS representations; conduct ablation studies and evaluate k-shot transfer to new cohorts.

Result: EHR-TS embeddings learned by LLM4EHR improve downstream clinical task performance and show transferability to unseen cohorts with limited data; ablations quantify the contribution of temporal alignment and the contrastive objective.

Conclusion: Temporal alignment of EHR events and time-series with a domain-adapted LLM and TS encoder yields more generalisable, performant clinical foundation models, enabling effective cross-cohort deployment via few-shot adaptation.

Abstract: Recent research in clinical machine learning, focusing on outcome predictions in intensive care unit (ICU), has shifted from bespoke supervised models to foundation models, utilising modern representation learning methods. Here, foundation models are pre-trained on mixtures of complex clinical data modalities, useful for various downstream tasks. Existing works often utilise Electronic Health Records (EHR) to provide rich and diverse patient observations to train clinical foundation models. However, existing methods do not sufficiently explore the shared temporal structures between clinical events and time series (TS) observations recorded in EHRs. This limitation potentially leads to less robust and adaptive clinical foundation models, resulting in reduced performance on downstream tasks. To fully exploit this temporal structure, we propose LLM4EHR, a new clinical foundation model trained on ICU EHR data. Combining domain adapted large language models with a transformer TS encoder, we pre-trained LLM4EHR by temporally aligning the EHR events and TS. For this, we propose a regularised contrastive objective to learn robust EHR TS representations conditioned on EHR event embeddings produced by the domain adapted LLM. Supported by an ablation study, we find that learnt EHR TS embeddings from LLM4EHR improve performance on various downstream clinical tasks with competitive performance. Further, we empirically demonstrate that LLM4EHR learns transferable clinical TS embeddings that can be deployed to new cohorts via k-shot adaptation. These findings provide a step towards building more generalisable and performant clinical foundation models.

</details>


### [90] [Relevant and Irrelevant: A Renormalization Group Analysis of Transformer Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15449)
*Parviz Haggi-Mani,Irina Rish*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Attention in Transformers acts as a Wilsonian RG perturbation of the MLP fixed point; relevance depends on the data spectrum, with clear predictions validated on synthetic Markov chains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain when attention helps by connecting to fixed-point RG flows and the spectral structure of the data-generating process.

Method: Derive a fixed-point shift under RG perturbation; predict fixed-point geometry, effective rank, layer specificity, and perturbation decay; test on synthetic Markov chains with controlled correlation length; analyze dominance of the first layer head and spectral decay regimes.

Result: Four predictions are validated: (1) long correlation: attention is relevant and drives a phase transition with effective rank exceeding input dimensionality at layer 1 and then stabilizing; (2) short correlation: attention is irrelevant, converging to the same loss and fixed-point as the MLP though with faster perturbation contraction; (3) the first-layer head (L0H0) dominates, contributing >4x the representational shift of subsequent heads; (4) perturbation decay shows regime reversal: slow Markov modes preserved in long correlation (5.4x dynamic range) vs. 1.3x for MLP; in short correlation, all modes are suppressed faster than MLP with no spectral selectivity.

Conclusion: A first-order Wilsonian RG perturbation framework provides a predictive, data-dependent account of attention relevance, suggesting that the impact of attention is governed by the spectral properties of the data rather than architectural alone.

Abstract: Using the language of Wilsonian renormalization group theory (RG), we treat the Transformer's attention mechanism as a perturbation of the trained MLP residual-stack fixed point and ask whether it constitutes a relevant, marginal, or irrelevant operator. We derive a fixed-point shift formula and obtain four testable predictions for the fixed-point geometry, effective rank profile, layer specificity, and perturbation decay spectrum. Testing these on synthetic Markov chain sequences with controlled correlation length, we find: (1) For large chains(long correlation), attention is strongly relevant: it closes a residual loss gap the MLP cannot bridge and drives a phase transition in representation space, with effective rank jumping above input dimensionality at layer 1 and stabilizing at a high-dimensional plateau. (2) For short chains(short correlation), attention is irrelevant: the Transformer converges to the same loss and fixed-point geometry as the MLP, though it contracts perturbations faster. (3) The transition is dominated by the first-layer head (L0H0), which accounts for more than 4 times the representational shift of any subsequent head, consistent with the prediction that the relevant operator acts before the MLP begins integrating out positional variation. (4) Perturbation decay experiments reveal a regime reversal: in the long correlation regime the Transformer selectively preserves slow Markov modes (5.4 times the dynamic range in decay length vs. 1.3 times for the MLP); in the short correlation regime it suppresses all modes faster than the MLP, with no spectral selectivity. Together, these results show that the relevance of attention is not a property of the architecture but of the spectral structure of the data-generating process, and that a first-order RG perturbation framework provides a predictive account of that difference.

</details>


### [91] [Looped Latent Attention: Cross-Loop KV Compression for Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15456)
*James O' Neill,Fergal Reid*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLA shows looped cache is low-rank; introduces a post-training latent cache codec to compress K/V latents; achieves near-lossless compression and significant throughput gains across large models; outperforms baselines in decoding efficiency and math tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce memory and compute costs of decoding in looped, weight-tied Transformers by compressing recurring K/V caches without sacrificing performance; validate across models and tasks.

Method: Introduce Looped Latent Attention (LLA) with per-head and 2D variants; post-training cache codec initialized from teacher activations via SVD; refine with KL and attention-output distillation; reconstruct per-read K/V vectors on attention; evaluate on Ouro-2.6B-Thinking and Huginn-3.5B for cache compression and capacity; compare against MLA, cross-layer sharing, KV quantization, and final-loop reuse.

Result: At matched cache budgets, per-head LLA outperforms MLA and other baselines; decoder-independent evaluation shows near-lossless 32x compression. Cache reduction is exact. On H200, latent-store path increases Ouro-1.4B batch capacity from 32 to 768 sequences (21.3x compression) at 4k context. For long math rollouts, on-policy refinement of student prefixes raises MATH-500 from 0.43 to 0.66 (4x) and reduces no-answer generations. Transfers to Huginn-3.5B with SVD codec remaining near-lossless to 32x. 

Conclusion: The recurrent cache in looped Transformers is low-rank but not safely collapsible to a single state; LLA achieves exact, efficient cache compression, enabling larger effective context and throughput with cross-model transfer.

Abstract: Looped, weight-tied Transformers reduce parameters by reusing a block, but decoding still stores a separate K/V cache for every recurrence step. We show that this loop-indexed cache is highly structured. For a fixed token, layer and head, K/V vectors trace a short low-rank trajectory across loops, while the head and layer axes remain much flatter. We introduce Looped Latent Attention (LLA), a post-training cache codec that stores compact K and V latents and reconstructs loop-specific K/V vectors only when attention reads them. The default per-head codec compresses recurrence, while LLA-2D also folds heads into one latent for the extreme-compression regime. The codec is initialized from SVD of teacher activations and refined with KL and attention-output distillation. At matched cache budget, per-head LLA outperforms head-axis MLA, cross-layer sharing, KV quantization and final-loop reuse, showing that the recurrent cache is low-rank but not safely collapsible to a single state. The same axis advantage holds on Ouro-2.6B-Thinking and transfers to Huginn-3.5B, where an SVD codec remains near-lossless to 32x in decoder-independent evaluation. The cache reduction is exact. On one H200, the latent-store path increases measured Ouro-1.4B batch capacity at 4k context from 32 to 768 sequences at 21.3x compression. For long math rollouts, on-policy refinement on student-generated prefixes raises MATH-500 at 4x from 0.43 to 0.66 and reduces no-answer generations.

</details>


### [92] [Robust Peak-cost Constrained Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15457)
*Shilpa Mukhopadhyay,Sourav Ganguly,Santosh Mohan Rajkumar,Honghao Wei,Debdipta Goswami,Arnob Ghosh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces RP-CRL with peak-cost constraints; reveals potential nonzero duality gap in peak-cost MDPs; offers a surrogate robust optimization and IPM-based value estimation; proves epsilon-violation bound and matches robust reward; demonstrates safety and performance empirically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-critical RL where a single peak cost is catastrophic; standard CMDP (expected cumulative cost) misses rare but extreme violations; need robustness to model mismatch.

Method: Develops surrogate optimization framework; robust value estimation via integral probability metrics; analyzes duality properties; extends to robust setting under transition perturbations.

Result: Surrogate solution achieves same robust reward as original while violating constraint by at most epsilon, under suitable hyperparameters; experiments show safety under dynamics perturbations with strong reward.

Conclusion: Robust peak-cost constrained RL offers safety guarantees under dynamics perturbations, explains potential duality gap differences from CMDPs, and provides a practical algorithm with theoretical guarantees.

Abstract: We study robust peak-cost constrained reinforcement learning (RP-CRL), where the objective is to maximize expected reward while controlling the maximum cost encountered along a trajectory. This setting is motivated by safety-critical applications in which a single large violation can be catastrophic and therefore cannot be adequately captured by the standard CMDP framework based on expected cumulative cost. Existing reachability-constrained RL methods adopt Lagrangian-based approaches, yet the underlying duality properties of peak-cost constrained MDPs remain unclear. We show that, unlike standard CMDPs, peak-cost constrained MDPs may not admit zero duality gap. We further consider a robust formulation to address simulator-to-real-world mismatch in the transition dynamics. To solve this problem, we develop a surrogate optimization framework and a robust value estimation method based on integral probability metrics. We prove that, with appropriate hyperparameter choices, the surrogate solution attains the same robust reward value as the original problem while violating the constraint by at most epsilon. Experiments show that the proposed method effectively enforces safety under dynamics perturbations while retaining strong reward performance.

</details>


### [93] [ADS-C: Antidistillation Sampling for Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15467)
*Khawaja Abaid Ullah,Mohammad Javad Khojasteh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ADS-C is an antidistillation defense for classification that preserves top-1 accuracy while degrading a knowledge-distilling attacker’s surrogate; it uses input-dependent, per-input margin budgets and temperature scaling to bound perturbations, creating a phase-transition behavior that favors defender with zero utility cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend antidistillation sampling—designed for large language models—to classification, where transfer of the defense (or its graph of effects) is not well understood; the goal is to study how per-input confidence margins govern transfer and to design a defense that preserves the teacher’s accuracy while hindering distillation.

Method: Adapt ADS to classification by enforcing a per-input margin budget on the served probability distribution, with a gradient-directed, input-dependent perturbation of the distribution. Use closed-form thresholds and temperature softening to control the perturbation. Propose ADS-C that guarantees top-1 accuracy preservation. Analyze thresholds, phase transition behavior, and derive that all temperature configurations lie on a common unfavorable trade-off curve. Evaluate on CIFAR-100, CIFAR-10, and Tiny-ImageNet; compare with unmodified ADS. Consider hard-label vs soft-label attacker settings.

Result: ADS-C preserves teacher accuracy identically to the undefended model; the attacker’s distilled student degrades by 17.4pp (CIFAR-100), 29.6pp (CIFAR-10), and 13.3pp (Tiny-ImageNet). Without ADS-C, defending the same scenario costs 27.5pp, 32.9pp, and 22.2pp of teacher accuracy. Hard-label attackers gain nothing; soft-output attackers are forced to train a student well below the defender’s floor (up to 29.7pp). Overall, ADS-C achieves a zero-utility-cost antidistillation defense for classification, the first of its kind in this setting.

Conclusion: ADS-C provides a provably zero-cost defender against distillation in classification by bound-per-input margin budgets and gradient-directed perturbations. It creates a phase-transition regime that drastically impairs distillation while preserving teacher accuracy. Temperature scaling shapes the transition but all configurations lie on a single trade-off curve, yielding an effective, zero-cost antidistillation defense.

Abstract: Knowledge distillation enables an adversary to replicate a proprietary classifier by querying its prediction interface and training a surrogate on the returned probability vectors. Antidistillation sampling, proposed for large language models, counters this threat with an input-dependent, gradient-directed perturbation of the served distribution; its transfer to classification has not been studied. Adapting the defense to classification, we show its behavior is governed by the distribution of the teacher's per-input confidence margins. Because well-trained classifiers are severely overconfident, the direct transfer exhibits an inert window: below a closed-form-predictable threshold, it affects neither attacker nor defender; beyond it, the defense undergoes a phase transition and degrades the teacher faster than the attacker's student. Temperature softening rescales the transition in closed form, and every temperature configuration lies on the same unfavorable trade-off curve. Our method, ADS-C, composes the perturbation under a closed-form, per-input margin budget that provably preserves every served top-1 prediction, so the defended teacher's accuracy equals the undefended teacher's identically. Under this guarantee the distilled student still loses 17.4 percentage points on CIFAR-100, 29.6 on CIFAR-10, and 13.3 on Tiny-ImageNet; matching this degradation with the unmodified defense costs 27.5, 32.9, and 22.2 points of teacher accuracy. Because served labels are unchanged, a hard-label attacker gains nothing, while the defended soft output trains a student up to 29.7 points below that floor: the incentive to distill served probabilities is not merely removed but reversed. To our knowledge, ADS-C is the first antidistillation defense for classification whose utility cost is exactly zero.

</details>


### [94] [Deep Learning Approaches for Sleep Apnea Classification from Polysomnographic EEG Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15477)
*Shashank Manjunath,Mukesh Cheemakurthi,Aarti Sathyanarayana*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comprehensive comparison of deep learning architectures (Vision Transformers and Graph Attention Networks) for pediatric sleep apnea detection from multi-channel EEG, across four feature representations (raw, STFT, coherence graphs, and TDA features). Achieves best test AUC of 0.750 with a Vision Transformer on TDA features; demonstrates feasibility of EEG-based automated OSA screening but with demographic and sleep stage dependent performance, highlighting deployment challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce reliance on resource-intensive polysomnography by leveraging EEG-based signals and modern deep learning models, while systematically evaluating representation choices and architectures on a large pediatric dataset.

Method: Train and compare ViT and GAT architectures on multichannel EEG using four representations: raw temporal signals, short-time Fourier transform spectrograms, coherence-based graphs, and topological data analysis (TDA) derived features. Data: 2410 pediatric subjects for training, 575 for testing, with age and sex matching. Evaluate performance across demographic strata (age, sex, AHI severity) and sleep stages (N1, N2, N3, REM).

Result: Best test AUC achieved: 0.750 using a Vision Transformer with TDA-derived features. Demographic stratification and sleep-stage analysis reveal significant variations in performance, underscoring challenges for clinical deployment.

Conclusion: EEG-based automated OSA screening in pediatric populations is feasible, but substantial challenges remain for clinical deployment due to performance variability across demographics and sleep stages; future work should address generalization, standardization of representations, and integration with clinical workflows.

Abstract: Sleep apnea diagnosis via polysomnography remains resource intensive and relies on time consuming manual data analysis and scoring. Recent work has demonstrated that central nervous system effects of sleep apnea events can be detected through electroencephalogram (EEG) signals. However, most work uses a single feature type on various datasets combined with different classification algorithms. In this work, we present a comprehensive comparison of deep learning architectures and feature representations for automated sleep apnea detection from multichannel EEG on a single dataset of pediatric subjects. We evaluate Vision Transformers and Graph Attention Networks across distinct signal representations: raw temporal signals, short-time Fourier transform spectrograms, coherence based graphs, and two topological data analysis (TDA) derived features. Using age and sex matching of our train and test sets, we train on 2410 pediatric subjects and test on 575 pediatric subjects. We achieve a best test AUC of 0.750 using a vision transformer based model trained on TDA features. Stratified analysis across patient demographics (age, sex, AHI severity) and sleep stages (N1, N2, N3, REM) reveals significant performance variation. Our results demonstrate the feasibility of EEG based automated OSA screening while highlighting essential challenges for clinical deployment.

</details>


### [95] [Inpainting Insights: Elevating Visual XAI with Photorealistic Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15482)
*Josef Lindl,Mariana Chaves,Damien Garreau*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Perturbation-based explanations for image models are improved by using generative inpainting to create photorealistic perturbations, addressing artifacts of pixel replacement and enhancing explanation fidelity within LIME.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional perturbations (e.g., pixel masking) can create out-of-distribution artifacts that mislead explanations. Aligning perturbations with the data manifold should yield more faithful local explanations in XAI for images.

Method: Integrate generative inpainting into LIME to generate photorealistic perturbations for image inputs, replacing masked regions with plausible content that matches the original distribution; evaluate explanation quality against standard perturbation methods.

Result: Photorealistic perturbed samples better align with the original data distribution and improve the quality/faithfulness of explanations produced by the perturbation-based method.

Conclusion: Generative inpainting enhances perturbation-based image explanations by reducing artifacts and increasing fidelity; suggests a path toward more reliable XAI that respects data manifold constraints; may generalize to other perturbation-based approaches.

Abstract: The increasing complexity of state-of-the-art machine learning models has made their behavior progressively harder to interpret, spurring rapid advancements in the field of eXplainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI). Among many methods proposed, perturbation-based approaches play a major role. By systematically altering (perturbing) input features, these approaches measure the impact on the model's predictions. For image data, traditional perturbation techniques, often involve replacing pixel values e.g., with a pre-defined color. However, such approaches, but also more refined deterministic techniques, generate unrealistic out-of-distribution samples and often leave visible artifacts, which can mislead the model and compromise explanation quality. In this work, we adjust LIME, a widely used perturbation-based method, to demonstrate how generative inpainting can improve perturbation-based explanations for images. We achieve photorealistic perturbed samples that align better with the original data distribution and enhance explanation quality.

</details>


### [96] [Diffusion models recover accurate mixture weights despite score function insensitivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15485)
*Andrew Dennehy,Ramchandran Muthukumar,Rebecca Willett,Nisha Chandramoorthy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Links diffusion score matching loss to mixture-weight estimation error via a new diffusion score sensitivity index (DSSI); shows DSSI governs parameter estimation from gener samples; proves order-equivalence for Gaussian mixtures; empirical evidence that noise schedules affect sensitivity and mode amplification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address observed paradox in diffusion models: they cover all modes but may misestimate relative mode weights. Introduce a quantitative handle (DSSI) to predict when DSM will recover mixture weights and other qualitative parameters.

Method: Theoretical analysis deriving the relation between DSM loss and mixture-weight estimation error; define DSSI as the sensitivity of DSM loss to parameter changes; prove for Gaussian mixtures that weight estimation error scales with DSM loss under mild conditions; empirical experiments track sensitivity during noising on benchmark distributions; test how noise schedules influence diffusion sensitivity and mode amplification; generalize framework to other qualitative parameters.

Result: Mixture weight estimation errors are of the same order as the DSM loss for Gaussian mixtures under mild assumptions; DSSI predicts the accuracy of parameter recovery from generated samples; sensitivity emerges during the noising process and varies with the schedule; appropriate noise schedules reduce diffusion sensitivity enhancing mode amplification; framework extends to recovering other qualitative distribution parameters.

Conclusion: A unified sensitivity-based framework explains when DSM-based diffusion models recover target parameters and how to modulate this recovery via noise schedules; diffusion sensitivity is a key lever for controlling mode amplification and the recovery of qualitative distribution features.

Abstract: Score-based generative models exhibit a puzzling behavior: they often appear to cover all modes of a target multimodal distribution and yet may fail to learn the correct relative mode amplitudes, which can be interpreted as mixture weights. We resolve this apparent paradox by relating the diffusion score matching (DSM) loss to the error in estimating mixture weights from generated samples. We show that, even when the target score is insensitive to mixture weights, generated samples can recover the weights accurately if the scores at intermediate noise levels are informative about the weights. Accordingly, we define the diffusion score sensitivity index (DSSI) as the variation in the DSM loss relative to changes in a parameter. We then show that the DSSI governs the accuracy with which the parameter of the target distribution can be estimated from generated samples. For Gaussian mixtures in arbitrary dimensions, we prove that the mixture weight estimation errors are on the same order as the DSM loss under mild conditions. Empirically, we show the emergence of sensitivity during the noising process of benchmark data distributions under typical noise schedules, and that these sensitivity values predict how well a well-trained model recovers mixture weights. Furthermore, we show that the choice of noise schedule can reduce diffusion sensitivity, leading to mode amplification. Although we focus on mixture weights, the proposed sensitivity framework governs the recovery of any qualitative parameter of the target distribution.

</details>


### [97] [An Auto-Scaling Approach for Serverless Environments Based on a Multi-Expert Consensus Mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15511)
*Mobina Kashaniyan,Mehrdad Ashtiani,Amirhossein Ghassemi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dependency-aware autoscaling framework for serverless apps using graph-based bottleneck analysis, ensemble forecasting (MLP, LSTM, CNN), and cost-aware control; achieves high predictive accuracy and cost reductions while handling cold-starts and inter-function dependencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address autoscaling challenges in serverless systems caused by dynamic workloads, cold-start latency, and function dependencies.

Method: Model serverless apps as directed graphs; identify structurally important (bottleneck) functions via weighted degree centrality; forecast resource demand with MLP, LSTM, and CNN; combine forecasts with a performance-weighted probabilistic ensemble inspired by Bayesian model averaging; incorporate cold-start awareness and cost comparison to choose scale actions.

Result: Supervised forecasting outperforms unsupervised clustering for autoscaling decisions; ensemble achieves about 99.88% prediction accuracy and reduces prediction error versus hybrid methods; cost reductions observed across various cloud pricing models while maintaining performance targets.

Conclusion: Integrating dependency analysis, multi-expert forecasting, and cost-aware control yields a robust, practical serverless autoscaling solution.

Abstract: Serverless computing provides automatic resource management and pay-per-use execution, but effective autoscaling remains challenging because of dynamic workloads, cold-start latency, and dependencies among functions. We present a dependency-aware autoscaling framework that integrates graph-based bottleneck identification, short-term workload forecasting, multi-model consensus, and cost-aware scaling control. Serverless applications are represented as directed dependency graphs, and structurally important functions are identified using weighted degree centrality. Resource demand is predicted using lightweight MLP, LSTM, and CNN models. Their outputs are combined through a performance-weighted probabilistic ensemble inspired by Bayesian model averaging. The controller further incorporates cold-start awareness and cost comparison to select among scale-up, scale-down, and hold actions. Experiments using real workload traces show that supervised forecasting substantially outperforms unsupervised clustering for autoscaling decision generation. The proposed ensemble achieves 99.88 percent prediction accuracy and reduces prediction error compared with representative hybrid forecasting methods. Evaluations across multiple cloud pricing models also demonstrate consistent infrastructure cost reductions while maintaining performance targets. The results show that combining dependency analysis, multi-expert forecasting, and cost-aware control provides a robust and practical solution for serverless autoscaling.

</details>


### [98] [Cache-Aware Prompt Compression:A Two-Tier Cost Model for LLM API Caching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15516)
*Yan Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAPC (Cache-Aware Prompt Compression) outperforms naive caching and query-aware compression in production LLM deployments by combining cache-agnostic compression with explicit cache controls and tier-preserving bounds, achieving substantial cost reductions with minimal quality loss across diverse workloads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Questioning the practicality of cache-centric cost reductions in LLM serving: real-world caches are two-tier with imperfect hit rates; query-aware compression invalidates prefix caching. The work aims to design a cache-aware compression strategy that preserves caching benefits while enabling effective compression.

Method: Empirical characterization of Anthropic Sonnet 4.6 cache behavior; development of CAPC that pairs query-agnostic compression with explicit cache_control and a tier-preserving ratio bound; evaluation on LongBench-v2 and three production workloads (enterprise tool with 94k-token schema, graph-knowledge RAG across two codebases, and tau-bench 50 tasks), comparing cache-only, query-aware compression, and vanilla baselines.

Result: CAPC consistently yields the lowest cost across configurations: mean savings of 49% vs cache-only, 64% vs query-aware compression, and 90% vs vanilla on LongBench-v2; substantial cost reductions in production workloads (e.g., 51.7% at r=3 for a 94k-token schema, 9.3x vs cache-all on FastAPI, 2.4x on httpx; tau-bench shows CAPC as cheapest with rewards equal to vanilla). Query-aware compression is the most expensive among tested strategies (+40.1% over vanilla on tau-bench).

Conclusion: In production settings, CAPC is the cheapest and most robust strategy for reducing LLM costs with minimal quality loss (≤0.05) and without pushing cached prefixes into the hot tier. The results challenge the efficacy of cache-only or query-aware compression in realistic rho regimes and demonstrate the practical ROI of a cache-aware compression design.

Abstract: Production LLM deployments combine two cost-reduction primitives: prompt caching (a discounted rate for re-used token prefixes) and prompt compression (fewer tokens sent). The compression literature has standardized on query-aware methods that produce a different compressed prefix per query, mechanically invalidating the prefix-strict cache on every call. We characterize this cost empirically on Anthropic's Sonnet 4.6 API and find caching is far from the rho=1.0 ideal the literature assumes: Sonnet's cache has a two-tier architecture with a sharp threshold near 3,500 tokens, below which the hit rate plateaus at rho~0.83 across 30-call sessions. Our cost model predicts, and experiments confirm, that under realistic rho, query-aware compression beats naive caching at high compression ratios (r>=6). We propose Cache-Aware Prompt Compression (CAPC), pairing query-agnostic compression with explicit cache_control plus a tier-preserving ratio bound that prevents over-compression from pushing the cached prefix into the hot tier. CAPC is the cheapest strategy in 16/16 configurations on LongBench-v2, with mean savings of 49% over cache-only, 64% over query-aware compression, and 90% over vanilla, at quality within 0.05 of the uncompressed baseline. We validate CAPC on three production workloads: an enterprise tool-using assistant with a 94k-token schema prefix (51.7% cost reduction at r=3); a graphify knowledge-graph RAG pipeline across two codebases (9.3x vs cache-all on FastAPI, 2.4x on httpx); and the public tau-bench retail benchmark (50 tasks), where CAPC is the cheapest of four strategies with reward exactly equal to vanilla (both 36/50, p=1.00) while query-aware compression is the most expensive at +40.1% over vanilla -- the first production confirmation of the crossover model's negative-ROI prediction on a public benchmark.

</details>


### [99] [Recursive Harness Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15524)
*Hyunin Lee,Jinglue Xu,Jeffrey Seely,Donghyun Lee,Matei Zaharia,Yujin Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RHI enables self-improving harnesses to boost performance and trace quality with lightweight, iterative prompt-level refinements, achieving higher ceilings than fixed high-reasoning baselines while cutting inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In model–harness co-evolution, harnesses generate traces used to train future models; updating provider-built scaffolds is costly. Optimizing user-crafted harnesses offers a lightweight path to better traces and improved agent performance.

Method: Treat the harness as a prompt-level specification of the agent loop and iteratively refine it via pairwise feedback over its own revision history (few-shot, self-referential improvement).

Result: Across 30 synthetic ML tasks spanning finance, robotics, and pharmacy, a few RHI iterations substantially raise the performance ceiling of low-reasoning-effort agents, surpassing the maximum-reasoning-effort setting and reducing inference cost by up to 60%. Gains arise mainly from improved task-specific context management and inter-agent information flow, not longer reasoning traces.

Conclusion: Formalizes an information-theoretic hypothesis for RHI’s implicit optimization objective and positions RHI as a practical continual-learning algorithm within model–harness co-evolution.

Abstract: Under model--harness co-evolution, harnesses are not merely inference-time scaffolds but data-generating components whose execution traces can shape future foundation models. This motivates harness-in-the-loop learning: optimizing harnesses for both immediate agent performance and the quality of traces used for future model training. However, continually updating provider-built scaffolds is costly and labor-intensive. We therefore investigate whether optimizing user-constructed harnesses in a task-specific manner can improve execution-trace quality while remaining computationally lightweight and requiring only a few update iterations.
