To this end, we introduce Recursive Harness Self-Improvement (RHI), which represents the harness as a prompt-level specification of the agent loop and iteratively refines it using pairwise feedback over its own revision history. Across 30 synthetic machine-learning research tasks spanning quantitative finance, robotics, and pharmacy, a few RHI iterations suffice to substantially raise the performance ceiling of low-reasoning-effort agents, exceeding the corresponding maximum-reasoning-effort setting while reducing inference cost by up to 60%. We show that these gains arise primarily from improved task-specific context management through more effective inter-agent information flow rather than longer reasoning traces. Finally, we formalize this behavior as an information-theoretic hypothesis for RHI's implicit optimization objective, suggesting RHI as a practical algorithm for continual learning within the paradigm of model--harness co-evolution.

</details>


### [100] [Kolmogorov--Arnold Networks for Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15525)
*Felippe Alves,Renato Vicente*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Small-basis Kolmogorov--Arnold Networks (KANs) provide a practical interface to audit learned scalar edge transformations, but across benchmarks they show no consistent advantage over strong MLP baselines; edge usage is interpretable, pruning is sparse-tolerant, yet larger KANs do not outperform baselines on standard tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate claims that KANs offer interpretable, fixed-parameter equivalents to transformer feed-forward networks and whether they yield practical benefits over traditional MLPs across benchmarks.

Method: Six-layer, ~10M-parameter B-spline KAN used to reconstruct 884,736 feed-forward edges; prune lowest-activity 20–25%; replication on BabyLM; grid-size sweeps to assess compression; compare MLP, SwiGLU, grouped Chebyshev, and rational GR-KAN variants on BabyLM; evaluate on BLiMP (59,875 pairs across 10 seeds), EWoK; larger tests on Wikitext-103; 286M-parameter GR-KAN.

Result: Reconstructed edges: 87.8% exceed (NLS>0.1); 0.4% inactive. Pruning 20–25% yields negligible loss; structured MLP neuron pruning tolerates similar sparsity. Grid-size sweeps show near-total fPCA compression and high closed-form-fit coverage tied to low-capacity grid-2 basis rather than universal KAN behavior. Replacement experiments: KAN-family and gated variants improve validation loss versus GELU MLP, but rankings do not consistently transfer to benchmarks. BLiMP results: 62.4–63.1% accuracy; EWoK at chance; GR-KAN +0.7 effect reverses in supplement. Larger tests: parameter-matched MLPEdge underperforms MLP on Wikitext-103; 286M GR-KAN remains below SwiGLU ClimbMix baseline after stabilization.

Conclusion: Small-basis KANs are a practical, corpus-transferable interface for auditing learned scalar transformations, but the tested replacements show no consistent benchmark, quality, or latency advantage over strong MLP baselines.

Abstract: Kolmogorov--Arnold Networks (KANs) replace fixed node activations with learned one-dimensional edge functions, offering an explicit interface for interpretation and a possible alternative to transformer feed-forward networks. We test these claims separately. In a six-layer, 10M-parameter B-spline KAN, we reconstruct all 884,736 feed-forward edges: 87.8\% exceed (NLS>0.1) and 0.4\% are inactive. Pruning the lowest-activity 20--25\% causes negligible loss increase, although structured MLP neuron pruning tolerates comparable sparsity. The audit replicates on BabyLM, but grid-size sweeps show that near-total fPCA compression and high closed-form-fit coverage are properties of the low-capacity grid-2 basis, not universal KAN behavior. For replacement, we evaluate MLP, SwiGLU, grouped Chebyshev, and rational GR-KAN networks on BabyLM. The KAN-family and gated variants improve validation loss over the GELU MLP, but this ordering does not transfer to standardized benchmarks: across ten seeds and 59,875 BLiMP pairs, accuracies span 62.4--63.1\%, EWoK remains at chance, and a (+0.7)-point GR-KAN effect on BLiMP reverses on the supplement. Larger tests are also cautionary: parameter-matched MLPEdge underperforms the MLP on Wikitext-103, and 286M-parameter GR-KAN remains below a SwiGLU ClimbMix baseline after stabilization. Thus, small-basis KANs provide a practical, corpus-transferable interface for auditing learned scalar transformations, but the tested replacements show no consistent benchmark, quality, or latency advantage over strong MLP baselines.

</details>


### [101] [Publicly-Verifiable Certificates for Statistical Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15528)
*Michael Ngo,Michael P. Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces publicly-verifiable certificates of statistical validity (pvCSVs) for non-interactive proofs of learning, focusing on Adaptive Statistical Query (SQ) algorithms; pvCSVs enable public verification of a learning hypothesis with distribution-dependent certificates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend interactive proofs of learning to non-interactive, publicly verifiable certificates that are robust to distributional assumptions and enable efficient verification by any user with a target distribution.

Method: Define pvCSV, construct pvCSVs for adaptive SQ algorithms, achieving sample complexity for certificate verification of O(log k) for certifying k adaptive queries, and compare to the learning-best complexity ~ tilde O(sqrt(k)). Study broader proof systems for learning within the SQ framework.

Result:  pvCSVs enable public, efficient verification of learning outputs under user-specific distributions; for k adaptive SQ queries, certification requires only O(log k) samples, contrasting with the ~sqrt(k) sample requirements of optimal learning algorithms; the work also characterizes strengths and limitations of SQ-based proof systems.

Conclusion: The pvCSV framework advances non-interactive, publicly-verifiable certification in learning, highlighting favorable verification complexity under the SQ model and clarifying the trade-offs and boundaries of SQ-based proof systems.

Abstract: Following Goldwasser, Rothblum, Shafer, and Yehudayoff, who defined a framework for interactive proofs of learning [ITCS'21], we initiate the study of non-interactive proofs of learning. We define and study a new notion: Publicly-Verifiable Certificates of Statistical Validity (pvCSVs), which allow for public, distributionally-robust certification that the result of a learning algorithm is valid. In a pvCSV, a learner publishes a hypothesis $h$ and corresponding certificate $π$; then, any user, who holds a user-specific distribution, can read the pair $(h,π)$ and determine efficiently whether the hypothesis is valid according to the user-specific distribution.
  We construct pvCSVs in the context of Adaptive Statistical Query (SQ) Algorithms. To certify SQ algorithms that makes $k$ adaptive queries, we construct pvCSVs where the sample complexity scales with $O(\log k)$, whereas the sample complexity of the best learning algorithms scale with $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{k})$. More generally, we study proof systems for learning in the SQ model, demonstrating the model's strengths as well as its limitations.

</details>


### [102] [From Feasibility to Desirability: Plan, Learn, Adapt (PLA) Framework for Personalized On-Device Itinerary Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15552)
*Himel Dev,Tanmoy Sen,Madhusudan Basak,Bashima Islam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PLA (Plan, Learn, Adapt) is a three-stage on-device framework for personalized itineraries that combines a diverse set of lightweight planners, a Bradley-Terry reward model learned from pairwise comparisons to capture pacing and coherence, and feasibility-preserving local refinement within device compute budgets. It yields 100% feasibility, strong user-preference alignment, and significant deployment gains over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the tension between hard feasibility constraints and soft subjective user preferences in personalized itineraries, while meeting mobile-device resource limits.

Method: Plan: create a heterogeneous ensemble of lightweight planners to generate diverse feasible candidates. Learn: fit a compact Bradley-Terry reward model from pairwise itinerary comparisons to capture emergent schedule properties (pacing, geographic coherence, day balance) not captured by per-POI features. Adapt: perform feasibility-preserving local refinement within a device-aware compute budget, ensuring every intermediate state is feasible.

Result: Evaluation on 2,519 pairwise comparisons across >100 U.S. cities shows reward-guided ensemble achieves 67.8% win rate (11.2 pp above best single planner) with 100% feasibility. Frontier LLMs (GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.5, Gemini 3 Pro) achieve 0% feasibility under the same constraints. Reward model generalizes with 67.6% leave-one-city-out accuracy. In production (FlyEnJoy), PLA increases itinerary completion rates by 91% and averages 109.9 ms on-device latency.

Conclusion: PLA demonstrates that a carefully staged combination of planning, learning from human comparisons, and feasibility-preserving adaptation can deliver feasible, personalized itineraries on-device, outperforming large-language-model baselines and enabling strong generalization and deployment gains.

Abstract: Generating personalized trip itineraries is a complex planning task and involves a tension between hard combinatorial feasibility and soft latent desirability. Classical optimization enforces constraints but fails to capture subjective traveler preferences. While learning-based approaches model preferences, they cannot guarantee feasibility. Mobile deployment imposes additional resource constraints on both. To address this, we propose Plan, Learn, Adapt (PLA), a three-stage framework for personalized on-device itinerary generation. The Plan stage builds a heterogeneous ensemble of lightweight planners that produces structurally diverse feasible candidates. From pairwise itinerary comparisons, Learn fits a compact Bradley-Terry reward model that captures emergent schedule properties such as pacing, geographic coherence, and day balance, which per-POI signals miss. Finally, Adapt applies feasibility-preserving local refinement within a device-aware compute budget; every intermediate state is feasible by construction. On 2,519 pairwise human comparisons across more than 100 U.S. cities, the reward-guided ensemble achieves a 67.8% win rate, 11.2 percentage points above the best single planner, with 100% feasibility. Three frontier LLMs, GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.5, and Gemini 3 Pro, achieve 0% feasibility under the same constraints. The reward model generalizes across held-out cities, with a 67.6% mean leave-one-city-out accuracy. In production deployment within FlyEnJoy, PLA increased itinerary completion rates by 91%, with 109.9 ms average on-device latency.

</details>


### [103] [Hard Rules, Soft Preferences: Bridging Reasoning, Learning, and Optimization for Personalized Packing Checklist Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15562)
*Himel Dev,Madhusudan Basak,Tanmoy Sen,Paromita Shome,Bashima Islam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A three-stage reasoning-guided learning framework for personal, regulation-aware packing checklists, combining symbolic constraints, preference learning, and CP-SAT optimization; achieves high feasibility and user alignment, with strong deployment gains in a production app.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Packing for air travel is a recurrent, error-prone task that requires personal context, regulatory compliance, item dependencies, and luggage limits. Existing solutions are template-driven or unconstrained, often yielding regulation or capacity violations. The work proposes a general pattern for constrained personalization that can handle hard feasibility with sparse preference signals.

Method: Stage 1: a symbolic engine generates a regulation-aware seed checklist with explicit dependency structure. Stage 2: a two-stage preference learner estimates inclusion and priority utilities from user add/remove actions while mitigating survivorship bias. Stage 3: a CP-SAT optimizer selects a compact, compliant subset. Evaluation uses 604 labeled trip scenarios (29K inclusion labels, 343K pairwise comparisons).

Result: The symbolic engine achieves 99.7% recall and 0.96 rubric validity (vs 0.78–0.81 for frontier LLMs). Gradient-boosted trees (GBDT) yield AUC-ROC 0.943; LambdaMART yields NDCG@5 0.923. CP-SAT attains 100% constraint satisfaction, vs 28% for greedy and 10% for random selection. Deployment in FlyEnJoy doubled checklist completions and reduced editing/completion time.

Conclusion: The architecture demonstrates a general pattern for constrained personalization where hard feasibility coexists with sparse preference signals, with applicability beyond packing to any domain requiring regulatory and capacity constraints alongside user preferences.

Abstract: Packing for air travel is recurring and error-prone: the checklist must be personal and context-aware, yet feasible under safety rules, item dependencies, and luggage limits. Existing packing assistants are template-driven and generic, or recommendation-driven but unconstrained, leaving users to manually patch regulatory and capacity violations. We propose a reasoning-guided learning framework with three stages: (1) a symbolic engine that generates a regulation-aware seed checklist with explicit dependency structure, (2) a two-stage preference learner that estimates inclusion and priority utilities from user add and remove actions while mitigating survivorship bias, and (3) a CP-SAT optimizer that selects a compact, compliant subset. The architecture instantiates a general pattern for constrained personalization, applicable wherever hard feasibility coexists with sparse preference signals. On 604 labeled trip scenarios, comprising 29K inclusion labels and 343K pairwise comparisons, the symbolic engine attains 99.7% recall and 0.96 rubric validity, compared with 0.78 to 0.81 for frontier LLMs. Gradient-boosted trees and LambdaMART reach an AUC-ROC of 0.943 and an NDCG@5 of 0.923. CP-SAT attains 100% constraint satisfaction, compared with 28% for greedy selection and 10% for random selection. Deployment in FlyEnJoy, a production iOS travel app, doubled checklist completions and reduced editing and completion time.

</details>


### [104] [Information-Directed Sampling for Causal Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15577)
*Muhammad Qasim Elahi,Murat Kocaoglu,Mahsa Ghasemi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bayesian contextual causal bandits with non-manipulable variables; known DAG and no latent confounding; develop Thompson Sampling and Information-Directed Sampling variants that leverage shared causal mechanisms via observational CPTs; establish entropy-based Bayesian and IDS regret bounds, with Monte Carlo error analysis; provide high-probability MC estimates and show empirical gains over causal/non-causal baselines on synthetic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In many settings some variables affect outcomes but cannot be directly manipulated. Exploiting the causal structure allows information from one intervention to update reward estimates for others, accelerating learning in contextual bandits.

Method: Adopt a Bayesian model where the observational CPTs are the unknown parameters; use observed context and post-intervention data to update posteriors. Develop causal variants of Thompson Sampling and Information-Directed Sampling that exploit shared causal mechanisms. Provide entropy-dependent regret bounds for TS, and entropy-dependent bounds for IDS that also account for Monte Carlo approximation error; supply high-probability bounds for MC estimates.

Result: Both TS and IDS achieve sublinear, entropy-dependent regret bounds. IDS bounds reduce to the standard IDS rate when MC estimates are exact. High-probability bounds are provided for the Monte Carlo estimates. Experiments on synthetic causal bandit tasks show the proposed methods outperform causal and non-causal baselines by effectively leveraging cross-intervention information."

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that modeling the observational CPTs in a known causal graph enables information sharing across interventions, yielding provable sublinear regret under both TS and IDS, with explicit accounting for MC approximation error in IDS. It advances causal bandit learning in settings with non-manipulable variables and motivates future work on relaxing graph knowledge and extending to latent confounding.

Abstract: Causal bandits exploit structural relationships among variables to share information across interventions and accelerate the identification of high-reward decisions. In many applications, however, some variables cannot be directly manipulated, even though they influence the reward and provide useful information about the underlying causal system. We study contextual causal bandits with non-manipulable variables, where context variables are observed before action selection and additional variables are observed after each intervention. Assuming a known causal graph without latent confounding, we adopt a Bayesian formulation in which the conditional probability tables of the observational distribution constitute the unknown parameter. This representation allows observations collected under one intervention to update reward estimates for other interventions through their shared causal mechanisms. We develop causal variants of Thompson Sampling and Information-Directed Sampling (IDS) for this setting. For Thompson Sampling, we establish an entropy-dependent sublinear Bayesian regret bound. For IDS, we derive an entropy-dependent regret bound that explicitly quantifies the additional error introduced by Monte Carlo approximation of the expected regret and information gain; when these quantities are available exactly, the bound recovers the standard sublinear IDS rate. We further provide high-probability confidence bounds for the Monte Carlo estimates used by the algorithm. Experiments on several synthetic causal bandit tasks show that the proposed methods outperform causal and non-causal baselines by more effectively exploiting information shared across interventions.

</details>


### [105] [Rethinking Transfer in Continual Learning: A Replay-Based Realisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15587)
*Yang Meng,Zhenya Liu,Zhuokai Zhao,Yuxin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transfer-Selective Replay (TSR) framework: selectively replay past data to aid new tasks by predicting which past experiences will benefit the incoming task, improving forward transfer while preserving past task stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between forward transfer and practical replay strategies by formalizing when transfer can occur and ensuring replay data is compatible with prior knowledge while aiding future tasks.

Method: Three measurable conditions for transfer: room for improvement; information survives continued optimization; replay from compatible tasks. TSR selects replay data via a zero-training task signature; uses distillation to stabilize previous tasks; evaluates under low-budget continual learning against baselines.

Result: Empirically better forward transfer and preserved stability across diverse task streams compared to standard replay baselines.

Conclusion: Transfer should be a first-class objective; design transfer-aware mechanisms prior to engineering transfer; TSR provides a concrete approach and validation.

Abstract: Continual learning studies how deployed language models can continually acquire new tasks without expensive retraining from scratch. Existing methods, whether rehearsal-based (replaying stored past data) or rehearsal-free (regularising or isolating parameters), overwhelmingly target one objective: preventing catastrophic forgetting. Forward transfer, the past helping the future, has meanwhile been pursued almost exclusively through parameter reuse, with no explicit account of when transfer should be expected at all. We begin one step earlier: before designing a transfer mechanism, we ask when transfer should exist at all. We answer with a framework of three measurable conditions: the target task must leave room for improvement beyond its own limited supervision, transferable information must survive continued optimisation, and replay must come from compatible previous tasks. We instantiate this view as Transfer-Selective Replay (TSR), which selects replay data predicted to benefit the incoming task rather than replaying past examples indiscriminately. Selection is guided by a zero-training task signature, while distillation preserves stability on previous tasks. Under the standard continual learning protocol in the low-budget regime, TSR consistently improves forward transfer while maintaining stability, outperforming existing replay baselines across heterogeneous and homogeneous task streams. More broadly, the results argue for treating transfer as a first-class objective of continual learning, to be understood before it is engineered.

</details>


### [106] [Field-Aware RankMixer with Dual-Stream Bilinear Fusion for the Tencent UNI-REC Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15590)
*Yufeng Zhang,Zhengqi Xu,Jiajun Cui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Field-Aware RankMixer (FA-RankMixer) with dual-stream bilinear fusion for joint modeling of multi-domain user behavior sequences and non-sequential multi-field features to predict target-ad pCVR; achieves 9th place on the KDD Cup 2026 Tencent UNIREC Challenge; code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The task requires integrating sequential user behavior across multiple domains with non-sequential multi-field features for accurate pCVR prediction. Existing approaches often treat modalities or domains separately, potentially missing cross-domain interactions.

Method: - Apply target-aware DIN modules to extract user interests from multiple behavior domains. - Model recent and earlier interests separately for the longest behavior sequence. - Form semantic tokens based on feature fields and behavior domains. - Use RankMixer blocks for cross-token interaction. - Include a shallow MLP stream to complement the deep RankMixer stream. - Fuse representations with a group-wise bilinear module (dual-stream bilinear fusion).

Result: Final solution ranked ninth on the official leaderboard; code released at the provided GitHub link.

Conclusion: FA-RankMixer demonstrates that combining target-aware interest extraction across domains with token-level cross-token interaction and dual-stream fusion can effectively handle joint modeling of heterogeneous features for pCVR prediction; the architecture offers a path for balancing deep interaction modeling with a lightweight MLP branch.

Abstract: This paper presents our solution to the KDD Cup 2026 Tencent UNIREC Challenge. The task requires joint modeling of multi-domain user behavior sequences and non-sequential multi-field features for target-ad pCVR prediction. We develop a Field-Aware RankMixer (FA-RankMixer) with dual-stream bilinear fusion. The model first applies target-aware DIN modules to extract user interests from multiple behavior domains. It also models recent and earlier interests separately for the longest behavior sequence. The model then forms semantic tokens based on feature fields and behavior domains and uses RankMixer blocks for cross-token interaction. A shallow MLP stream complements the deep RankMixer stream, and a group-wise bilinear module fuses their representations. Our final solution ranks ninth on the official leaderboard. Our code is available at https://github.com/PixelCookie-zyf/TAAC-2026-SeRankMixer.

</details>


### [107] [Do Generative Models Keep Time? A Time-Aware Evaluation of Synthetic Sequential Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15606)
*Kiwan Kwon,Kangmin Kim,Hojin Lee,Yeseong Jung,Hyeongwoo Kong,Vamsi K. Potluru,Saerom Park,Yongjae Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A taxonomy-guided protocol to evaluate temporal fidelity in synthetic sequential tabular data, showing that conventional pooled-evaluation misses time-axis errors and altering model rankings; temporal evaluation is essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address privacy-preserving synthetic data that may emit temporally invalid patterns (backwards/repeated timestamps, impossible trajectories). Static, pooled evaluations miss such failures, risking privacy and data utility.

Method: Develop a taxonomy-guided evaluation protocol. Characterize datasets along four properties (time representation, regular sampling, trajectory interdependence, schema-history linkage). Determine meaningful evaluation dimensions from these properties. Measure timestamp validity, cross-sectional structure at aligned times, within-entity dynamics, and time-varying relational structure. Recast utility/privacy as trajectory-based metrics. Apply protocol to eight generative models across thirteen datasets in six domains.

Result: Conventional evaluations disagree substantially with the temporal evaluation rankings. Failures are coherent with model architectures rather than random. Temporal fidelity on the time axis reveals issues that pooled distributions miss.

Conclusion: Temporal fidelity must be measured on the time axis itself, not inferred from pooled record distributions; the proposed taxonomy-guided protocol enables meaningful temporal evaluation across diverse datasets.

Abstract: Synthetic sequential tabular data are increasingly used for privacy-preserving data sharing, yet a generator can reproduce every marginal and every foreign-key relationship while emitting timestamps that run backwards or repeat, and while sending entities along paths that no real entity followed. Conventional tabular evaluation, which pools records into static distributions, is blind to such failures. We present a taxonomy-guided evaluation protocol for temporal fidelity, in which the applicable measurements are determined by the data rather than fixed in advance. Each dataset is first characterized along four properties: how time is represented, whether observations are regularly sampled, whether trajectories are mutually dependent, and how the schema links entities to their histories. These properties determine which evaluation dimensions are meaningful. The protocol then measures timestamp validity, cross-sectional structure at aligned time points, within-entity dynamics, and time-varying relational structure, and recasts utility and privacy evaluation over trajectories rather than isolated rows. We apply the protocol to eight generative models across thirteen datasets spanning six domains. Rankings under conventional evaluation disagree substantially with those obtained under temporal evaluation, and the resulting failures are architecture-coherent rather than random. Temporal fidelity must therefore be measured on the time axis itself, rather than inferred from pooled record distributions.

</details>


### [108] [ASK-NN: An Asymmetric Nearest-Neighbor Test that detects Distribution Drifts in Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15607)
*Sergey Zakharov,Rodion Oblovatny,Alexey Zaytsev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Asymmetric two-sample test ASK-NN (directed k-NN graph) for prompt vs. response distributions in LLM outputs; introduces a finite-sample permutation-based inference with asymptotic normality, showing competitive performance for artificial text and hallucination detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompts (reference) and responses (query) differ in length and context; symmetric two-sample tests may misrepresent distributional shifts in LLM outputs. An asymmetric test tailored to the prompt–response setup aims to better detect distributional deviations indicative of hallucinations or artificial text.

Method: Define ASK-NN statistic: count reference points whose nearest neighbor in the pooled sample is also a reference point. Use a directed kNN graph; under the permutation null, obtain exact finite-sample conditional mean and variance; prove asymptotic normality and consistency under fixed alternatives. Implementation is computationally efficient and straightforward.

Result: Empirically competitive with kernel and graph-based baselines on synthetic benchmarks, artificial-text detection, and LLM-hallu cination detection from token-level hidden states; demonstrates effectiveness and practicality.

Conclusion: ASK-NN provides an effective, efficient asymmetric two-sample test for prompt–response distributions in LLM outputs, with strong finite-sample and asymptotic properties and solid empirical performance.

Abstract: Hallucinations and artificial text in LLM-generated outputs often appear as distributional deviations between prompt and response hidden-state distributions. Since prompts or retrieved contexts typically serve as reference samples and responses as query samples, with major differences in length, these asymmetries motivate the use of change test statistics that treat the two samples differently. We consider an asymmetric two-sample test ASK-NN based on the directed k-nearest-neighbor graph. Our statistic counts reference points whose nearest neighbor in the pooled sample is also a reference point. Under the permutation null, it admits an exact finite-sample conditional mean and variance; we further establish asymptotic normality and consistency under fixed alternatives. ASK-NN is computationally effective and easy to implement. Empirically, it is competitive with kernel and graph-based baselines on synthetic benchmarks, artificial-text detection, and LLM hallucination detection from token-level hidden states.

</details>


### [109] [Neural Non-Equilibrium Hamiltonian Monte Carlo for Corrected Boltzmann Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15682)
*Moxian Qian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NHMC is a train-then-correct learned Hamiltonian sampler for unnormalized Boltzmann densities, using path-based proposals and non-equilibrium work for corrections; enables path-SNIS, path-IMH, and a shared-bridge MH kernel. Performance hinges on path overlap; works on double-well and φ^4 lattice targets; also explores MD-prior/月 learned-force paths for molecular coordinates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve global, information-rich proposals for sampling from unnormalized Boltzmann densities while maintaining exactness via principled corrections, addressing path-space mismatch and improving efficiency over traditional MCMC.

Method: Learn stochastic Hamiltonian-style paths starting from a tractable base distribution; fix proposal parameters after training; use non-equilibrium work defined by forward-reverse path probability ratios as the bridge for importance weights and MH decisions; apply path-space SNIS and a shared-bridge Metropolis kernel (path-IMH); evaluate on lattice targets; include an MD-prior / learned-force path proposal for molecular coordinates.

Result: Corrected estimates when path overlap is sufficient; weight degeneracy and low acceptance when overlap is poor; long autocorrelation indicates proposal failure under such conditions; demonstrates feasibility with molecular internal-coordinate paths.

Conclusion: NHMC provides a principled framework integrating learned proposals with path-space corrections; success relies on adequate path overlap and accurate non-equilibrium work; capable of estimating normalizing constants and free-energy differences, and generalizable to MD-prior/learned-force paths, with noted limitations on overlap and computational cost.

Abstract: Sampling from an unnormalized Boltzmann density requires proposals that move probability mass globally while retaining enough path-probability information for statistical correction. We introduce Neural Non-Equilibrium Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (NHMC), a train-then-correct learned Hamiltonian sampler. Starting from a tractable base distribution, NHMC learns stochastic Hamiltonian-style paths toward the target. Once training is complete, the learned proposal parameters are fixed; the proposal then generates complete paths and endpoint configurations, which are statistically corrected using the recorded non-equilibrium work. This dimensionless generalized work is determined by the probability ratio between the forward proposal path and a reverse reference path. During training, minimizing its mean reduces a path-space KL divergence and controls an upper bound on endpoint mismatch. During evaluation, the same quantity defines weights for self-normalized importance sampling on paths (path-SNIS), estimates normalizing constants or free-energy differences, and gives the acceptance ratio for path-space independent Metropolis-Hastings (path-IMH). The same forward-reverse laws also define a shared-bridge round-trip Metropolis kernel that acts directly on configurations and preserves the Boltzmann target. On double-well and finite-volume lattice $φ^4$ targets, the NHMC construction gives corrected estimates when path overlap is sufficient; when overlap is poor, weight degeneracy, low acceptance, and long autocorrelation expose proposal failure. We additionally report a molecular internal-coordinate feasibility study using an MD prior and learned-force path proposal.

</details>


### [110] [Toward Federated Multimodal Graph Foundation Models: A Topology-Aware Multimodal Alignment Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15687)
*Xunkai Li,Guohao Fu,Yuming Ai,Zhengyu Wu,Hongchao Qin,Rong-Hua Li,Guoren Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedGAMMA introduces federated multimodal graph foundation learning via two-stage semantic–structural alignment: federated pre-training with a shared-private semantic enhancer, topology-aware graph fusion, and privacy-preserving client similarity, followed by prompt-based fine-tuning; it achieves strong cross-domain and few-shot gains on MAG benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal attributed graphs offer richer signals but data are siloed across privacy-bound platforms. Existing methods address either multimodal graphs or federated learning, not both, hindering transferable, privacy-preserving models. FedGAMMA aims to bridge these aspects through a two-stage architecture that preserves data privacy while aligning semantic and structural information.

Method: Pre-training stage: (1) shared-private semantic enhancer disentangles cross-modal commonality from modality-specific signals; (2) optimal transport aligns cross-modal semantics; (3) topology-aware graph fusion decouples semantic and structural views via semantic residual graphs and dual positional encodings; (4) dual-channel affinity-aware aggregation estimates client similarity from feature and graph centroids without exposing raw data. Fine-tuning stage: graph-aware prompts for adaptation; a shared prompt pool with controlled exploration; channel-wise prompt synchronization.

Result: Empirical evaluation on twelve multimodal graph datasets shows FedGAMMA consistently outperforms baselines across downstream tasks, with gains up to 12.96%. In few-shot settings across multi-domain datasets and tasks, it achieves up to 5.71% improvements.

Conclusion: FedGAMMA effectively unifies federated learning and multimodal graph foundation learning by aligning semantic and structural representations in a privacy-preserving manner, with strong cross-domain and few-shot performance and scalable prompts-based fine-tuning.

Abstract: Multimodal-attributed graphs (MAGs), whose nodes carry modalities such as images and text alongside topological structure, now pervade applications including social platforms, e-commerce, and biomedical networks, offering richer semantic signals than single-modality graphs. In practice, such graphs are fragmented across privacy-restricted silos owned by different platforms and institutions, so learning a broadly transferable model over them demands collaborative training that never exposes raw data. This places the task at the intersection of multimodal graph learning and federated learning, yet existing methods cover only one side of it. To address the challenges from these two perspectives, we propose FedGAMMA, casting federated multimodal graph foundation learning as a two-stage semantic-structural alignment problem of federated pre-training and prompt-based fine-tuning. During pre-training, a shared-private semantic enhancer disentangles cross-modal commonality from modality-specific information, aligning it through optimal transport, a topology-aware graph fusion module decouples semantic and structural views via semantic residual graphs and dual positional encodings, and a dual-channel affinity-aware aggregation mechanism estimates client similarity from feature and graph centroids without exposing raw data. During fine-tuning, FedGAMMA adapts the pretrained encoder through lightweight graph-aware prompts, a shared prompt pool with controlled exploration, and channel-wise prompt synchronization. Experiments on twelve multimodal graph datasets show FedGAMMA consistently surpassing a broad range of baselines across downstream tasks, with gains of up to 12.96%. FedGAMMA further outperforms competitive baselines accross multi-domain datasets on multiple tasks with up to 5.71% under few-shot learning scenario.

</details>


### [111] [A Benchmark for Electrical Load Forecasting Across Grid Levels: Time-Series Transformers Outperform Established Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15705)
*Matthias Hertel,Sebastian Pütz,Jonathan Kolar,Benjamin Schäfer,Ralf Mikut,Veit Hagenmeyer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transformer-based load forecasting significantly improves accuracy across grid levels; standard Transformer outperforms a reformulated YAformer; Chronos-2 shows zero-shot potential but misses events; long context, covariates, and continual retraining are crucial; the study provides a three-dataset benchmark for multi-level load forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate short-term load forecasting across transmission, distribution, and end-user levels is essential for grid operation and management; a unified benchmark evaluating diverse methods and architectures is needed to guide future research.

Method: Benchmark study using three datasets (TSO control area, LV feeders, end customers) to evaluate ten short-term load forecasting methods. Introduces YAformer (hybrid Transformer) and assesses Chronos-2 as a time-series foundation model. Performs ablations on input context, covariates, and retraining regimes.

Result: Transformers consistently outperform established methods, reducing forecast error by 6.6–10.7%. The standard Transformer surpasses YAformer, indicating architectural modifications are unnecessary for this task. Chronos-2 exhibits competitive zero-shot performance on two datasets but fails to capture special events in the TSO data. Ablation analyses show that long input contexts, covariates, and continuous retraining are important factors often overlooked in time-series forecasting literature.

Conclusion: Architectural changes to Transformer are not required for accurate multi-level load forecasting. Emphasize data-related aspects (long history, covariates, ongoing retraining) and consider robust event handling in foundation models like Chronos-2. The benchmark highlights strengths/weaknesses of different models and the value of long input contexts and covariate incorporation.

Abstract: Accurate load forecasting at multiple grid levels is essential for future smart grids, ranging from aggregated control area forecasts for balancing supply and demand to forecasts of individual end-consumer loads for demand-side management and energy management systems. We present a comprehensive benchmark for load forecasting across grid levels, comprising three datasets that represent a transmission system operator control area, low-voltage grid feeders, and individual end consumers. We evaluate ten methods for short-term load forecasting and find that Transformer-based approaches consistently outperform established methods, reducing forecast error by 6.6-10.7 %. To analyze the impact of architectural design, we introduce YAformer, a flexible Transformer architecture that integrates modifications from prior work and is optimized via hyperparameter optimization. However, the standard Transformer achieves superior performance, suggesting that these architectural modifications are not required for accurate load forecasting. We further evaluate the Transformer-based time-series foundation model Chronos-2, which demonstrates competitive zero-shot performance on two datasets but fails to accurately capture special events in the TSO data. Detailed analyses reveal model-specific strengths and weaknesses, and ablation studies highlight the importance of long input contexts, covariates and continuous retraining - aspects that are often overlooked in the time-series forecasting literature.

</details>


### [112] [CardioMeta: Calibrated Multi-Task Prediction of Diabetes, Hypertension, and Cardiovascular Disease Across Population and EHR Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15721)
*S M Asif Hossain,Ruksat Khan Shayoni,M. F. Mridha,Jungpil Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Calibrated multi-task model CardioMeta jointly predicts diabetes, hypertension, and cardiovascular disease across NHANES and MIMIC-IV, emphasizing leakage control and calibrated probabilities over raw accuracy; shows domain-shift challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address fragmentation in chronic-disease ML by focusing on leakage reduction, calibration, temporal robustness, and external transportability across heterogeneous data sources.

Method: Multi-task framework with a shared cardiometabolic encoder and disease-specific gated heads; disease-defining variables excluded from prediction heads to reduce label leakage; full feature set used as sensitivity analysis; post-hoc calibration; trained on NHANES with temporal validation; evaluated on MIMIC-IV under distribution shift; compared to gradient boosting and neural baselines.

Result: In leakage-reduced temporal validation: macro-AUROC 0.839, macro-AUPRC 0.536, macro-F1 0.614, ECE 0.024; modest gains over baselines; external MIMIC-IV evaluation shows degradation under domain shift; limited fine-tuning partially recovers performance.

Conclusion: Multi-task cardiometabolic modeling offers reproducible leakage control and calibrated, reliable predictions across heterogeneous data; strong calibration and transparent reliability reporting are the principal values, but external transportability remains challenged by domain shift.

Abstract: Cardiometabolic diseases remain among the most persistent drivers of preventable morbidity because diabetes, hypertension, and cardiovascular disease frequently co-occur and share metabolic, vascular, demographic, and behavioral determinants. Existing machine learning studies for chronic disease prediction often emphasize discrimination on a single dataset, while underreporting label leakage, calibration, temporal robustness, external transportability, and subgroup reliability. This paper presents CardioMeta, a calibrated multi-task framework for joint prediction of diabetes, hypertension, and cardiovascular disease across population survey and electronic health record (EHR) data. The study uses NHANES for population-level model development and temporal validation, and MIMIC-IV for EHR-domain evaluation under substantial distribution shift. To reduce circular label reconstruction, the primary analysis excludes disease-defining variables from the corresponding prediction heads, while a full-clinical feature setting is retained only as sensitivity analysis. CardioMeta combines a shared cardiometabolic encoder with disease-specific gated heads and post-hoc probability calibration. In the leakage-reduced temporal validation setting, the model achieved a macro-AUROC of 0.839, macro-AUPRC of 0.536, macro-F1 of 0.614, and expected calibration error of 0.024, with modest but consistent improvements over strong gradient-boosting and neural tabular baselines. External evaluation on MIMIC-IV showed clear degradation under domain shift, while limited fine-tuning partially recovered performance. The findings indicate that the principal value of multi-task cardiometabolic modeling lies not in inflated accuracy, but in reproducible leakage control, calibrated probabilities, and transparent reliability reporting across heterogeneous healthcare data sources.

</details>


### [113] [Learning Faster without Deeper Networks: A*-Inspired Batch Selection for Efficient CNN Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15745)
*Anxhelo Shehu,Enes Stastoli,Arben Cela*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A*-Inspired Batch Selection (A*-BS) treats mini-batch scheduling as a heuristic search problem, using an A*-like score that combines loss-based difficulty with a reuse penalty to pick informative, diverse batches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address slow convergence and diminishing learning signal from random mini-batch shuffling; enable informative gradient updates and batch diversity without changing architectures or optimizers.

Method: Treat each batch as a node in a search space; compute A*-like score combining a loss-based difficulty measure and a reuse penalty; lightweight, model-agnostic; evaluate on MedMNIST-v2 12 tasks with a ~225k-parameter CNN; compare to ResNet-18/50 Baselines.

Result: On half of the tasks, the lightweight model with A*-BS achieves higher accuracy and AUC than the ResNet baselines (up to 15% relative gains). Ablation shows A*-BS outperforms random shuffling on all tasks. Wall-clock training is substantially faster than ResNet-18/50 on identical hardware.

Conclusion: Intelligent batch ordering can partially compensate for reduced architectural complexity, offering a computationally efficient alternative to deeper models with robust performance.

Abstract: Common practice when training Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) is to use randomly shuffled mini-batches. This creates two limitations: slower convergence, and a diminishing learning signal, since many samples are quickly classified as easy during training. We address these inefficiencies with A*-Inspired Batch Selection (A*-BS), a lightweight, model-agnostic strategy that formulates mini-batch scheduling as a heuristic search problem. Each batch is treated as a node in a search space and ranked using an A*-like score combining a loss-based difficulty measure with a reuse penalty. This encourages informative gradient updates and batch diversity throughout training, without modifying network architectures or optimization algorithms, so it integrates seamlessly into existing pipelines. We evaluate A*-BS on the twelve 2D classification tasks of the MedMNIST-v2 benchmark, using a deliberately simple architecture of approximately 2.25x10^5 parameters, compared against the ResNet-18 and ResNet-50 baselines reported by the benchmark. On half of these tasks, the lightweight model with A*-BS reaches higher accuracy and AUC than both ResNet baselines, with relative gains of up to 15%. An ablation under identical architecture and hyperparameters shows A*-BS outperforms random batch shuffling on all twelve tasks. Wall-clock measurements further show the lightweight CNN with A*-BS trains substantially faster than ResNet-18 and ResNet-50 on identical hardware. These results indicate that intelligent batch ordering can partially compensate for reduced architectural complexity, offering a computationally efficient alternative to deeper models, with reliability reinforced by strong performance even against deeper, more sophisticated architectures.

</details>


### [114] [Trainable Spline Representations for Physics-Informed Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15751)
*Giovanni Canali,Nicola Demo,Gianluigi Rozza*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A spline-based alternative to physics-informed learning that directly parametrizes the field with tensor-product B-splines, achieving residual-based training with locality, smoothness control, and competitive performance vs PINNs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of neural networks in physics-informed learning by introducing a structured, efficient, and interpretable representation that provides explicit smoothness control, analytical derivatives, and strong boundary handling, increasing parameter efficiency and locality.

Method: Parametrize the unknown field with a tensor-product B-spline expansion; train the accompanying control coefficients; preserve the residual-based training paradigm of PINNs; impose boundary conditions strongly by fixing boundary coefficients when compatible with the spline representation; compare against standard physics-informed frameworks under matched governing equations, collocation sets, loss terms, and optimization procedures.

Result: PI-Splines are competitive and stable relative to neural PINNs, particularly in settings where structure, locality, and parameter efficiency are advantageous; numerical experiments show favorable performance under fair comparisons.

Conclusion: Structured spline-based representations can match or surpass neural PINNs under fair comparisons, offering explicit smoothness control, analytic derivatives, locality, and geometric interpretability, making them a viable alternative for physics-informed learning.

Abstract: This work introduces Physics-Informed Splines (PI-Splines), a structured spline-based architecture for physics-informed learning. Instead of representing the solution of a differential equation with a neural network, PI-Splines directly parametrize the unknown field through a tensor-product B-spline expansion with trainable control coefficients. This formulation preserves the residual-based training paradigm of Physics-Informed Neural Networks while providing compact support, explicit smoothness control, analytical derivatives, and a direct geometric interpretation of the trainable parameters. When compatible with the spline representation, boundary conditions can be imposed strongly by fixing suitable boundary control coefficients. The proposed method is evaluated on several benchmark problems of increasing difficulty and compared with standard physics-informed frameworks under matched governing equations, collocation sets, loss terms, and optimization procedures, so as to isolate the effect of the approximation architecture. Numerical experiments show that PI-Splines provide a competitive and stable alternative to neural physics-informed architectures, particularly in settings where structured representations, locality, and parameter efficiency are desirable.

</details>


### [115] [CoG-Guided Weight Correction for Fault-Tolerant Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15753)
*Bahram Parchekani,Samira Nazari,Ali Azarpeyvand,Mohammad Hasan Ahmadilivani,Tara Ghasempouri,Jaan Raik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoG-guided weight correction improves NN fault tolerance without retraining, achieving large fault-margin gains on LSTM and CNN models under hardware faults (BER=1e-3).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enhance reliability of safety-critical DNNs under hardware-induced weight faults without retraining or architectural changes; exploit spatial structure of weight tensors via Center of Gravity (CoG).

Method: Per-layer, distance-aware CoG-based rules detect and correct faulty weights; corrections rely on spatial characteristics rather than retraining; evaluated via fault injection at various BERs across LSTM (StageNet, MTFNet) and CNN (ResNet-18, VGG-16).

Result: Reported fault-tolerance gains up to 230× (LSTM StageNet) and 6.41× (MTFNet), and up to 49.55× (ResNet-18) and 20.79× (VGG-16) at BER=1e-3, with negligible accuracy loss; claims novelty in applying CoG to weight tensors.

Conclusion: CoG-based weight correction is a lightweight, retraining-free approach to improve NN reliability in fault-prone hardware; first to apply CoG to weight tensors; promising for safety-critical deployments, though broader validation and theoretical grounding are desirable.

Abstract: Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) used in safety-critical applications are vulnerable to hardware and memory faults that corrupt network weights and degrade reliability. In this paper, we propose a Center of Gravity (CoG) guided weight correction method that restores faulty weights based on their spatial characteristics within each layer. The proposed approach detects and corrects weight faults using distance-aware correction rules, eliminating the need for retraining or architectural modification. The effectiveness of the proposed method in terms of the capability of tolerating hardware faults has been evaluated through performing fault injection at different Bit Error Rates (BERs).
  Experiments on safety-critical LSTM-based Networks, including StageNet for disease progression tracking and MTFNet for cardiac anomaly detection, demonstrate fault tolerance improvements of up to 230x and 6.41x, respectively, at a BER of 10^{-3}, with negligible accuracy loss. When extended to Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), the method achieves up to 49.55x and 20.79x improvements under comparable fault conditions on ResNet-18 and VGG-16, respectively. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work to apply the CoG concept to neural network weight tensors for enhancing model reliability.

</details>


### [116] [From Diffusion to Reaction-Diffusion: A Dynamical-Systems View of Oversmoothing in Hypergraph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15773)
*Zhiheng Zhou,Mengyao Zhou,Yancheng Chen,Dengyi Zhao,Xingqin Qi,Guiying Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hypergraph oversmoothing is addressed by a reaction–diffusion framework that treats hypergraph message passing as diffusion while a reaction term stabilizes the transverse component, yielding depth-resistant HGNNs (HNRD) with provable well-posedness and energy lower-boundedness; empirical results show improved performance and stability on heterophilic hypergraphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Higher-order couplings in HGNNs enable expressive representations but amplify oversmoothing with depth; a principled dynamical-systems perspective is needed to maintain discriminative information while leveraging hypergraph structure.

Method: Define hypergraph gradient and divergence to cast message passing as a diffusion process on the incidence structure; analyze pure diffusion showing exponential contraction of the non-null component and Dirichlet energy decay; introduce Hypergraph Neural Reaction–Diffusion (HNRD) with a reaction term acting on the transverse component; prove global well-posedness and energy lower-boundedness; discretize with forward Euler and derive stability conditions.

Result: HNRD consistently improves performance over hypergraph baselines on both benchmark and synthetic heterophilic hypergraphs; depth robustness and perturbation stability are demonstrated; Dirichlet energy stays nonzero and well-behaved under deep propagation.

Conclusion: A principled dynamical framework for deep hypergraph learning is established, enabling higher-order expressiveness without representation collapse through coupled diffusion and reaction dynamics.

Abstract: Higher-order couplings enhance the expressive power of hypergraph neural networks (HGNNs), but they also intensify representation collapse in deep propagation due to strong multi-way feature mixing. This work investigates hypergraph oversmoothing from a dynamical-systems perspective and develops a reaction--diffusion framework for depth-resistant hypergraph learning. By defining hypergraph gradient and divergence operators, we interpret message passing as an incidence-level diffusion process. The analysis of pure diffusion shows that its continuous semiflow exponentially contracts the null-mode-free component of node representations and drives the Dirichlet energy to zero, revealing hypergraph oversmoothing as an intrinsic transverse-energy dissipation phenomenon. Motivated by this analysis, we propose Hypergraph Neural Reaction--Diffusion (HNRD), which introduces a reaction mechanism acting on the transverse component to compensate diffusion-induced dissipation and stabilize discriminative variations. We establish global well-posedness of the proposed dynamics and prove that the null-mode-free Dirichlet energy remains bounded away from zero. A forward-Euler discretization provides a practical HNRD layer with a stability condition for deep propagation. Experiments on benchmark and synthetic heterophilic hypergraphs demonstrate that HNRD consistently improves over representative hypergraph baselines. Depth, robustness, and efficiency analyses further show that HNRD preserves stable performance and nonzero Dirichlet energy under deep propagation and perturbations. These results provide a principled dynamical framework for designing deep hypergraph architectures that maintain higher-order expressiveness without representation collapse.

</details>


### [117] [Scaling Time Series Classification via XAI-Driven Data Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15774)
*Davide Italo Serramazza,Thach Le Nguyen,Georgiana Ifrim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: drXAI repurposes XAI attributions for data reduction in Time Series Classification, achieving high reduction (80-90%) with comparable accuracy and enabling scalable modeling on large datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: XAI methods in time series lack demonstrated performance gains for downstream tasks; modern TSC models (e.g., Transformers) are computationally heavy (quadratic in sequence length, linear in channels), limiting scalability. A fast, automated feature selection via explanations could improve efficiency without sacrificing accuracy.

Method: Use Hydra, a GPU-accelerated classifier, to generate local attributions; aggregate to global feature importances; apply elbow-cut heuristic to select salient features without manual thresholds; evaluate on synthetic and real-world univariate/multivariate data; compare to full-data baselines.

Result: On synthetic data, drXAI recovers ground-truth features where baselines fail. On real data, achieves 80-90% data reduction while maintaining accuracy; enables ConvTran to scale to previously memory-constrained datasets; code and data openly available.

Conclusion: XAI-based data reduction is a viable tool for feature selection and scalability in time series analysis; drXAI demonstrates that attribution-guided reduction can preserve performance while reducing data and enabling larger models.

Abstract: Explainable AI (XAI) for time series has seen significant algorithmic growth, but its utility in providing measurable performance gains for downstream tasks remains under-explored. This paper bridges this gap by introducing drXAI, a novel methodology that repurposes XAI attribution methods for effective data reduction in Time Series Classification (TSC). The core challenge in modern TSC is scalability; state-of-the-art models, such as Transformers, exhibit quadratic complexity relative to sequence length and linear complexity relative to the number of channels. This renders them computationally prohibitive for massive datasets. drXAI addresses this by using a fast, GPU-accelerated classifier (Hydra) to generate local attributions. We aggregate these into global feature importance scores and employ an automated elbow-cut heuristic to select the most salient features without requiring manual thresholds.
  We evaluate our approach on both synthetic and real-world univariate and multivariate datasets. On synthetic benchmarks, drXAI successfully recovers ground-truth features where traditional baselines fail. On real-world data, drXAI achieves between 80% and 90% data reduction while maintaining classification accuracy comparable to models trained on the full dataset. Most importantly, we show that drXAI allows resource-intensive models like ConvTran to scale to datasets that were previously inaccessible due to memory constraints. Our results show the benefits of using XAI not just for interpretability, but as a robust tool for feature selection and scalability in time series analysis. All our code and data are openly available.

</details>


### [118] [AquaAugmentor: A Novel Feature Augmentation Algorithm for Water Potability Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15775)
*Muntasir Tabasum,Al Zadid Sultan Bin Habib,Tanpia Tasnim,Md. Ekramul Islam,Md Younus Ahamed,Md Asif Bin Syed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AquaAugmentor, a novel feature augmentation approach, enhances ML/DL-based water potability classification on low-dimensional chemical datasets, evaluated via accuracy and AUC with and without augmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Water potability prediction is challenging due to variability and limited informative features; improving predictive performance supports health, policy, and environmental decisions.

Method: Proposes AquaAugmentor and evaluates ML and DL models on a dataset of chemical water attributes (pH, hardness, solids, chloramines, sulfate, etc.), comparing performance with and without AquaAugmentor using test accuracy and AUC.

Result: AquaAugmentor shows strengths and limitations in improving predictive performance; the study provides insights into effective techniques for water quality classification but no specific quantitative results are reported in the abstract.

Conclusion: The proposed AquaAugmentor offers a framework to enhance ML-based water quality classification, contributing to safer water access and informing researchers, policymakers, and public health officials.

Abstract: Access to potable water is crucial for health, economic development, and sustainability. However, accurately classifying water quality remains a significant challenge due to the complexity and variability of water source data. This paper addresses the challenge of predicting water potability through machine learning and deep learning algorithms. It introduces a novel feature augmentation algorithm, AquaAugmentor, to enhance the predictive performance of these models for low-dimensional datasets. Utilizing a dataset that includes chemical attributes of water, such as pH, hardness, solids, chloramines, sulfate, and others. This study evaluates the performance of the models with and without AquaAugmentor. Each model applied to classify water as potable or non-potable and its performance is then evaluated and compared based on test accuracy and AUC score. The results highlight the strengths and limitations of our proposed algorithm, providing insights into the most effective techniques for improving the predictive performance of water quality classification. This study contributes to the broader efforts of ensuring safe water access and serves as a framework for employing machine learning in environmental quality assessments. The findings aim to assist researchers, policymakers, and public health officials in making informed decisions based on reliable machine learning predictions.

</details>


### [119] [Knowledge-Assisted Multi-Graph Dependency Learning for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection in Multi-Stage Industrial Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15799)
*Jaeyeong Lee,Taeseong Yoon,Wonmo Koo,Heeyoung Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A knowledge-guided, multi-graph framework for MTAD in multi-stage industrial processes, using three graphs (data-driven plus two knowledge-refined) and a multi-graph attention network to improve anomaly detection; evaluated on two real datasets with improved performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MTAD in industrial multi-stage processes faces complex interdependencies; pure data-driven GNNs miss valuable process knowledge, limiting detection performance.

Method: Construct three graphs: one purely data-driven; two refined with process knowledge via structural constraints; apply multi-graph attention network to fuse representations for anomaly detection; evaluate on real-world datasets.

Result: Incorporating process knowledge substantially improves anomaly detection performance on two real-world datasets.

Conclusion: Knowledge-guided multi-graph GNNs are effective for MTAD in industrial settings and can be extended with richer constraints or additional graphs.

Abstract: Industrial processes often generate complex, interdependent time-series data from multiple sensors across multiple stages, forming complex dependencies among variables and process stages. Effective monitoring and timely anomaly detection of these time series through multivariate time series anomaly detection (MTAD) is crucial for preventing failures and ensuring the reliability of automated systems. Graph neural networks (GNNs) have advanced MTAD by leveraging data-driven graphs to model complex dependencies among variables, effectively capturing relational structures within multivariate time series to enhance anomaly detection performance. However, existing GNN-based approaches often overlook critical process knowledge, and even when this knowledge is considered, seamlessly incorporating it into existing models remains inherently challenging, leading to suboptimal performance. To address this limitation, we propose a knowledge-assisted multi-graph framework for modeling sensor dependencies in multi-stage industrial processes for MTAD, which explicitly incorporates process knowledge into graph learning to enhance dependency modeling and improve anomaly detection performance. Our method constructs three complementary graphs: one purely data-driven and two refined by integrating structural constraints derived from process knowledge. To effectively leverage these graphs for anomaly detection, we employ a multi-graph attention network, enabling a more accurate and robust representation of complex dependencies. Comprehensive experiments on two real-world, multi-stage industrial datasets demonstrate that incorporating process knowledge substantially enhances anomaly detection performance.

</details>


### [120] [QUADS: Stabilizing NVFP4 Reinforcement Learning for MoE via QUantization-error Alignment across Dual Sides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15810)
*Zhengyang Zhuge,Hao Yu,Xin Wang,Zheng Li,Yizhong Cao,Dayiheng Liu,Jianwei Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QUADS stabilizes NVFP4 RL rollout for MoE LLMs, achieving BF16‑level accuracy and higher throughput (≈16% over FP8) by mitigating activation quantization errors with asymmetric quantization-aware training and residual activation compensation on rollout.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rollout generation is a major bottleneck in RL for Mixture-of-Experts LLMs. Direct NVFP4 application suffers from activation-induced instability, not weight errors.

Method: On trainer side: apply Asymmetric Quantization-Aware Training by fake-quantizing weights while keeping activations in higher precision. On rollout side: use Residual Activation Compensation to correct high-error activation channels, leveraging native W4A4 FP4 GEMMs.

Result: QUADS yields BF16-level accuracy on MoE RL benchmarks, improves average pass@1 by 21.49 points over naive NVFP4 RL, and achieves ~16% higher rollout throughput than FP8.

Conclusion: QUADS stabilizes NVFP4 RL for MoE with substantial accuracy and throughput gains, enabling faster and reliable rollout generation.

Abstract: Rollout generation is a major bottleneck in Reinforcement Learning (RL) for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) Large Language Models, motivating low-precision rollout acceleration such as FP8. As an emerging low-precision format, NVFP4 combines fine-grained scaling for accuracy preservation with native W4A4 FP4 GEMMs for higher throughput than FP8. However, we find that directly applying NVFP4 to MoE RL rollout is impractical. NVFP4 rollout with BF16 training collapses after roughly 150 steps, accompanied by rapidly growing rollout-trainer log-probability gaps. Through training-inference error analysis and controlled ablations, we identify activation error, rather than weight error, as the dominant source of FP4 RL instability: weights can be synchronized and aligned by a shared quantization-dequantization path, whereas activations are recomputed online and error is amplified by the coarse E2M1 grid. Therefore, to stabilize NVFP4 RL for MoE, we propose QUantization-error Alignment across Dual Sides (QUADS). On the trainer side, we introduce Asymmetric Quantization-Aware Training fake-quantizing weights while keeping activations unquantized for better alignment. On the rollout side, Residual Activation Compensation corrects high-error activation channels while preserving native W4A4 GEMMs. In our MoE RL experiments on several benchmarks, QUADS achieves BF16-level accuracy, improves average pass@1 by 21.49 points over naive NVFP4 RL, and delivers ~16% higher rollout throughput than FP8.

</details>


### [121] [Graph Coloring Approach to Solving Sudoku with Oscillatory Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15814)
*Filip Sabo,Aida Todri-Sanial*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An ONN-based Sudoku solver built on a Graph Coloring reformulation, incorporating a cheaper graph-coloring solver with an extra term to enforce Sudoku constraints. It outperforms prior HNN- and ONN-based solvers, achieving near-perfect accuracy on 4x4 and high accuracy on 9x9 Sudoku across varying degrees of missing data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate the viability of oscillatory neural networks for constrained combinatorial optimization, using Sudoku as a canonical CSP and Graph Coloring as an encoding that leverages physics-inspired dynamics.

Method: Rewrite Sudoku as a Graph Coloring problem, adapt an existing graph-coloring solver to be computationally cheaper, and add an additional term enforcing Sudoku-specific constraints. Implement the solver within an ONN framework and benchmark against HNN and prior ONN solvers across Sudoku sizes and numbers of unknown digits.

Result: Significant accuracy gains over existing HNN- and ONN-based solvers, with nearly flawless performance on 4x4 Sudoku and high accuracies on 9x9 Sudoku for various levels of missing information.

Conclusion: The proposed ONN-based Sudoku solver validates the effectiveness of physics-inspired optimization for CSPs, showing clear advantages over previous approaches and indicating potential for scalability to larger instances and related constraint satisfaction problems.

Abstract: Oscillatory Neural Networks (ONNs) present an attractive physics-based computing paradigm rooted in the dynamics of a network of typically fully coupled oscillators aiming to minimize an underlying energy function. In this paper, we propose an ONN-based solver for one well-known constrained combinatorial optimization problem, namely a Sudoku, by formulating the problem as a Graph Coloring problem. By modifying the already existing Graph Coloring solver to a computationally cheaper version and introducing an additional term ensuring the fulfillment of the Sudoku constraints, our solver is shown to significantly outperform the existing HNN- and ONN solvers in terms of accuracy. In particular, we are able to achieve nearly flawless accuracies on $4 \times 4$ as well as rather high accuracies on $9 \times 9$ Sudoku puzzles for different numbers of unknown digits.

</details>


### [122] [In-context learning of closed form solution to simple linear regression task using transformer with linear self-attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15819)
*Katsuyuki Hagiwara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A transformer with linear self-attention can in-context learn the least squares estimator in a simple regression task, with the closed-form solution approximated by layer normalization rather than gradient-descent-like iterations; an experimental example shows this behavior in a setting with l1 regularization where the target is the LS estimate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the in-context solver observed in transformers corresponds to an actual learned algorithm and whether a closed-form estimator like least-squares can be realized via in-context learning instead of iterative gradient-based optimization.

Method: Construct a transformer using linear self-attention and analyze its ability to in-context learn the least-squares estimator in a simple regression task; examine whether layer normalization yields the closed-form solution; compare against gradient-descent-based solvers; include an experimental example where the model is trained with l1 regularization and the target is the LS estimate.

Result: The least-squares solution is approximately obtained via layer normalization rather than gradient-descent-like iterations; the transformer demonstrates in-context learning of the LS estimator in the described setup.

Conclusion: In-context learning can realize closed-form solvers such as the least-squares estimator through components like layer normalization within linear-attention transformers; regularization can influence the emergent solver behavior, suggesting that in-context solutions may mirror analytical solvers in addition to iterative gradient-based methods.

Abstract: In-context learning is a remarkable property of transformers and has recently received a lot of interest. In many studies of in-context learning, it has been shown that transformers are capable of implementing solver for linear and non-linear regression problems, in which the most of them implement gradient descent algorithm. However, it is still unclear whether those implementations have actually been acquired through training. In this paper, we construct a transformer with linear self-attention, which in-context learns the least squares estimate in a simple regression task. The point here is that the closed form (analytical) solution is approximately obtained by using layer normalization rather than an approximate solution based on gradient descent algorithm. Then, we show an experimental example, in which our implementation is mainly used in the transformer trained with l1 regularization when the target output is the least squares estimate.

</details>


### [123] [Data-Native Global Optimization for Big Data K-means Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15835)
*Ravil Mussabayev,Rustam Mussabayev,Zukhra Yerdaliyeva,Kuldeyev Nursultan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Big-means++ offers a scalable, data-native global search for MSSC on arbitrarily tall data by using sample-induced surrogate landscapes, a flowing-incumbent strategy, a geometric shaking mechanism, and multi-agent asynchronous exploration, achieving robust, high-quality clustering with competitive efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MSSC (K-means) is NP-hard and prone to poor local minima; large-scale or infinite-like data (arbitrarily tall data) requires techniques that exploit finite samples while maintaining global-search capability and robustness.

Method: Big-means++ constructs a sequence of sample-induced MSSC approximations, each with perturbed local optima. It uses a flowing-incumbent—the centroid state carries across samples with K-means refinements, without rollback. A shaking mechanism varies sample size geometrically to explore multiple resolution scales. A competitive multi-agent system asynchronously explores independent sampled landscapes, with automatic convergence detection to stop agents upon reaching high-quality solutions.

Result: Empirical evaluation on 22 datasets against 11 competing algorithms shows that Big-means++ is effective, efficient, and robust for big-data clustering, outperforming or matching strong baselines across diverse datasets.

Conclusion: The proposed data-native, multi-agent surrogate-landscape search substantially improves global search quality and scalability for MSSC on big data, offering a practical, universal speed-quality control and strong robustness across datasets.

Abstract: Big data clustering remains challenging: the Minimum Sum-of-Squares Clustering (MSSC) problem underlying K-means is NP-hard, and existing methods either reach poor local minima or require prohibitive metaheuristic hybrids. We target arbitrarily tall data: a fixed feature space may contain arbitrarily many, possibly infinitely many, observations, while the algorithm accesses only finite random samples. We propose Big-means++, an algorithm achieving scalability and global-search quality by curating inputs to MSSC optimization on big data. It orchestrates local K-means refinements into a data-native global search for big data clustering. Rather than optimizing the full-data MSSC objective, Big-means++ traverses sample-induced surrogate landscapes. Each sample defines a distinct empirical MSSC approximation with a perturbed local-optimum structure, turning sample-to-sample variation into a global-search mechanism. Unlike Big-means, a flowing-incumbent strategy propagates centroid state across empirical landscapes through K-means refinements on fresh samples without rollback to a best-so-far solution. This increases mobility and favors stable, high-quality configurations across approximations of the full-data structure. A new shaking mechanism varies sample size geometrically, broadening the surrogate landscapes explored across resolution scales, accounting for cluster imbalance, and improving solution quality. A competitive multi-agent system asynchronously explores independent sampled landscapes, transforming diverse stochastic trajectories into collective search intelligence. Automatic convergence detection stops each agent after attaining a high-quality solution but before further search risks degrading it, while providing a universal speed-quality control. Experiments on 22 datasets against 11 competing algorithms demonstrate the effectiveness, efficiency, and robustness of Big-means++.

</details>


### [124] [ContinuityBench: A Benchmark and Systems Study of Stateful Failover in Multi-Provider LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15899)
*Vishal Pandey,Gopal Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a stateful, multi-provider proxy for LLM failover that preserves conversation context; defines Continuity Preservation Rate (CPR) and Continuity Latency Overhead (CLO); releases continuity-bench; empirically achieves 99.20% CPR over 750 failover events, outperforming stateless approaches; recommends asynchronous backoff to avoid retry storms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap where high API availability does not guarantee conversational continuity. Stateless failover discards conversation history during provider outages or rate limits, disrupting user experience. The work defines metrics and a mechanism to maintain conversation state across heterogeneous LLM endpoints.

Method: Proposes a stateful, multi-provider proxy architecture with a History-Forwarding strategy to reconstruct conversational state across LLM endpoints during failover. Releases continuity-bench as an open evaluation harness to stress-test context preservation under high-concurrency failure conditions.
