Conducts empirical evaluation with N=750 failover events, analyzes CPR and latency distributions, and advocates asynchronous exponential backoff with jitter to prevent retry storms.

Result: The stateful proxy achieves a CPR of 99.20% with 95% CI [98.27%, 99.63%], significantly outperforming a near-0% preservation rate for stateless architectures. Continuity latency characteristics are characterized, informing failover latency management.

Conclusion: Provides a principled foundation for building robust, state-preserving multi-model inference systems. The work delivers actionable metrics, an open evaluation tool, and architectural guidance for practitioners deploying multi-provider LLM inference.

Abstract: In production large language model (LLM) deployments, high API availability guarantees do not equate to conversational continuity. When a primary provider experiences an outage or strict rate-limiting, naive stateless failover mechanisms successfully maintain uptime but silently discard conversation history, severely disrupting the user experience. To rigorously quantify and resolve this failure mode, we introduce two novel metrics: Continuity Preservation Rate (CPR) and Continuity Latency Overhead (CLO). We propose a stateful, multi-provider proxy architecture utilizing a History-Forwarding strategy to seamlessly reconstruct conversational state across heterogeneous LLM endpoints during failover events. Furthermore, we release continuity-bench, https://github.com/Vishal-sys-code/continuity-bench, an open evaluation harness designed to stress-test context preservation under high-concurrency provider failure conditions. Our empirical evaluation ($N=750$ failover events) demonstrates that our stateful proxy achieves a 99.20\% CPR [95\% CI: 98.27\%, 99.63\%], cleanly transferring deep conversational context to fallback providers, compared to a near-0\% preservation rate for standard stateless architectures. Finally, we characterize failover latency distributions, identifying the critical necessity of asynchronous exponential backoff with jitter to prevent cascading retry storms against strict-limit fallback APIs. Our results provide a principled foundation for building robust, state-preserving multi-model inference systems.

</details>


### [125] [A Semiparametric Framework for Stochastic Fundamental Diagram Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15907)
*Pengnan Chi,Xiaoliang Ma,Magnus Jansson,Magnus Nordenvaad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A semiparametric SFD framework aligns the conditional flow distribution with physical moments via moment-matching, ensuring a unique solution for location-scale families and extendable to non-location-scale forms; empirically outperforms baselines with better probabilistic accuracy and uncertainty quantification in congested regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing stochastic traffic models struggle to enforce physical constraints while remaining flexible to capture nonlinear patterns; a framework that guarantees moment-consistency and well-posedness is needed for robust uncertainty-aware traffic modeling.

Method: Introduce specialized functional forms that inherently satisfy moment constraints on the conditional flow distribution given density, integrate neural-network-based components to model nonlinear empirical patterns, derive moment-matching equations to parameterize the distribution, prove a unique solution for the location-scale family, and extend to non-location-scale distributions with additional boundary constraints.

Result: Empirical evaluation on real-world data shows the proposed method consistently outperforms representative baselines, delivering superior probabilistic accuracy and robust uncertainty quantification, especially in congested regimes.

Conclusion: The framework provides a theoretically grounded and flexible foundation for stochastic traffic flow modeling, balancing physical fidelity with empirical flexibility.

Abstract: The stochastic fundamental diagram (SFD) provides a probabilistic description of the relationship between traffic density and flow or speed, enabling uncertainty-aware traffic modeling. However, existing stochastic models frequently struggle to accommodate rigorous physical constraints while retaining sufficient flexibility to capture complex nonlinear patterns. To address this, we propose a novel semiparametric SFD modeling framework by leveraging specially designed functional forms. These functions intrinsically satisfy physical constraints defined on the moments of the conditional flow distribution given traffic density while incorporating neural-network-based structures to capture complex empirical patterns. We derive a system of moment-matching equations to convert physical constraints into the parameterization of the conditional distribution, proving that a unique solution exists for the location-scale family of distributions, thereby guaranteeing model well-posedness. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the framework can be extended to non-location-scale distributions, including those requiring additional boundary constraints. Empirical evaluations on a real-world dataset reveal that our approach consistently outperforms representative baselines, delivering superior probabilistic accuracy and robust uncertainty quantification, particularly in congested regimes. Overall, the proposed framework provides a theoretically grounded and flexible foundation for stochastic traffic flow modeling.

</details>


### [126] [(MPO)$^2$: Multivariate Polynomial Optimization based on Matrix Product Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15916)
*Niccolò Ciolli,Anders Vestergaard Nørskov,Michael Kastoryano,Petr Taborsky,Morten Mørup*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces MPO^2, a Matrix Product Operator-based framework for multivariate polynomial learning that yields order-independent, compact representations by embedding features with learned MPOs and structured weight tensors, outperforming CP and TT-based tensorized polynomial models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Polynomial models grow exponentially with degree due to tensor coefficients; existing tensor decompositions (CP, TT) have expressivity limits or order-dependence, limiting scalability and flexibility.

Method: Combine learned Matrix Product Operator (MPO) feature embeddings with compact polynomial weight tensors; allow structured operators (projections, convolutions, masks) to enforce weight tensor symmetries and enable order-independent polynomial representations; evaluate on regression and classification benchmarks.

Result: MPO^2 demonstrates improved performance over existing tensor-decomposition-based polynomial models on regression and classification tasks, offering a flexible and efficient alternative for polynomial function approximation.

Conclusion: MPO^2 provides order-independent, structured polynomial representations with compact weights, enabling more expressive and scalable polynomial models and broad applicability across ML and signal processing tasks.

Abstract: Central to machine learning and signal processing is the ability to perform universal function approximation and learn complex input-output relationships from limited numbers of observations. Multivariate polynomial models offer a natural way to express such relationships through multiplicative feature interactions, but their coefficient tensors grow exponentially in size with the polynomial degree. Existing tensorized polynomial models reduce this cost, yet canonical polyadic decompositions have rank-limited expressivity, and tensor train formulations are feature order dependent. We introduce Multivariate Polynomial Optimization based on Matrix Product Operators (MPO)$^2$, a framework that combines learned MPO feature embeddings with compact polynomial weight tensors. This yields feature order independent polynomial representations that can incorporate structured operators such as projections, convolutions, and masks for weight tensor symmetries. Across regression and classification benchmarks, (MPO)$^2$ improves over existing tensor decomposition based polynomial models and provides a flexible alternative for efficient polynomial function approximation.

</details>


### [127] [On the Failure of Boundary-Seeking Distillation in Bottlenecked Generative Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15919)
*Mohamed Amine Kina*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Boundary-seeking data-free distillation (CAKE) does not naturally extend to autoencoders; gradient conflicts arise due to decoders acting as coupled feature classifiers under a bottleneck; manifold-aware synthesis offers a better baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether boundary-seeking sample generation for data-free distillation on classifiers extends to autoencoder distillation, using MNIST and a reformulated per-feature classification view.

Method: Reformulate continuous reconstruction as a dense per-feature classification task with the decoder producing categorical logits; analyze a single-instance objective versus a decoder-wide set of tightly coupled feature-level classifiers constrained by a shared latent bottleneck; compare independent sampling of contrastive targets against manifold-aware synthesis; empirical evaluation on MNIST.

Result: Boundary-seeking objectives are ill-posed for bottlenecked generative architectures; independently sampling contrastive targets for coupled outputs induces gradient conflicts and misleads optimization; manifold-aware synthesis avoids these issues and provides a strong baseline for data-free generative distillation.

Conclusion: Boundary-seeking principles do not directly transfer to autoencoder distillation; respecting latent-manifold geometry via manifold-aware synthesis is essential for effective data-free distillation in generative settings.

Abstract: Data-free knowledge distillation transfers the knowledge encoded in a teacher model to a student model without access to the original training data. Prior work such as Contrastive Abductive Knowledge Extraction (CAKE) achieves this for classifiers by synthesizing samples near the teacher's decision boundary. In this work, we investigate whether this boundary-seeking principle extends to autoencoder distillation through experiments on the MNIST dataset . To enable a direct comparison, we reformulate continuous reconstruction as a dense, per-feature classification task, allowing the decoder to output categorical logits. We show that boundary-seeking objectives are fundamentally ill-posed in bottlenecked generative architectures. CAKE operates on a single, instance-level objective, but a decoder acts as an array of tightly coupled, feature-level classifiers constrained by a shared low-dimensional bottleneck. Independently sampling contrastive targets for these coupled outputs violates the geometry of the learned latent manifold and produces severe gradient conflicts instead of informative boundary samples. Manifold-aware synthesis bypasses these conflicts entirely and establishes an effective baseline for data-free generative distillation.

</details>


### [128] [Knowledge-Guided Cross-Modal Fusion for Adult-to-Pediatric ECG Transfer via Label-Conditioned Contrastive Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15928)
*Xinran Liu,Yuwen Li,Hongxiang Gao,Heyang Xu,Jianqing Li,Zongmin Wang,Chengyu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PEACE is a knowledge-guided cross-modal framework to improve pediatric ECG transfer from adult-trained models. It uses axis-based, diagnosis-informed tokens and a label-query cross-attention mechanism with a label-set aware contrastive objective and curriculum adaptive fusion. Training leverages adult ECG data; inference uses ECG alone. It shows strong zero-shot and few-shot gains on ZZU-pECG and high performance after fine-tuning on PTB-XL, with ablations highlighting the importance of label-conditioned knowledge alignment over global text fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electronically recorded ECGs show age-dependent differences; models pretrained on adult data poorly transfer to pediatric settings when pediatric labels are scarce. Existing multimodal ECG–text methods mix signals and diagnoses at a coarse level, hampering pediatric transfer. A knowledge-guided, axis-aware alignment can inject pediatric-relevant information without contaminating inference.

Method: - Diagnosis is decomposed into rhythm, morphology, and ST–T axes. For each recording, only positive-label descriptors are composed into three axis tokens plus a fused embedding. - Label Query Network (LQN) uses diagnostic labels to cross-attend over ECG tokens and axis tokens. - Label Set Aware Bidirectional Contrastive Learning (LSBC) aligns pooled ECG features with the fused embedding when recordings share diagnoses. - Curriculum Adaptive Fusion (CAF) adjusts alignment strength based on smoothed classification loss and training progress. - The knowledge branch guides training supervision; at inference, only ECG signals are used.

Result: On ZZU-pECG: macro AUCs of 59.39% (zero-shot), 81.74% (50-shot), 91.56% (full fine-tuning). Gains are most pronounced under limited supervision compared to baselines; zero-shot notably improves over domain-adaptation baselines, and 50-shot AUC is comparable to DANN. After fine-tuning on PTB-XL: macro AUC 96.90% over nine harmonized labels. Ablations indicate the key role of label-conditioned knowledge alignment rather than global text fusion in driving pediatric transfer gains.

Conclusion: Knowledge-guided, axis-aware cross-modal alignment enables effective pediatric transfer from adult ECG data, especially under limited supervision. The LQN, LSBC, and CAF components together yield robust zero-shot and few-shot improvements, with strong finetuning performance after PTB-XL exposure. The axis-token design and diagnosis-focused supervision are crucial to the gains.

Abstract: Adult and pediatric electrocardiogram (ECG) interpretation relies on age-sensitive criteria, and models pretrained mainly on adult ECGs often transfer poorly to pediatric populations when pediatric labels are scarce. Existing multimodal ECG--text methods typically align waveforms and text at the global sample level, entangling evidence from co-occurring diagnoses and limiting transfer under this gap. We propose Pediatric-Adult ECG Alignment via Cross-modal Enhancement (PEACE), a knowledge-guided framework pretrained on the largely adult MIMIC-IV ECG corpus. PEACE describes each diagnosis along rhythm, morphology, and ST--T axes and, per recording, composes only positive-label descriptors into three axis tokens and a fused embedding. A label query network (LQN) uses diagnostic labels as queries to cross-attend over ECG tokens and axis tokens, while label set aware bidirectional contrastive learning (LSBC) aligns pooled ECG features with the fused embedding when recordings share diagnoses. Curriculum adaptive fusion (CAF) gates alignment strength according to smoothed classification loss and training progress, limiting disruption during early optimization. The knowledge branch is used only for training supervision; inference uses ECG signals alone. On ZZU-pECG, PEACE reaches macro average AUCs of 59.39%, 81.74%, and 91.56% under zero-shot, 50-shot, and full fine-tuning, with the clearest gains over foundation and knowledge-pretraining baselines under limited supervision; versus domain adaptation initializations, zero-shot improves substantially while 50-shot AUC is comparable to DANN. After fine-tuning on PTB-XL, PEACE reaches 96.90% macro average AUC over nine harmonized labels. Ablations confirm that label-conditioned knowledge alignment, rather than global text fusion, is the key driver of pediatric transfer gains.

</details>


### [129] [An Exploratory Study of Single Channel Surface Electromyography for Hand Gesture Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15972)
*Daanish Hindustani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Single-channel sEMG with a feature-based representation and a compact neural network can achieve up to 90% accuracy for ten gestures, enabling low-power, single-channel gesture recognition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce practical constraints of multi-channel sEMG and heavy models by exploring a compact, single-channel pipeline suitable for embedded systems.

Method: Collect raw sEMG from a single channel; extract a feature set spanning time-domain, frequency-domain, higher-order-crossing, and relative-intensity features; reduce feature redundancy via Pearson correlation filtering and removal of highly correlated features; apply selective dimensionality reduction (LDA and PCA); evaluate three classifiers (feed-forward NN, KNN, SVM) across four experimental conditions.

Result: Combining time and frequency features with Pearson filtering and a compact NN yields up to 90% accuracy, despite limited temporal and spatial information.

Conclusion: Single-channel sEMG with feature-based representations and lightweight classifiers shows promise for cost-effective, low-power gesture recognition, though details on dataset, generalizability, and practical deployment require further validation.

Abstract: Accurate hand gesture recognition using surface electromyography (sEMG) typically relies on multichannel sensor arrays and computationally intensive models. This limits practical deployment in low-power and embedded systems. This study investigates the feasibility of classifying ten hand gestures using a single sEMG channel combined with lightweight machine learning architectures. Raw sEMG signals were transformed into a comprehensive feature-based representation, including time-domain, frequency-domain, higher-order-crossing, and relative-intensity features. Feature redundancy was reduced using Pearson correlation filtering and the removal of highly correlated features, while dimensionality-reduction techniques (LDA and PCA) were applied selectively. Three classifiers, a feed-forward neural network (NN), k-nearest neighbors (KNN), and a support vector machine (SVM), were systematically evaluated across four experiments. Results demonstrate that combining time and frequency features with Pearson filtering and a compact NN can achieve up to 90 percent accuracy, even with limited temporal and spatial information. These findings highlight the potential of single-channel sEMG systems for cost-effective, low-power gesture-recognition applications.

</details>


### [130] [DebrisTracer: Reliable Tracking in Hypervelocity Impact Fast Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15986)
*Théophane Loloum,Fabien Vivodtzev,David Hébert,Baptiste Reynier,Michel Arrigoni,Julien Tierny*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DebrisTracer extends a topology-based debris tracking pipeline with domain knowledge to reliably track debris in noisy hypervelocity impact imaging, enabling accurate mass/speed distributions and interpretable analysis; validated against crater depth and ejected mass, with open-source C++ implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurately tracking numerous debris fragments from hypervelocity impacts is challenging due to noisy, fragment-rich data; reliable physical quantities (mass, speed) are crucial for aerospace safety and material response studies.

Method: Builds on an off-the-shelf topology tracking framework using critical point extraction and matching, augmented with domain knowledge and physical assumptions; produces automatic, interpretable debris tracking and statistical summaries; validated against physical experiments; provides open-source C++ code and dataset.

Result: Improved accuracy over established tools in predicting ejected mass and crater depth; identifies distinct regimes within debris populations via statistical summaries; supports multiple impact angles; extensive experiments corroborate physical validation.

Conclusion: DebrisTracer enables reliable, interpretable debris tracking in hypervelocity impact imaging, facilitating physical analysis and decision-making in aerospace contexts; released as open-source for broad use.

Abstract: This application paper presents DebrisTracer, a framework for the reliable tracking of debris in hypervelocity impact fast imaging. These noisy and highly specific datasets capture the ejection of a large number of debris fragments after the impact of a projectile launched at hypervelocity into a target material. The reliable estimation of debris mass and speed distributions is of major importance in aerospace applications. We document how to extend an off-the-shelf topology tracking framework based on critical point extraction and matching, in order to incorporate domain knowledge and physical assumptions. Our approach automatically produces an accurate and reliable debris tracking, enabling an interpretable visual analysis of this complex space-time phenomenon. Extensive experiments demonstrate the accuracy improvements provided by our approach over established tools used by domain experts in terms of physical validation, specifically via the prediction of the experimental ejected mass and crater depth profiles. We illustrate the utility of our approach across several use cases (with varying impact angles and physics). We show that our statistical summaries enable the visual identification of distinct regimes within the debris population, corroborating and refining prior expectations of domain experts. Our database and our C++ implementation are available at this address: https://github.com/tloloum/DebrisTracer.

</details>


### [131] [Presentation, Not Mechanism: A Render Confound in Deprecation-Aware Memory Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16019)
*Zhaoyang Jiang,Zhizhong Fu,Zicheng Li,Yunsoo Kim,Jiacong Mi,Xuanqi Peng,Fei Teng,Honghan Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Presentation confounds drive most observed gains in Evidence-State Revision; after render-control, coarse invalidation is the meaningful mechanism for current-state queries; fine-grained RevisionLedger provides negligible residual benefit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disentangle mechanism from presentation in memory revision systems and identify which retention strategy best supports current-state and provenance queries across real-world data streams.

Method: Render-matched control; compare flat retrieval, coarse edge invalidation, and fine-grained RevisionLedger on 2,907 high-agreement questions from GitHub, multi-repo issue histories, Wikipedia, and DyKnow-style temporal streams; assess current-state vs provenance performance.

Result: Most gains attributed to easier presentation; mechanism residual is indistinguishable from zero; after controlling for rendering, coarse invalidation outperforms fine-grained RevisionLedger for current-state queries by about 0.084; provenance remains adequately supported by retained invalidated evidence; richer typing adds little value.

Conclusion: Memory evaluations should fix the render; deprecation-aware systems should deploy the coarsest retained state that covers their queries; avoid evaluating mechanism in isolation without controlling presentation.

Abstract: AI systems increasingly retrieve from records that revise themselves: issue threads, encyclopedic histories, policy logs, and long conversations. The challenge is not only finding relevant evidence, but deciding which claims remain in force, which were superseded, and when to abstain. Structured memories promise to solve this with typed edges, temporal updates, and conflict status, yet evaluations often change mechanism and prompt presentation together. We study this as Evidence-State Revision, comparing flat retrieval, coarse edge invalidation, and fine-grained RevisionLedger on 2,907 high-agreement questions from GitHub, multi-repo issue histories, Wikipedia, and DyKnow-style temporal streams. A render-matched control (same layout, deprecation disabled) reveals the central confound: when a value is changed and later restored, RevisionLedger appears to beat a flat baseline by +0.182, but almost all the gain comes from easier presentation; the fine-grained mechanism residual is indistinguishable from zero (+0.021 to +0.025 across two judge families). After presentation is controlled, coarse invalidation is the only mechanism that pays for current-state queries, beating the fine ledger by 0.084; the same query-sufficiency principle says provenance mainly needs retained invalidated evidence, not richer typing. Memory evaluations should hold render fixed, and deprecation-aware systems should deploy the coarsest retained state that covers their queries.

</details>


### [132] [Constrained Hebbian Learning Supports Efficient Representational Allocation under Structural Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16027)
*Patrick Inoue,Florian Röhrbein,Andreas Knoblauch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hebbian learning lowers task-information cost under sparsity constraints, trading some accuracy for more efficient representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test whether an excitatory competitive Hebbian rule can allocate synaptic resources under anatomical/metabolic constraints and yield favorable cost-performance representations in audiovisual tasks.

Method: Quantify representational cost with Variational Information Bottleneck–derived mutual information; evaluate fixed audiovisual embeddings from AVE, Kinetics-Sounds, VGGSound100; compare Hebbian learning to DDTP and backpropagation under matched sparsity and architectural constraints.

Result: Hebbian learning achieves lower task-information cost (CTI) than sparse BP and DDTP in main comparisons; CTI comparable to shallow BP with nonnegative weights; it shifts the trade-off to lower CTI at similar performance, with occasional accuracy reductions.

Conclusion: Hebbian learning acts as a mechanism for synaptic resource allocation under biological constraints, yielding cost-efficient representations rather than universal accuracy gains.

Abstract: Introduction: Biological systems face anatomical and metabolic constraints, including costly synaptic maintenance and limited connectivity. These constraints favor neural codes that compress behaviorally relevant information into low-redundancy patterns. We test whether an excitatory competitive Hebbian rule can support synaptic resource allocation under such constraints and whether the resulting representations occupy a more favorable cost-performance regime than reference learning rules.
  Methods: Representational cost is quantified using mutual-information-based measures derived from the Variational Information Bottleneck. Experiments use fixed audiovisual embeddings from three audiovisual benchmarks (AVE, Kinetics-Sounds, VGGSound100) to isolate downstream associative plasticity. Hebbian learning is compared with Dense Difference Target Propagation (DDTP) and backpropagation (BP) under matched sparsity and architectural constraints.
  Results: Hebbian learning achieves lower task-information cost (CTI) than sparse BP and DDTP in the main compressed comparisons, while reaching CTI values comparable to shallow BP with nonnegative weights. Rather than uniformly improving classification performance, Hebbian learning shifts the trade-off between task-relevant information and representational cost, yielding lower CTI at comparable functional performance in several settings.
  Discussion: The results indicate a cost-performance trade-off rather than uniform accuracy gains. For a given level of task-relevant information, Hebbian representations retain less input information while preserving functional performance, although accuracy is slightly reduced on some datasets. These findings support interpreting Hebbian learning as a mechanism for synaptic resource allocation rather than as a general strategy for maximizing audiovisual classification accuracy.

</details>


### [133] [CLaC@FinMMEval 2026 Task 3: Sentiment-Augmented Deep Reinforcement Learning for Active Trading -- An Alpha-Reward Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16028)
*Andrei Neagu,Eeham Khan,Leila Kosseim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DRL-based trading system for BTC and TSLA tested on CLEF 2026 FinMMEval Task 3; compares PG, PPO, DQL, DDPG; uses tech indicators, calendar encodings, LLaMA sentiment; alpha reward; hyperparameter tuning; DDPG strongest on test, DQL highest validation Sharpe, notable generalization gap bull vs bear.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate the efficacy of multiple deep reinforcement learning algorithms for daily decision-making in crypto and equities, under a standardized benchmark (CLEF Task 3), and to assess transferability and generalization across market regimes.

Method: Formulate the problem as a discrete-action MDP. Features include technical indicators, cyclical calendar encodings, daily news sentiment (LLaMA 3.2 1B). Introduce an alpha reward based on excess market return and randomize episode starts. Hyperparameters tuned with Ray Tune across 180 trials per algorithm–asset pair; early stopping; model selection based on validation Sharpe ratio.

Result: DDPG achieves the strongest performance on the CLEF Task 3 test set; DQL chosen a priori for live deployment due to highest validation Sharpe ratio (without test access). TSLA: DDPG 54.96% and DQL 52.62% cumulative returns vs 16.45% buy-and-hold. BTC: DDPG 1.58% vs -34.27% buy-and-hold. A substantial validation-to-test generalization gap observed, indicating difficulty in transferring policies from bull to bear regimes.

Conclusion: DRL can outperform buy-and-hold in this setting, with DDPG showing robust test performance, but policy generalization across market regimes remains a key challenge; live-deployment choice favored DQL based on validation performance, highlighting risk of test-set leakage and regime mismatch.

Abstract: This paper presents our system for Task 3 of the CLEF 2026 FinMMEval Lab, which requires daily long, flat, or short trading decisions for Bitcoin (BTC) and Tesla (TSLA) using news and historical market data. We formulate the problem as a discrete-action Markov Decision Process and compare four deep reinforcement learning algorithms: Policy Gradient (PG), Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), Deep Q-Learning (DQL), and Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (DDPG). The agents use technical indicators, cyclical calendar encodings, and daily news sentiment scores produced by LLaMA 3.2 1B. To reduce overfitting and align training with the objective of outperforming buy-and-hold, we introduce an alpha reward based on excess market return and randomize episode start dates. Hyperparameters are optimized with Ray Tune over 180 trials per algorithm-asset pair, with early stopping and model selection based on validation Sharpe ratio. On the CLEF Task 3 test set, DDPG achieves the strongest overall performance. DQL was selected a priori for the live endpoint because it obtained the highest validation Sharpe ratio, with selection performed without access to the test period. For TSLA, DDPG and DQL achieve cumulative returns of 54.96% and 52.62%, respectively, compared with 16.45% for buy-and-hold. For BTC, DDPG achieves a positive return of 1.58% while buy-and-hold declines by -34.27%. The results also reveal a substantial validation-to-test generalization gap, highlighting the difficulty of transferring policies selected in bull-market conditions to a bear-market regime.

</details>


### [134] [Revisiting data-driven dynamic security assessment with a tabular foundation model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16031)
*Olayiwola Arowolo,Maosheng Yang,Jochen Cremer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A single tabular foundation model (TFM) uses in-context learning to perform data-driven pre-fault dynamic security assessment across multiple contingencies, eliminating retraining and reducing labeling needs; electrical distance coordinates (EDC) aid generalization to unseen contingencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address two key limits of existing data-driven DSA: (1) reliance on large labeled databases with a separate model per contingency, and (2) poor generalization to unseen contingencies; aim to deploy a single model across many contingencies with minimal labeled data.

Method: Employ a tabular foundation model (TFM) with in-context learning to assess stability for many contingencies simultaneously, using EDC as continuous features; analyze when EDC improves generalization; demonstrate with few labeled samples (e.g., ~120 per contingency) and no model retraining; evaluate on IEEE 68-bus system.

Result: TFM achieves an average Macro F1 of ~90% with ~120 labeled samples per contingency, about two orders of magnitude fewer samples than conventional approaches; for unseen contingencies, ~10 labeled samples with EDC encoding match the best transfer-learning oracle that requires fully labeled data.

Conclusion: Foundational models are promising for power-system operations, enabling cross-contingency assessment with minimal labeling and no retraining; EDC can enable generalization to unseen contingencies; broader application across operational tasks warrants further exploration.

Abstract: Data-driven pre-fault dynamic security assessment (DSA) rapidly evaluates the dynamic risk of credible contingencies on a power system using machine learning. Existing approaches face two limitations. First, they require a large labelled database for training, with a separate model trained, tuned, and maintained for each contingency in a potentially long list of credible contingencies. Second, the trained models generalize poorly to unseen contingencies. This work addresses the limitations by using a tabular foundation model (TFM) that assesses stability through in-context learning, requiring no retraining or hyperparameter optimization. A single TFM can assess many contingencies at once, removing the need for one model per classifier. We also characterize when the use of electrical distance coordinates (EDC) as continuous features enables generalization of TFM to unseen contingencies and when they do not, demonstrating how a few labelled samples can reliably improve generalization. Through comprehensive case studies on the IEEE 68-bus system, we show that a single TFM attains an average Macro F1 score of about 90% with only 120 labelled samples per contingency, roughly two orders of magnitude fewer than conventionally assumed, without any model retraining or hyperparameter tuning. For new/unseen contingencies, we show that using just 10 labelled samples of the new contingency with EDC encoding matches the best achievable transfer learning oracle model, which requires fully labelled data and is not deployable in practice. Overall, this initial study paves the way towards developing and deploying foundation models for power system operations, with possible applications across multiple operational tasks.

</details>


### [135] [DELUGE: Towards Continental-Scale Daily Pluvial Flood Damage Prediction via Interpretable Conditioning on Foundation Model Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16050)
*Yuya Kawakami,Daniel Cayan,Dongyu Liu,Kwan-Liu Ma,Tom Corringham*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DELUGE is a high-resolution national-scale predictive framework for daily pluvial flood damage (~1 km) using a multimodal DL model with interpretable hydrometeorology modules, outperforming tree-based baselines on top 100 most-claim cells.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pluvial floods drive 45% of NFIP claims; prediction is challenging due to coarse, regional or computationally heavy models. There is a need for national-scale, high-resolution, and interpretable models to support risk management.

Method: Train a multimodal deep learning model across hazard–exposure–vulnerability components; focus on the top 100 highest-claim 75 km cells (~81% of claims); introduce hydrometeorology branch with Value Modulator and Temporal Modulator conditioned on terrain descriptors and AlphaEarth embeddings; incorporate spatial block holdout; compare against tuned Random Forest, XGBoost, LightGBM; evaluate using PR-AUC emphasizing rare high-cost claims; claim interpretability and transferability of conditioning scheme.

Result: DELUGE outperforms baseline models by ~9–30% PR-AUC under spatial block holdout, focusing on high-cost, rare claims; top 100 cells capture ~81% of pluvial flood claims; provides directly inspectable hydrological parameters, enabling architecture-level interpretability; proposed conditioning scheme with foundation-model embeddings is transferable to other geospatial tasks.

Conclusion: Interpretable conditioning via Value/Temporal Modulators and foundation-model embeddings enables high-resolution national-scale pluvial flood damage prediction, with improved performance and broader applicability beyond DELUGE.

Abstract: Pluvial (rainfall-driven) flooding accounts for 45% of National Flood Insurance Program (NFIP) claims in the United States and is harder to predict than its riverine and coastal counterparts, with existing approaches limited to coarse resolution, regional domains, or computationally intensive process-based models unsuitable for daily continental-scale use. We present DELUGE, a multimodal deep learning framework for daily pluvial flood damage prediction at ~1 km resolution and national scale, trained on spatially and temporally corrected NFIP claims (2017-2022) and structured around the hazard, exposure, and vulnerability components of disaster risk. Rather than blanket coverage of the Conterminous United States (CONUS), we model the top 100 highest-claim 75 km cells, distributed nationwide and accounting for ~81% of total pluvial flood claims. Our architectural novelty is a pair of parametric modules in the hydrometeorology branch, a Value Modulator and a Temporal Modulator, conditioned on terrain descriptors and AlphaEarth foundation-model embeddings, that expose directly inspectable hydrological response parameters and provide architecture-level interpretability-by-design. Under a spatial block holdout, DELUGE outperforms tuned Random Forest, XGBoost, and LightGBM baselines by 9% to 30% on a dollar-weighted area under the precision-recall curve (PR-AUC), a metric that emphasizes the rare, high-cost claims of greatest operational interest. Beyond DELUGE, we argue this interpretable conditioning scheme is a transferable pattern for integrating foundation-model embeddings into other geospatial prediction tasks.

</details>


### [136] [When Model Merging Rivals Joint Multi-Task Reinforcement Learning: A Task-Vector Geometry Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16062)
*S. Aaron McClendon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Model merging of RL agents can achieve parity with jointly trained models; task vectors are near-orthogonal with a small shared direction, causing RAM/TIES-style merging to approximate uniform averaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether model merging can truly substitute joint multi-agent training in RL by comparing merged specialists against a jointly trained model on the same data, addressing a gap in the literature.

Method: Train Qwen3-8B specialists on AppWorld using LOOP; create merged models (TIES, RAM+) from independently trained specialists; compare to a jointly trained model on identical data; analyze task-vector geometry.

Result: Merging achieves task-goal completion on par with joint RL; all merge variants are statistically indistinguishable from joint training.

Conclusion: Task-vector geometry (near-orthogonality with a small shared direction) explains why merge methods converge to near-uniform averaging; direction and support are decoupled, making RAM/TIES effectively equivalent to averaging; authors release code and statistics.

Abstract: Model merging is promoted as a substitute for joint multi-task training, yet in the reinforcement-learning setting this substitution is essentially never tested against the baseline it claims to replace: methods merge independently released agents precisely because a joint model is unavailable. We build the missing comparison. Training difficulty-1 and difficulty-2 Qwen3-8B specialists on the AppWorld agent benchmark with LOOP, we merge them (TIES, RAM+) and pit the result against a jointly trained model on the same data. On task-goal completion, merging matches joint RL -- and every merge variant is statistically indistinguishable. To explain why merge method does not matter here, we measure the geometry of the specialists' task vectors, which carries no task-sampling noise: they are near-orthogonal (cosine 0.06 - 0.10) despite ~65% support overlap, a small, shared direction that grows over training and that we calibrate against a random-init floor and a same-run ceiling to confirm it reflects learning, not the low-rank parameterization. Because direction and support are decoupled, support and sign-based merging (RAM, TIES) collapse to near-uniform averaging. We release all code and statistics.

</details>


### [137] [Physics-Based Deep Spatiotemporal Hyperlocal Radar Nowcasting with a Multi-Variable U-Net for High-Resolution Precipitation Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16080)
*Akshay Sunil,Muhammed Rashid,Raja Sekhar Sivaraju,Sushma Nair,Subimal Ghosh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A compact radar-only nowcasting framework using a multi-elevation radar set and proxy kinematic features within an encoder–decoder U-Net to forecast 12 future 7.5-minute-reflectivity fields up to 90 minutes for Mumbai; includes attention on convective cores and physics-guided attribution; validated on 2023 Mumbai radar data with CSI up to 0.437 at 90 minutes; shows improvements over persistence and real-time operability on a standard PC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the latency and data-assimilation demands of conventional high-resolution numerical weather prediction for urban nowcasting. Leverage high-frequency radar observations to produce fast, locally-relevant forecasts, particularly in Mumbai where monsoon convection and land–sea interactions drive intense short-term rainfall.

Method: Encoder–decoder U-Net using radar-only inputs: multi-elevation reflectivity, Doppler radial velocity, and radial-velocity-gradient proxy features. Outputs 12 future composite reflectivity fields at 7.5-minute intervals (up to 90 minutes). Derived channels include velocity magnitude, divergence-like, directional-shear, and vorticity-like fields. Introduces a high-reflectivity attention module and physics-guided attribution. Trained on Mumbai Doppler radar data (May–Aug 2023) and evaluated on temporally independent events.

Result: CSI at 90 min: ≥10 dBZ 0.437, ≥20 dBZ 0.332, ≥30 dBZ 0.193. Outperforms persistence in RMSE and spatial correlation at longer lead times. Real-time capable on a standard computer; nowcasts produced within seconds.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates effective radar-only nowcasting with interpretable kinematic proxies and attention mechanisms, achieving useful predictive skill at up to 90 minutes in an urban setting. It offers a computationally efficient, deployable solution for real-time flood management, with potential for broader regional generalization yet limited validation beyond Mumbai.

Abstract: Precipitation nowcasting over the immediate 10-90 min period is important for flood management and real-time decision-making in urban regions. Conventional short-range forecasting with high-resolution numerical weather prediction requires frequent data assimilation, model initialization, and spin-up, introducing computational latency. Machine learning provides an alternative by learning storm evolution directly from high-frequency observations and producing forecasts quickly after training. This is particularly relevant for Mumbai, India, where monsoon convection, land-sea interactions, and localized intense rainfall make short-term prediction difficult. Here, we develop a compact radar-only nowcasting framework that combines multi-elevation reflectivity, Doppler radial velocity, and radial-velocity-gradient proxy features within an encoder-decoder U-Net. Using the most recent radar volume scan, the model predicts 12 future composite reflectivity fields at 7.5-min intervals up to 90 min lead time. The derived velocity magnitude, divergence-like, directional-shear, and vorticity-like channels represent kinematic signatures associated with convergence and boundary interactions without requiring full wind-field retrieval. A high-reflectivity attention module improves sensitivity to convective cores, and physics-guided attribution examines whether the learned sensitivities are meteorologically meaningful. The model is trained using Mumbai Doppler radar observations from May to August 2023 and evaluated on temporally independent events. At 90 min lead time, Critical Success Index values are 0.437, 0.332, and 0.193 for $\geq$10, $\geq$20, and $\geq$30 dBZ thresholds, respectively. Compared with persistence, the model gives lower RMSE and higher spatial correlation at longer lead times. Once trained, it runs on a standard computer, generating nowcasts within seconds for real-time use.

</details>


### [138] [Neural spectroscopy of AlphaFold2 reveals encoded protein conformational landscapes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16087)
*Kaustav Mehta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Treats AlphaFold2's weights as a scientific object and shows that reading them with neural spectroscopy reveals emergent conformational landscapes; Gaussian smoothing (Scaled Gaussian Convolution) applied to Evoformer weights yields protein-specific perturbation landscapes that align with known biology and differ from random noise, suggesting conformational organization is an emergent, byproduct of training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend the view of AlphaFold2 from a sequence-to-structure predictor to a learned encoding of protein conformational organization that can be probed directly to gain insight into structural constraints and representations shaped by evolutionary data.

Method: Apply a Gaussian convolution to Evoformer weight tensors (Scaled Gaussian Convolution), then scale results to reveal structured landscapes. Perturbation experiments on ubiquitin, KaiB, and alpha-synuclein across five independently trained models; compare landscapes; use matched-power noise controls to distinguish learned signal from debris; interpret readouts as emergent properties rather than direct training targets.

Result: Ubiquitin: native contacts break in the folding-order pattern consistent with decades of folding data. KaiB: the alternative fold is not recovered under perturbation across models. Alpha-synuclein: five models yield five coherent landscapes, mapping learned representation regions vs. unlearned areas. Matched-power noise yields debris rather than conformations, indicating the signal is not an artifact of corruption. The conformational organization observed under perturbation was not explicitly trained.

Conclusion: AlphaFold2's weights encode structural constraints beyond unperturbed inference, shaped by evolutionary and structural training data. The proposed reading approach—neural spectroscopy using Scaled Gaussian Convolution—offers a protocol to study these representations as a scientific object, revealing emergent conformational organization.

Abstract: AlphaFold2's 93 million parameters, shaped by the evolutionary record of protein structure encoded in the Protein Data Bank and in sequence alignments, are conventionally treated only as machinery for converting sequence to structure. We propose they are also a scientific object that can be analyzed directly: a learned encoding of protein conformational organization that can be probed and characterized. By smoothing the Evoformer's weight tensors with a Gaussian convolution and scaling the result, we show that the trained model produces physically structured conformational landscapes. Under perturbation, ubiquitin's native contacts break in the order established by decades of folding experiments. For KaiB, five independently trained models agree that the alternative fold is not recovered under perturbation. For alpha-synuclein, five models produce five different but coherent landscapes, mapping where the training signal has determined the representation and where it has not. Matched-power noise controls confirm that random corruption of equal magnitude produces debris, not conformations. The model learned to predict static structures; the conformational organization visible under perturbation was not an explicit training target, suggesting it emerged as a byproduct of that objective. AlphaFold2's weights appear to encode structural constraints, shaped by evolutionary and structural training data, that extend beyond what unperturbed inference reveals. We call the approach of reading them neural spectroscopy, and Scaled Gaussian Convolution one such protocol.

</details>


### [139] [DADiff: Diffusion-Driven Cross-Domain Policy Adaptation for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16090)
*Hanyang Chen,Anirudh Satheesh,Longchao Da,Hua Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DADiff is a diffusion-based framework for online dynamics adaptation in reinforcement learning that uses trajectory-level diffusion discrepancies between source and target to estimate dynamics mismatch, enabling two adaptation strategies (reward modification and data selection).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address dynamics mismatch in cross-domain RL under limited target data by a generative-model perspective, beyond discriminators, value-guided filtering, or representation learning.

Method: Propose DADiff, a diffusion-based generative model that explicitly compares source and target trajectory distributions in the next-state generation to quantify dynamics mismatch; develop two adaptation variants—reward modification and data selection; provide theoretical guarantees relating policy performance gap to generative trajectory deviation.

Result: Theoretical bound showing policy performance difference between source and target domains is bounded by the generative trajectory deviation; extensive experiments across environments with various shifts demonstrate superior performance over baselines; code released at the provided GitHub link.

Conclusion: A diffusion-based generative approach is effective for online dynamics adaptation in RL, offering practical variants and theoretical guarantees, and expanding beyond prior domain classifiers or representation-learning strategies.

Abstract: Transferring policies across domains poses a vital challenge in reinforcement learning, due to the dynamics mismatch between the source and target domains. In this paper, we consider the setting of online dynamics adaptation, where policies are trained in the source domain with sufficient data, while only limited interactions with the target domain are allowed. There are a few existing works that address the dynamics mismatch by employing domain classifiers, value-guided data filtering, or representation learning. Instead, we study the domain adaptation problem from a generative modeling perspective. Specifically, we introduce DADiff, a diffusion-based framework that leverages the discrepancy between source and target domain generative trajectories in the generation process of the next state to estimate the dynamics mismatch. Both reward modification and data selection variants are developed to adapt the policy to the target domain. We also provide a theoretical analysis to show that the performance difference of a given policy between the two domains is bounded by the generative trajectory deviation. More discussions on the applicability of the variants and the connection between our theoretical analysis and the prior work are further provided. We conduct extensive experiments in environments with various shifts to validate the effectiveness of our method. The results demonstrate that our method provides superior performance compared to existing approaches, effectively addressing the dynamics mismatch. We provide the code of our method at https://github.com/hanyang-chen/DADiff-release

</details>


### [140] [Understanding Reasoning from Pretraining to Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16097)
*Jingyan Shen,Ang Li,Salman Rahman,Yifan Sun,Micah Goldblum,Matus Telgarsky,Pavel Izmailov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A controlled chess-based study shows pretraining choice strongly shapes RL gains in language models; post-RL performance scales with pretraining loss and tokens, and RL effects differ by problem difficulty. The findings generalize to non-chess domains (math text).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle pretraining and RL effects, answering (1) how pretraining choices shape RL returns, and (2) what RL actually changes in the model, using a controlled pretraining–post-training pipeline.

Method: Pretrain language models from 5M to 1B parameters on human chess games, fine-tune on synthetic reasoning traces, then apply RL on chess puzzles with verifiable rewards. Evaluate across varying pretraining sizes, tokens, and RL compute. Replicate the experiment in a math-domain corpus with a 1B model to test generality.

Result: Post-RL performance at fixed RL compute is well-predicted by the pretraining loss; the slope of RL reward curves scales roughly linearly with the amount of pretraining tokens. RL amplifies correct moves in easy puzzles that the SFT policy already preferred, and reveals correct moves that were nearly absent under SFT in hard puzzles. In math-domain training, longer pretraining yields higher post-RL performance and faster improvement under RL. The work provides a quantitative account of the pretraining–RL interface and a controlled testbed for studying reasoning across the full pipeline.

Conclusion: The study establishes a quantitative framework linking pretraining dynamics to RL outcomes, demonstrating that, beyond scaling, RL interacts with pretraining to reshape reasoning on both easy and hard tasks; the chess testbed serves as a tractable platform for systematically studying the full pretraining-to-post-training pipeline and reasoning development.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become central to improving large language models (LLMs) on complex reasoning tasks, yet RL post-training is largely studied in isolation from the pretraining that precedes it. As a result, two basic questions remain open: (1) how do pretraining choices (model size, data) shape the returns to RL compute, and (2) what does RL actually do to the model? These questions are difficult to study in the standard LLM setting: pretraining corpora are vast and uncontrolled, making it hard to attribute behaviors to pretraining versus RL, and systematic compute sweeps across both stages are prohibitively expensive. To address these challenges, we use chess as a controlled testbed for studying reasoning across the full pretraining-to-post-training pipeline. We follow the standard LLM training pipeline by pretraining language models from 5M to 1B parameters on human chess games, supervised fine-tuning on synthetic reasoning traces, and running RL on chess puzzles with verifiable rewards. Using this framework, we find that the post-RL performance at given RL compute level is well-predicted from the pretraining loss, and slope of the RL reward curves improves approximately linearly with the pretraining tokens. Beyond scaling, we find that RL does not simply sharpen the SFT policy: on easy puzzles it amplifies correct moves the SFT policy already preferred, while on hard puzzles it surfaces correct moves that were nearly absent under SFT. We further test whether our findings transfer beyond chess by training a 1B language model on math-domain text, where the same predictive pattern emerges: longer-pretrained checkpoints reach higher post-RL performance and improve faster under RL. In sum, we provide a quantitative account of the pretraining-to-RL interface and a controlled testbed for studying the science of reasoning across the full pretraining-to-post-training pipeline.

</details>


### [141] [The Honest Quorum Problem: Epistemic Byzantine Fault Tolerance for Agentic Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16109)
*Jun He,Deying Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EBFT (Epistemic Byzantine Fault Tolerance) extends standard BFT by accounting for semantic faults in agentic validators. It introduces two budgets, eδ for coherent invalid endorsements outside the Byzantine set and uε for unusable validator support that hurts liveness, and derives quorum-thresholds for semantic validity, agreement, and liveness along with a calibration method. It argues diversity helps fault tolerance only if it reduces tail risk of invalid endorsements or unusable support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SMR/BFT guarantees of agreement assume correct execution of transition semantics by participants outside the Byzantine set. Agentic validators may semantically endorse invalid transitions due to reasoning errors, creating an Honest Quorum that can form certificates for invalid states. This epistemic fault undermines semantic safety and execution safety, and is likely correlated across agents with shared data or tools.

Method: Define EBFT with two confidence-indexed budgets, eδ and uε. Derive quorum-threshold conditions for semantic validity, consensus, and liveness, and provide a calibration methodology to estimate these budgets. Analyze how adding distinct agents affects fault tolerance via tail-concentration effects on invalid endorsements and unusable support.

Result: A theoretical framework that separates semantic-safety risk from liveness risk and provides explicit quorum-threshold conditions and calibration guidance. It also provides a criterion for when increasing agent diversity improves fault tolerance: only if it measurably reduces the upper-tail concentration of invalid endorsements or unusable support.

Conclusion: EBFT enables a principled trade-off between semantic validity and liveness in agentic distributed systems by quantifying epistemic faults with eδ and uε. It shows that simply increasing agent count is beneficial only if diversity reduces tail risks; the framework supports threshold selection and practical calibration to maintain semantic safety and deployment feasibility.

Abstract: State machine replication (SMR) and Byzantine fault-tolerant (BFT) consensus guarantee agreement despite a bounded number of arbitrary, colluding faulty participants. However, these guarantees rely on participants outside this set correctly executing the protocol's transition semantics. Agentic validators expose a weaker boundary: an authenticated, responsive, non-equivocating, and protocol-compliant reasoning participant may still endorse a semantically invalid transition due to reasoning errors.
  We call this failure mode an epistemic fault, and the collective phenomenon the Honest Quorum Problem (where "honest" means protocol-compliant, not semantically correct). Such a quorum can satisfy ordinary checks while forming a certificate for an invalid transition. Thus, agreement alone does not guarantee semantic validity or execution safety. Furthermore, because agentic validators often share model weights, training distributions, prompts, or toolchains, they are highly susceptible to correlated epistemic faults.
  We define Epistemic Byzantine Fault Tolerance (EBFT), a fault-tolerance model for agentic infrastructure and post-deterministic distributed systems. EBFT augments the conventional Byzantine fault bound with two separate, confidence-indexed quantities: $e_δ$ bounds coherent invalid endorsements outside the Byzantine set, and $u_ε$ bounds unusable validator support that degrades liveness. These quantities characterize semantic safety risk and liveness degradation independently. We derive quorum-threshold conditions for semantic validity, consensus agreement, liveness, and feasible threshold selection, and outline a calibration methodology for estimating these budgets. We show that adding nominally distinct agents improves fault tolerance only when it measurably reduces the upper-tail concentration of invalid endorsements or unusable support.

</details>


### [142] [When Do Multi-Agent Systems Help? An Information Bottleneck Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16133)
*Wendi Yu,Lianhao Zhou,Xiangjue Dong,Sai Sudarshan Barath,Declan Staunton,Byung-Jun Yoon,Xiaoning Qian,James Caverlee,Shuiwang Ji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MAS with bounded inter-agent communication can outperform SAS by compressing redundant context, but only when relay information is not too lossy; with infinite bandwidth, SAS and MAS are equivalent in principle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resolve inconsistent findings on whether multi-agent systems outperform single-agent systems by adopting an information-bottleneck lens to explain when context sharing helps or hurts.

Method: Formulate the MAS vs SAS distinction via an information bottleneck framework, derive a trade-off controlled by beta, prove that SAS can simulate MAS under infinite relay bandwidth, and validate with 18 controlled experiments across five benchmarks and three model scales.

Result: Demonstrates a fundamental equivalence between SAS and MAS under unlimited relay bandwidth; shows MAS gains arise under bounded relays due to context compression balancing information loss; gains are larger for weaker models and when relays are near-sufficient, diminishing for stronger models.

Conclusion: MAS design is an information-bottleneck optimization problem; bounded inter-agent communication can be beneficial or detrimental depending on relay sufficiency and model capability, explaining when MAS provides advantages.

Abstract: LLM powered multi-agent systems (MAS) have emerged as a promising paradigm for complex tasks. However, their advantages over single-agent systems (SAS) remain unclear, with performance varying inconsistently across settings. Here, we provide an information bottleneck perspective on elucidating the differences between MAS and SAS. Specifically, our key observation is that a SAS accumulates its full reasoning trace in one shared context, while a MAS uses isolated local contexts connected by bounded relay messages. We show that, under infinite relay bandwidth, any SAS can be simulated by a MAS that transmits the full upstream context. Thus, the nontrivial advantage of MAS arises under bounded relays, where compression introduces a fundamental trade-off: reducing redundant context can improve efficiency, but may also incur loss of task-relevant information. We formalize this trade-off as an information bottleneck controlled by an effective parameter $β$, which captures how the balance shifts with model capability, and shows that MAS gains arise when context reduction outweighs relay information loss. We conduct 18 controlled experiments across five benchmarks and three model scales to validate our theoretical studies. We observe that MAS consistently helps when relays are near-sufficient, especially for weaker models. In contrast, MAS gains shrink or reverse when relays incur information loss, especially for stronger models that can already extract useful information from redundant context and thus gain little from compression. Our study shows that multi-agent design is fundamentally an information-bottleneck optimization problem. This perspective explains when bounded inter-agent communication helps or hurts.

</details>


### [143] [Improving Improved Kernel PLS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16138)
*Ole-Christian Galbo Engstrøm*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IKPLS speeds up kernel PLS calibration via two core innovations: (1) a direct evaluation strategy for X-rotations R that parallelizes better while preserving the same number of multiplications, and (2) equivalences that allow Y-loadings Q to be computed from quantities already available in the iteration, reducing the per-loading cost from Θ(KM) to Θ(M) for certain M (M=1 or 2 ≤ M < K). These changes yield exact same W, P, Q, R, T as the original IKPLS and provide substantial speedups. Benchmarks show up to two orders of magnitude speedups for the isolated steps and roughly 2× (CPU) or 6× (GPU) for full fits; implemented in the open-source Python package ikpls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To accelerate kernel PLS calibration (IKPLS) on modern hardware without altering the resulting factor matrices and scores, enabling faster fits on large datasets.

Method: R: replace term-by-term accumulation with a direct evaluation that preserves total multiplications but improves parallelism. Q: derive and exploit equivalences to express each Y loading from quantities computed earlier in the same iteration, reducing per-loading complexity from Θ(KM) to Θ(M) for M=1 or 2 ≤ M < K, while keeping W, P, Q, R, T identical to the original.

Result: Demonstrated that the two improvements yield the exact same factor matrices and scores as the original IKPLS. Benchmarks indicate large speedups: up to two orders of magnitude for isolated steps; approximately 2× speedup on CPU and 6× on GPU for full fits. The methods are implemented in the open-source package ikpls.

Conclusion: The work delivers substantial, hardware-friendly efficiency gains for IKPLS without sacrificing accuracy, using a parallelizable evaluation for X-rotations and principled equivalences for Y-loadings, with practical impact demonstrated via npm benchmarks and an open-source implementation.

Abstract: Improved Kernel Partial Least Squares (IKPLS) algorithms 1 and 2 are among the fastest PLS calibration algorithms. This article focuses on two shared steps, the computation of the $\mathbf{X}$ rotations, $\mathbf{R}$, and the $\mathbf{Y}$ loadings, $\mathbf{Q}$, and accelerates both. For $\mathbf{R}$, term-by-term accumulation is replaced by a direct evaluation strategy that requires the same number of multiplications but parallelizes better on modern hardware. For $\mathbf{Q}$, I identify - to the best of my knowledge, for the first time - equivalences showing that each $\mathbf{Y}$ loading is obtainable, up to explicitly derived constants, from quantities already computed earlier in the same iteration, and I exploit them in IKPLS to reduce the cost of each loading from $Θ\left(KM\right)$ to $Θ\left(M\right)$ operations whenever $M = 1$ or $2 \leq M < K$, with $K$ predictor variables (number of columns in $\mathbf{X}$) and $M$ response variables (number of columns in $\mathbf{Y}$). Both improvements provably yield exactly the same $\mathbf{W}$, $\mathbf{P}$, $\mathbf{Q}$, $\mathbf{R}$, and $\mathbf{T}$ as the original algorithms. Benchmarks with NumPy (CPU) and JAX (GPU) show speedups of up to two orders of magnitude for the isolated steps and of approximately $2\times$ (CPU) and $6\times$ (GPU) for entire fits. Both improvements are implemented in the free, open-source Python package \texttt{ikpls}.

</details>


### [144] [PRISA: Proactive Infrastructure LiDAR Framework for Intersection Safety Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16156)
*Tam Bang,Hussam Abubakr,Emiliano de la Garza Villarreal,Truc Phuong Nguyen,Austin Harris,Toru Hirano,Mina Sartipi,Yunfei Xu,Hoang H. Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Modular roadside LiDAR framework (PRISA) for privacy-preserving, real-time risk assessment at intersections using edge-deployed trajectory prediction and dual safety metrics (TTC, PPET) with automatic site-specific data curation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban intersections are high-risk multi-agent environments requiring proactive monitoring; challenges include real-time forecasting, privacy, data labeling burden, and VRU safety.

Method: Two-layer system: sensing/perception layer and plug-and-play risk assessment module. The risk module auto-curates training data from perception outputs to train a trajectory predictor, then runs continuous forecasting and evaluates TTC (longitudinal) and PPET (VRU interactions). Edge deployment on NVIDIA Jetson; evaluation on R-LiViT; live intersection test.

Result: PPET inference latency ~194 ms end-to-end over a 2.4 s predictive horizon; TTC-based detection and perception remain within real-time constraints; validated on public dataset and a live intersection deployment; demonstrates feasibility of proactive intersection safety monitoring with privacy-preserving perception.

Conclusion: PRISA enables privacy-preserving, real-time, edge-based risk assessment at intersections via automatic data curation and dual safety metrics, suitable for long-term traffic observation and proactive VRU safety.

Abstract: Urban intersections are among the most hazardous locations in road networks, posing significant risks to vehicles and vulnerable road users (VRUs) such as pedestrians and cyclists. The complexity of multi-agent interactions demands continuous, real-time monitoring systems capable of anticipating conflicts before they escalate into crashes. We present PRISA, a modular infrastructure LiDAR framework leveraging privacy-preserving, low-light-robust roadside sensors for long-term traffic observation and real-time risk detection at the edge. The framework comprises two core components: a sensing and perception layer and a plug-and-play risk assessment module. The latter automatically curates site-specific training data from accumulated perception outputs to train a trajectory prediction model without manual annotation. It then deploys the trained model for continuous motion forecasting and dual surrogate safety evaluation, using Time-to-Collision (TTC) for longitudinal conflicts and Predicted Post-Encroachment Time (PPET) for crossing and VRU-involved interactions. PRISA is evaluated on the public R-LiViT dataset and deployed on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Thor at a live signalized intersection in Chattanooga, Tennessee. PPET-based assessment operates at 194~ms end-to-end latency over a 2.4-second predictive horizon, with TTC-based detection and perception remaining within real-time constraints, demonstrating practical feasibility for proactive multi-agent intersection safety monitoring.

</details>


### [145] [Behaviour-Conditioned Neural Processes for Adaptive Residential Short-Term Load Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16168)
*Ramin Soleimani,Andrea Visentin,Dirk Pesch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A behaviour-conditioned Attentive Neural Process for context-aware residential STLF, leveraging a discrete behavior latent and a continuous uncertainty latent, with weak supervision; achieves notable MAE/CRPS gains over ANP and better RMSE vs baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Residential short-term load forecasting is challenged by heterogeneity, temporal variability, and diverse behavioral routines; embedding inferred behavioral structure into the forecasting model may improve accuracy and uncertainty quantification.

Method: Introduce a behaviour-conditioned Attentive Neural Process (BC-ANP) where each load profile is a forecasting task. A discrete latent variable, inferred from context, encodes behavior for decoder conditioning, and a continuous latent captures shared functional uncertainty. Weak supervision is provided via clustering-derived signals during training; test-time conditioning uses context-inferred class distributions. Evaluated on SGSC with user-disjoint splits, variable context, and multi-step horizons, against a label-agnostic ANP baseline and fixed-window baselines.

Result: The proposed variants consistently improve MAE and CRPS over ANP across horizons and context settings, with the largest gains under limited context. The best variant yields about 7.9% MAE reduction and 6.9% CRPS reduction relative to ANP. Compared to fixed-window baselines, it achieves lower RMSE across horizons while remaining competitive in MAE, suggesting fewer large deviations under heterogeneous patterns.

Conclusion: Single-model, uncertainty-aware forecasting can effectively handle heterogeneity across households, contexts, and horizons when behavioral structure is embedded in the forecasting process.

Abstract: Residential short-term load forecasting (STLF) is challenging because household demand is heterogeneous, temporally variable, and shaped by diverse behavioural routines. This work investigates whether inferred behavioural structure can be embedded within the forecasting mechanism of a Neural Process-based probabilistic model, rather than used only as an external grouping signal, for context-conditioned residential STLF. We propose a behaviour-conditioned Attentive Neural Process framework that treats each load profile as a forecasting task. Behavioural structure is represented by a discrete latent variable inferred from the available context and used for behaviour-conditioned decoder conditioning, while a continuous latent variable captures shared functional uncertainty across heterogeneous profiles. To enable conditioning without ground-truth behavioural labels, clustering-derived information provides weak supervision during training, whereas test-time conditioning relies only on context-inferred class distributions. Experiments on the Smart Grid, Smart City (SGSC) dataset use user-disjoint train/validation/test splits, variable context lengths, and multi-step forecast horizons, with comparisons against a label-agnostic ANP baseline and fixed-window deterministic STLF baselines. The proposed variants improve MAE and CRPS over ANP across horizons and context settings, with the largest gains under limited context. The best-performing variant achieves average reductions of 7.9% in MAE and 6.9% in CRPS relative to ANP. Compared with fixed-window baselines, this variant achieves lower RMSE across all evaluated horizons while maintaining competitive MAE, suggesting fewer large prediction deviations under heterogeneous consumption patterns. These results support single-model, uncertainty-aware forecasting across heterogeneous households, contexts, and horizons.

</details>


### [146] [When Does Muon Help Agentic Reinforcement Learning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16169)
*Kai Ruan,Jinghao Lin,Zihe Huang,Ziqi Zhou,Qianshan Wei,Xuan Wang,Hao Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Muon can substantially improve RL post-training performance in GiGPO setups by updating hidden weights, with large gains over AdamW in sparse-reward agentic RL; effectiveness depends on learning rate and advantage estimator; results are exploratory and require broader validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate Muon's effectiveness for reinforcement learning post-training and compare with AdamW in sparse-reward agentic RL tasks.

Method: Vanilla Muon applied to hidden weight matrices in GiGPO variants on ALFWorld using Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct; matched single-seed comparisons with AdamW; tested at learning rates 3e-5 and 1e-5; measured final-window validation success, AUC, and update timing; compared GRPO and GraphGPO; high-rate AdamW as controls; multi-seed validation left for future.

Result: Under GiGPO, Muon applied to hidden matrices raises final-window validation success from 0.290 to 0.546 (+88%). At 3e-5, GRPO improves from 0.161 to 0.268; GraphGPO’s late-window gap narrows near saturation. At 1e-5, GraphGPO Muon reaches 0.901, raises normalized validation AUC from 0.399 to 0.556, and achieves 0.5 and 0.75 success 30 and 60 updates earlier, respectively.

Conclusion: Muon can benefit agentic RL and warrants joint study of the policy optimizer, advantage estimator, and learning rate; multi-seed and cross-task validation remain open.

Abstract: Muon is competitive with AdamW in large-scale pre-training, but its value for reinforcement-learning (RL) post-training remains unclear. We study vanilla Muon in sparse-reward agentic RL through matched single-seed comparisons with AdamW on ALFWorld using Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct. Under Group-in-Group Policy Optimization (GiGPO), applying Muon only to hidden weight matrices raises final-window validation success from 0.290 to 0.546 (+88%); high-rate AdamW controls retain no post-update success. The effect depends on the advantage estimator and learning rate. At 3e-5, Muon improves GRPO from 0.161 to 0.268, whereas GraphGPO's late-window gap narrows near saturation. At 1e-5, GraphGPO Muon reaches 0.901, raises normalized validation AUC from 0.399 to 0.556, and reaches 0.5 and 0.75 success 30 and 60 updates earlier, respectively. These exploratory results show that Muon can benefit agentic RL and motivate studying the policy optimizer, advantage estimator, and learning rate jointly. Multi-seed and cross-task validation remain open.

</details>


### [147] [Physics-enhanced reinforcement learning for real-time optimal control of dynamical systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16177)
*Matteo Tomasetto,Nicolò Botteghi,Gabriele Bruni,Andrea Manzoni*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physics-Enhanced Reinforcement Learning (PEARL) introduces an actor-adjoint framework that leverages differentiable dynamics and adjoint-based sensitivities to reduce interactions and enable scalable, high-dimensional RL for parametric dynamical systems, demonstrated on unsteady-flow navigation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL often suffers from sample inefficiency and the curse of dimensionality when applied to nonlinear, parametric dynamical systems. Incorporating physics-based differentiable models and adjoint sensitivities can reduce data requirements and stabilize learning.

Method: PEARL uses an actor-adjoint algorithm: automatic differentiation computes short-horizon policy gradients; adjoint-based sensitivities of future returns are approximated via neural networks. This physics-guided learning lowers environment interactions and mitigates long-term gradient instability while exploiting differentiable dynamics.

Result: In two challenging parametric navigation problems in unsteady flows, PEARL outperforms state-of-the-art RL methods, is sample-efficient, generalizes across multiple scenarios, and scales to high-dimensional state/action spaces without requiring low-dimensional representations or multi-agent setups.

Conclusion: PEARL effectively bridges reinforcement learning and traditional optimal control for differentiable, parametric dynamical systems, enabling scalable, physics-guided RL. It achieves better sample efficiency and generalization, though it relies on differentiable dynamics and surrogate adjoint sensitivities; future work could address non-differentiable components and robustness to model mismatch.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has recently emerged as a promising feedback control strategy for nonlinear and complex dynamical systems. However, RL algorithms are sample inefficient and require a large number of interaction with the environment to synthesize optimal control strategies. Consequently, applications of RL are typically limited to sparse sensors and actuators due to the curse of dimensionality entailed by the exploration-exploitation dilemma in high-dimensional spaces. In this work, we bridge RL and traditional optimal control for dynamical system with a novel Physics-EnhAnced Reinforcement Learning (PEARL) paradigm tailored to the control of high-dimensional and parametric dynamical systems, exploiting the differentibility of their dynamics.
