Specifically, PEARL employs an actor-adjoint algorithm that leverages automatic differentiation to compute policy gradients over short horizons and adjoint-based sensitivities of future returns approximated via neural networks, significantly reducing the number of environment interactions, while mitigating long-term gradient instabilities. Through two challenging parametric navigation problems in unsteady flows, we show that PEARL (i) effectively exploits differentiable environments to outperform state-of-the-art RL algorithms, (ii) is sample efficient, thanks to the physics-guided policy learning, (iii) generalizes across multiple scenarios, which is crucial when dealing with parametric systems, and (iv) enables scaling RL to high-dimensional state and action spaces, without requiring low-dimensional state representations or multi-agent strategies.

</details>


### [148] [A Blueprint for Equilibrium-Based Differentiable Continuous-Variable Thermodynamic Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16183)
*Owen Lockwood,Jérémy Béjanin,Joost Bus,Christopher Chamberland,Patrick Huembeli,Frank Schäfer,Guillaume Verdon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Blueprint for energy-efficient, fast probabilistic ML using stochastic analog thermodynamic hardware implementing Langevin dynamics to sample from energy-based models; preliminary superconducting hardware demonstrates feasibility; aims to reduce energy and latency in ML workloads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address rising energy consumption and latency in ML by shifting computation to hardware-native stochastic processes, enabling efficient sampling from energy-based models within a probabilistic graphical models framework.

Method: Design tunable energy potentials realized in physical hardware to drive Langevin dynamics; sample and train energy-based models; analyze runtime and energy via theory and numerical simulations; present preliminary stochastic analog superconducting circuits driven by thermal noise.

Result: Provide a coherent blueprint and theoretical/numerical evaluation; demonstrate construction/training of hardware-native energy-based models; present a preliminary experimental realization with stochastic analog superconducting circuits; outline a path toward energy-efficient thermodynamic ML hardware.

Conclusion: Thermodynamic hardware leveraging stochastic analog processes can enable energy-efficient probabilistic ML; the proposed stack shows promise, with theoretical, numerical, and initial experimental support; further hardware demonstrations and optimization are anticipated.

Abstract: To address the escalating energy and latency demands of machine-learning workloads, we introduce a blueprint for an energy-efficient and fast thermodynamic computing stack that leverages stochastic analog processes in physical hardware. In this work, we focus on energy-based thermodynamic computing where the stochastic process is well described by Langevin dynamics with tunable energy potentials. The implementation of such potentials in physical hardware enables us to generate and sample from basic parameterized energy-based models. We demonstrate how to construct and train popular classes of machine learning models based on these hardware-native energy-based models, using the framework of probabilistic graphical models. We analyze the runtime and energy consumption of different models in this thermodynamic paradigm based on theoretical considerations and numerical studies. As a preliminary experimental realization of such hardware, we present our stochastic analog superconducting circuits driven by thermal noise. Together, these results outline a path toward energy-efficient thermodynamic hardware for probabilistic machine learning.

</details>


### [149] [PagedWeight: Efficient MoE LLM Serving with Dynamic Quality-Aware Weight Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16184)
*Yuchen Yang,Yifan Zhao,Anisha Dasgupta,Sasa Misailovic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PagedWeight proposes a runtime dynamic quantization for MoE LLMs that balances expert weight precision with KV-cache memory, achieving FP16-equivalent accuracy while delivering substantial memory savings and throughput gains, outperforming existing baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In KV-cache-intensive MoE serving, there is a tension between the GPU memory required for model weights and the growing KV cache; a method is needed to navigate accuracy-memory-throughput tradeoffs.

Method: Dynamic runtime quantization of MoE weights (paged weight concept) to balance precision and KV-cache sizes; exposes and navigates accuracy, memory, and throughput/latency tradeoffs across memory-sensitive serving scenarios.

Result: Achieves FP16-equivalent accuracy with up to 72.0% GPU memory savings and 1.94× throughput; improves quality over quantization methods by up to 39.3% at similar memory budgets with at most 4.1% throughput loss.

Conclusion: PagedWeight effectively navigates the accuracy-memory-throughput tradeoffs in MoE LLM serving, delivering improved quality-memory performance across scenarios.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is a popular class of large language models (LLMs), offering high efficiency and accuracy. However, in KV-cache-intensive serving scenarios, MoEs often exhibit a tension between the GPU memory requirements of the model weights and the growing KV cache. We propose PagedWeight, a novel management method for MoE LLM serving that dynamically quantizes MoE model's weights at runtime and balances expert-weight precision with the KV cache sizes. PagedWeight exposes and effectively navigates the complex tradeoff between the model's task accuracy, memory consumption, and throughput/latency. Across several memory-sensitive MoE serving scenarios, PagedWeight improves the quality-memory tradeoff over several existing quantization baselines. PagedWeight achieves FP16-equivalent accuracy with up to 72.0% GPU memory savings and 1.94$\times$ throughput improvement, and improves quality over quantization methods by up to 39.3% at a similar memory budget with at most 4.1% throughput loss.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [150] [Xiaomi-Robotics-1: Scaling Vision-Language-Action Models with over 100K Hours of Real-World Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15330)
*Xiaomi Robotics Team,Jun Guo,Piaopiao Jin,Jason Li,Peiyan Li,Yingyan Li,Futeng Liu,Wanli Peng,Optimus Qin,Yifei Su,Nan Sun,Qiao Sun,Runze Suo,Heyun Wang,Yunhong Wang,Rujie Wu,Caoyu Xia,Lina Zhang,Jack Zhao,Guoliang Chen,Wenlong Chen,Xinze He,Bin Li,Qing Li,Zhuorong Li,Heng Qu,Wenxuan Song,Diyun Xiang,Yifan Xie,Peiran Xu,Hangjun Ye,Wen Ye,Han Zhao,Quanyun Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A scalable two-stage VLA model (Xiaomi-Robotics-1) for robot manipulation with zero-shot generalization and data-efficient fine-tuning; achieves SOTA on RoboCasa365 and RoboDojo; will release code and checkpoints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To build a foundational robot policy that can follow natural language instructions and generalize to unseen environments, enabled by large-scale real-world trajectories and language-conditioned action learning.

Method: Two-stage training: (1) pre-training on 100k+ hours of real-world manipulation trajectories; (2) scalable auto-labeling that attaches natural language descriptions of scene state transitions to trajectory clips; (3) post-training to align with robot embodiments and human imperative prompts. Evaluation across multiple simulation benchmarks; efficient fine-tuning for dexterous tasks.

Result: Demonstrates strong scaling: larger pre-training data and models improve post-training real-robot performance in unseen environments. Outperforms prior SOTA on RoboCasa365 (57.6% vs 46.6%) and RoboDojo (average 20.07 vs 13.07). Robust across multiple benchmarks; code and model checkpoints planned for release.

Conclusion: Xiaomi-Robotics-1 provides a robust foundation policy with high data efficiency for rapid transfer to downstream and unseen tasks, achieving state-of-the-art results and establishing scalable VLA capabilities; emphasizes the importance of language-conditioned trajectory auto-labeling and two-stage training.

Abstract: We present Xiaomi-Robotics-1, a foundational vision-language-action (VLA) model capable of (1) following diverse language instructions to perform a wide range of mobile manipulation tasks in unseen environments out-of-the-box, and (2) efficiently adapting to novel downstream tasks with minimal fine-tuning data. We propose a two-stage training recipe consisting of pre-training and post-training. During pre-training, we imbue the model with broad and generalizable action-generation capabilities by training on over 100k hours of real-world manipulation trajectories collected via UMI devices. Crucially, we develop a scalable auto-labeling pipeline that annotates trajectory clips with natural languages describing scene state transitions, providing rich and precise conditioning for action learning. During post-training, we aim to align these capabilities with robot embodiments and imperative instructions that humans naturally use to prompt robots. Extensive experiments demonstrate strong scaling behavior. Xiaomi-Robotics-1 consistently improves with increased data scales and model sizes during pre-training. This scaling behavior directly transfers to post-training, where a stronger pre-training model yields better out-of-the-box real-robot performance in unseen environments. Furthermore, Xiaomi-Robotics-1 serves as a strong robot foundation policy that can be efficiently fine-tuned on complex, dexterous tasks with high data efficiency. Across multiple simulation benchmarks, Xiaomi-Robotics-1 outperforms state-of-the-art methods. Notably, it establishes a new state-of-the-art with a 57.6% success rate on RoboCasa365, surpassing the previous best of 46.6%. Furthermore, it achieves an average score of 20.07 on RoboDojo, significantly outperforming the prior state-of-the-art (13.07). Code and model checkpoints will be released. Project page: https://robotics.xiaomi.com/xiaomi-robotics-1.html

</details>


### [151] [NeuroCommitSSM: Decision-Centric Shared Autonomy for Safe Assistive Manipulation via EEG-EMG-ET Commit Readiness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15395)
*Tipu Sultan,Param Sangani,Kody Cool,Pascal Sikorski,Guangping Liu,Hadi Akbarpour,Madi Babaiasl*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A decision-centric multi-modal framework (NeuroCommitSSM) for safe assistive robotic manipulation, predicting commit-readiness from EEG/EMG/eye-tracking and gating actions with a feasibility-aware supervisor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce unsafe or premature robotic actions by modeling when to execute, not just what to do, in assistive manipulation using neural signals and real-time perception/robot-state checks.

Method: Continuous commit-readiness score c_t in [0,1] from synchronized EEG/EMG/ET, converted to discrete commits via dwell and hysteresis. A three-state HAC supervisor gates execution by requiring sustained neural readiness and feasibility signals (target visibility, IK solvability, collision-free planning). Evaluation on N=32 subjects across 5 ADL tasks with LOSO CV and seven sensor-dropout scenarios (S0-S6). Compared against a Temporal Convolutional Network baseline; HIL validation on Kinova Gen3. Metrics include action-balanced accuracy and FP/1k REST.

Result: 0.950 action-balanced accuracy with 0.75 FP/1k REST overall. EEG-only: 0.785 balanced accuracy and 0.29 FP/1k REST; TCN baseline yields 99.95 FP/1k REST under EEG-only. HAC reduces false starts and decision instability; hardware-in-the-loop shows safer, more stable execution without sacrificing task success; robustness to sensor loss across dropout scenarios.

Conclusion: Integrates neural readiness with feasibility-aware safety checks to improve safe, reliable assistive manipulation. Materials are open-sourced; results suggest substantial gains in safety with maintained task performance. Future work includes latency analysis, broader validation, and cross-device generalization.

Abstract: We present NeuroCommitSSM, a decision-centric framework that models when to execute, not just what to do, for safe commit-to-execute control in assistive robotic manipulation. NeuroCommitSSM predicts a continuous commit-readiness score c_t in [0,1] from synchronized electroencephalography (EEG), electromyography (EMG), and eye-tracking (ET), and converts it into discrete commit events through dwell and hysteresis filtering. A three-state finite-state supervisor, HOLD-ASSIST-COMMIT (HAC), gates execution by requiring both a sustained commit-readiness signal from the neural model and real-time perception and robot-state feasibility, including target visibility, inverse kinematics solvability, and collision-free planning, before initiating motion. We evaluate the framework on N=32 subjects performing five activities of daily living (ADL) tasks aligned with the International Classification of Functioning, Disability and Health (ICF), using leave-one-subject-out (LOSO) cross-validation and seven sensor-dropout scenarios (S0-S6). NeuroCommitSSM achieves 0.950 action-balanced accuracy with 0.75 false commit events per 1000 REST windows (FP/1k REST), and maintains low false commits and stable state transitions under sensor loss. For example, in the EEG-only condition, it achieves 0.785 balanced accuracy and 0.29 FP/1k REST, whereas the Temporal Convolutional Network baseline produces 99.95 FP/1k REST under the same condition. Hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) validation on a Kinova Gen3 arm shows that feasibility-checked execution reduces false starts and decision instability without sacrificing task success. Supplementary materials, including code, datasets, videos, and additional analyses, are available at https://madibabaiasl.github.io/NeuroCommitSSM/.

</details>


### [152] [Robust Silicone Pour Casting and Sensor Embedding Procedures for Soft Robotic Actuators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15422)
*Harshit Thakker,Paul Dela Cruz,Mostafa Mo. Massoud,Jacqueline Libby*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Shows robust, scalable two-part pour-cast fabrication of soft pneumatic actuators with embedded thin-film flex sensors, plus FEM analysis, PID-controlled actuation, and automated calibration, benchmarked across two operators and 24 fabrications to enable broader adoption of soft robotics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reproducible and scalable fabrication of soft robots is challenging due to labor-intensive methods and operator variability; two-part pour casting remains attractive if issues like cavity clogging and air-tight sealing are resolved, along with reliable sensor integration.

Method: Developed robust two-part pour casting procedures to prevent internal cavity clogging and ensure airtight seals; integrated thin-film flex sensors with a robust embedding process; performed FEM to analyze stress/deformation under pressure; implemented PID-controlled pneumatic actuation; used automated image processing to calibrate the flex sensor to bending angle; conducted staircase and sinusoidal actuation tests; validated with both simulated and experimental bending angles; cross-operator and multi-fabrication benchmarking (two operators, 24 fabrications).

Result: Demonstrated repeatable and robust fabrication across operators and 24 fabrications; achieved airtight seals and clog-free cavities; accurate, repeatable sensor data with auto-calibration; angle responses show repeatability with some hysteresis due to viscoelasticity; FEM and experimental results show comparable bending-angle performance; established benchmarks for broader adoption of soft robotics.

Conclusion: The presented fabrication and sensing workflow yields repeatable, scalable production of soft pneumatic actuators and reliable sensing, with validated benchmarks across operators and fabrications, supporting wider industrial adoption of soft robotics.

Abstract: Soft robots are well-suited for applications such as rehabilitation and surgery that require adaptable and safe interaction with their environment. However, the challenges of reproducible and scalable fabrication of soft robots limit their real-world deployment. Various fabrication methods have been introduced, but many are labor-intensive and prone to human error. Therefore, traditional two-part pour casting remains an attractive option. This paper presents procedures for robust, repeatable, and scalable fabrication of soft pneumatic actuators using two-part pour casting. The presented methods prevent internal cavity clogging and ensure air-tight sealing. Additionally, a robust sensor embedding procedure for thin-film flex sensors is presented, which allows for accurate and repeatable data acquisition. Finite Element Modeling (FEM) of the soft actuator is performed to analyze stress and deformation from internal pressure loadings. Pneumatic actuation experiments with PID pressure control are performed. Automated image processing is used to calibrate the embedded flex sensor to bending angle measurements. Staircase and sinusoidal profile actuation experiments validate the performance of the fabricated actuator. Angle response experiments for the staircase input show repeatable performance, and the sinusoidal input shows a small amount of hysteresis consistent with viscoelastic response to pneumatic actuation of soft actuators. Simulated and real-world bending angles show comparable response. These methods provide a repeatable and robust fabrication procedure, validated across two operators and 24 successful fabrications, along with benchmark simulations and experimental testing. These benchmarks will enable more widespread adoption of soft robotics.

</details>


### [153] [VTAP Gripper: Synergizing Fingertip Sensing and a Visuo-Tactile Active Palm for Dexterous In-Hand Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15448)
*Yuhao Zhou,Sheeraz Athar,Zhixian Hu,Binghao Huang,Yunzhu Li,Juan Wachs,Yu She*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A tactile-reactive VTAP gripper with reconfigurable tactile-fingers enabling robust grasping and manipulation via visuo-tactile fusion and staged teleoperation retargeting; validated on diverse tasks; offers a practical reference architecture for learning-based dexterous manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable dexterous manipulation with robust grasping while reducing reliance on high-DOF anthropomorphic hands by leveraging visuo-tactile sensing and staged teleoperation.

Method: Integrates Visuo-Tactile Active Palm (VTAP) with compliant, reconfigurable fingers carrying tactile arrays; fuses long-range visual localization with contact-rich tactile feedback; introduces staged, gesture-conditioned retargeting framework for dexterous teleoperation; evaluates on tasks.

Result: Reactive grasping of YCB and fragile objects, in-hand syringe reorientation and plunger actuation, singulation of clustered objects down to 3 mm, and vision-tactile peg-in-hole insertion; demonstrates high manipulation performance via coordinated finger-palm interaction and multi-modal sensing; avoids reliance on high-DOF anthropomorphic designs.

Conclusion: The VTAP gripper and retargeting framework provide a practical, flexible reference architecture for dexterous manipulation and data collection, enabling effective manipulation and learning-based approaches without requiring highly anthropomorphic hands.

Abstract: This paper presents a tactile-reactive gripper that integrates a Visuo-Tactile Active Palm (VTAP) and compliant, reconfigurable fingers equipped with tactile array sensors. The design exploits structured finger-palm synergy and multi-modal perception to achieve both robust grasping and fine manipulation. The actuated bi-modal palm seamlessly combines long-range visual localization with contact-rich tactile feedback, substantially extending the system's manipulation capability. To bridge the embodiment gap between human hand motion and the heterogeneous three-finger structure, we further propose a staged, gesture-conditioned retargeting framework for dexterous teleoperation. Extensive experiments validate the system across a range of challenging tasks: reactive grasping of YCB and fragile objects, in-hand syringe reorientation and plunger actuation, singulation of clustered objects down to 3 mm in diameter, and vision-tactile peg-in-hole insertion. Results demonstrate that high manipulation performance can be achieved through coordinated finger-palm interaction and multi-modal sensing, without resorting to high degrees of freedom anthropomorphic designs. The VTAP gripper and its retargeting framework offer a practical reference architecture for dexterous gripper design, manipulation, and contact-rich data collection in support of learning-based approaches. Project webpage: https://yuhochau.github.io/vtap/.

</details>


### [154] [Risk-Aware Preference Learning for Stochastic Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15483)
*Yi-Shiuan Tung,Yuni Wu,Wei Jiang,Alessandro Roncone,Bradley Hayes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CPT-based reward learning reduces regret vs EU when human preferences are risk-sensitive in robot navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humans overweight rare negative events and exhibit loss aversion; treating preferences with EU may misalign learned rewards in safety-critical, stochastic robot tasks.

Method: Compare EU and CPT within a Bradley-Terry preference learning framework; use preliminary experiments with risk-sensitive preference generation.

Result: CPT-based learners achieve substantially lower regret than EU-based learners in the presence of risk-sensitive user preferences.

Conclusion: Modeling human risk sensitivity (via CPT) is beneficial for learning rewards from preferences over stochastic robot outcomes, especially in safety-critical domains.

Abstract: Learning reward functions from human preferences is a widely used approach for aligning robot behavior with user expectations in human-robot interaction. Most existing approaches assume that humans evaluate uncertain outcomes using expected utility (EU), aggregating outcome utilities linearly with their probabilities. However, behavioral evidence shows that humans are systematically risk-sensitive, overweighting rare negative events and exhibiting loss aversion. We study the consequences of this mismatch in social robot navigation, where safety-critical outcomes (e.g., collisions) are rare but highly consequential. We compare EU with Cumulative Prospect Theory (CPT), a nonlinear model of human decision-making, within a Bradley-Terry preference learning framework. Our preliminary experiments show that when preferences are generated by risk-sensitive users, CPT-based learners recover reward functions with substantially lower regret compared to EU-based learners. Our results highlight the importance of modeling human risk sensitivity when learning rewards from preferences over stochastic robot outcomes.

</details>


### [155] [Environment Design for Reliable Shared Autonomy with Probabilistic Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15487)
*Yi-Shiuan Tung,Himanshu Gupta,Gyanig Kumar,Heyang Huang,Bradley Hayes,Alessandro Roncone*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Optimizing workspace layout improves goal inference reliability in shared autonomy by increasing separability of candidate goals under noisy user input, with probabilistic guarantees and validation in simulation and a real-world system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address how workspace design, not just inference algorithms, affects the difficulty of inferring user intent in shared autonomy.

Method: Model the effect of object layout on the separability of candidate goals under bounded input noise, formulate a layout-optimization problem with a probabilistic correctness guarantee, and validate via tabletop simulations across scenarios plus a real-world shared autonomy system integrating the proposed inference framework.

Result: Simulations show optimized layouts reduce ambiguity and improve goal inference reliability compared with baseline arrangements; a real-world system demonstrates feasibility of integrating the inference framework with environment design.

Conclusion: Workspace design is a complementary axis to algorithmic inference for enhancing shared autonomy, enabling more reliable intent inference when combined with optimized layouts.

Abstract: Shared autonomy enables humans and robots to collaboratively perform tasks by combining human input with autonomous assistance. Most prior work focuses on improving intent inference under a fixed environment, overlooking how workspace design itself affects inference difficulty. We observe that the physical arrangement of objects directly influences the separability of candidate goals under noisy user inputs. We formulate workspace design as an optimization problem and derive a probabilistic correctness guarantee under a bounded noise model. Through simulation experiments across multiple tabletop scenarios, we show that optimized layouts improve goal inference reliability and reduce ambiguity compared to baseline arrangements. We further demonstrate a real-world shared autonomy system that integrates the proposed inference framework. This highlights the role of environment design as a complementary axis for improving shared autonomy systems.

</details>


### [156] [Multi-Objective Kinodynamic Motion Planning with Asymptotic Pareto Optimality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15508)
*Yusif Razzaq,Anne Theurkauf,Nisar Ahmed,Morteza Lahijanian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A unified SST-based framework for multi-objective kinodynamic motion planning that supports lexicographic, constrained, and Pareto-front optimization, overcoming scalarization limitations with theoretical guarantees and empirical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address multi-objective motion planning under kinodynamic constraints and the inadequacy of cost scalarization methods for continuous systems, necessitating algorithms that can handle lexicographic, constrained, and Pareto-front objectives.

Method: Replace the single witness representative in SST with a set of locally Pareto-optimal representatives, yielding three algorithms: lexSST (lexicographic minimization), coSST (constrained optimization), and poSST (Pareto-front approximation), with theoretical guarantees.

Result: The authors establish completeness and optimality guarantees for the proposed algorithms and validate their performance through extensive experiments, demonstrating effectiveness across the three problem classes.

Conclusion: A unified, theoretically-grounded framework for multi-objective kinodynamic planning that overcomes scalarization limitations and provides practical algorithms for lexicographic, constrained, and Pareto-front optimization.

Abstract: In this paper, we address the challenge of multi-objective motion planning for systems under kinodynamic constraints. We consider three problem classes: (i) lexicographic optimization, in which objectives are minimized according to a strict priority ordering, (ii) constrained optimization, in which a primary objective is minimized subject to bounds on the remaining costs, and (iii) Pareto front optimization, in which the goal is to approximate the full set of optimal trade-offs among competing objectives. We first show that established cost scalarization methods for multi-objective problems cannot be extended to continuous-domain systems with correctness guarantees. Then, we propose a unified algorithmic framework built upon the Stable Sparse-RRT (SST) algorithm, in which the single representative maintained at each witness neighborhood is replaced by a representative set of locally Pareto-optimal nodes. This structure gives rise to three distinct algorithms: lexSST for lexicographic minimization, coSST for constrained optimization, and poSST for Pareto-front approximation. We provide theoretical guarantees for the completeness and optimality of our algorithms and demonstrate their effectiveness through extensive empirical evaluations.

</details>


### [157] [PACE: Persona Adaptation through Conversational Elicitation in Human-Robot Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15579)
*Peizhen Li,Longbing Cao,Megani Rajendran,Timothy Liu,Aik Beng Ng,Simon See*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PACE enables dynamic, user-contextual personas on Ameca via interactive elicitation and structured prompts, with embodied multimodal deployment and HRI evaluation against a generic baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address rigidity and poor adaptability of static robot identities in HRI; enhance trust, relevance, and interaction quality by tailoring personas to individual users.

Method: Interactive Persona Elicitation Pipeline gathers user input through Q&A, synthesizes a psychologically grounded, multi-perspective persona; compiles a structured persona prompt; integrates with embodied Ameca system to generate multimodal behaviors; evaluates in HRI.

Result: Dynamic, personalized personas improved user trust, perceived anthropomorphism, persona consistency, personal relevance, and interaction quality versus a generic baseline; demonstrates scalable deployment for embodied humanoid assistants.

Conclusion: The framework enables scalable, interactive, and reliable identities in embodied humanoids, with demonstrated feasibility on Ameca and a publicly available video demonstration.

Abstract: Equipping humanoid robots with coherent and adaptable personas is crucial for fostering natural, engaging, and trustworthy human-robot interaction (HRI). However, existing approaches often rely on static, hard-coded identities that lack the flexibility to adapt to individual user contexts. In this paper, we present PACE (Persona Adaptation through Conversational Elicitation), a novel framework for the interactive generation and deployment of structured personas on the Ameca humanoid robot. Our system introduces an Interactive Persona Elicitation Pipeline, enabling the robot to dynamically synthesize a tailored, psychologically grounded identity through user Q&A. This elicitation process feeds into a persona prompt compilation phase, generating a structured persona prompt built upon multi-perspective dimensions. We detail the Embodied System Integration required to translate this structured specification into expressive, multimodal humanoid behaviors. Through a comprehensive empirical HRI evaluation, we assess the impact of dynamically generated personas on user trust, perceived anthropomorphism, persona consistency, personal relevance, and interaction quality compared to a generic baseline. These contributions establish a scalable pathway for deploying personalized, interactive, and reliable identities in embodied humanoid assistants. Video demo is available at: https://lipzh5.github.io/PACE/

</details>


### [158] [A Model-Based Decoupling Strategy for Proprioception and Contact Sensing in an Architected Soft Manipulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15582)
*Francesco Stella,Annan Zhang,Cosimo Della Santina,Josie Hughes,Daniela Rus*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Model-based, sensor-rich soft segment decouples proprioception and contact using six embedded pressure sensors per segment and a piecewise constant curvature mapping with Huber-based outlier detection; demonstrated in Air-Helix with high shape accuracy and contact detection, enabling tactile sensing and manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Soft continuum robots lack robust proprioception and contact sensing due to sparse, deformable architectures; need concurrent sensing with local redundancy resolution without bulky sensors.

Method: Each ITH segment houses six air channels in a zigzag around the circumference. With three primary DOFs (axial, bending x, bending y), pressures form an overdetermined mapping to shape. A piecewise constant curvature model translates pressures to shape; Huber regression identifies outlier channels whose residuals signal external contact. Eight segments assembled into Air-Helix; demonstrations include tactile teaching, admittance-controlled force regulation, and tactile object reconstruction.

Result: Single-segment proprioception yields bending error 0.11 ± 0.02; contact detection rate 97% over 178 trials. Eight-segment Air-Helix shows exploratory whole-arm capabilities.

Conclusion: Localized fluidic innervation with model-based redundancy resolution enables concurrent proprioception and contact sensing in architected soft robots, offering a practical path forward.

Abstract: Soft continuum robots require embedded sensing for proprioception and contact detection, yet integrating sensors into sparse, highly deformable architected structures remains challenging. We present a model-based strategy that decouples proprioceptive and contact signals from a common set of fluidic pressure sensors embedded in a soft architected segment. Each segment of the Innervated Trimmed Helicoid (ITH) contains six air channels routed in a localized zigzag pattern along the circumference. With only three principal kinematic degrees of freedom (axial compression, bending in x, bending in y), the six pressure readings form an overdetermined system. A piecewise constant curvature model maps pressures to shape, and Huber regression identifies outlier channels whose residuals indicate external contact. On a single ITH segment, this approach achieves proprioceptive shape estimation with a relative bending error of 0.11 +/- 0.02 and a contact detection rate of 97% across 178 trials. We integrate eight ITH segments into Air-Helix, a tendon-driven soft continuum manipulator, and present exploratory whole-arm demonstrations that include tactile teaching by demonstration, admittance-controlled force regulation, and tactile object reconstruction. The results suggest that localized fluidic innervation combined with model-based redundancy resolution is a practical path toward concurrent proprioception and contact sensing in architected soft robots.

</details>


### [159] [MemoGuard: An Adaptive Runtime for Guarding Against Memory Traps in Communication-Limited Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15589)
*Rajat Bhattacharjya,Hyeonjong Ju,Sing-Yao Wu,Eli Bozorgzadeh,Nikil Dutt*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MemoGuard is an adaptive runtime that validates episodic memories against topology, resources, and outcomes before reuse in communication-limited robots, balancing safety and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory-based action reuse can be unsafe when topology, resources, or prior outcomes have changed; there is a safety-efficiency trade-off between similarity-driven reuse and always-onboard reasoning.

Method: Graph-based corridor-inspection simulator; MemoGuard enforces topology/resource/outcome contracts on memories before reuse, fallback to local reasoning only when validation fails; compares against similarity-only top-1 reuse and always reasoning baselines; evaluates on NVIDIA Jetson AGX Xavier with local llama3.2:3b.

Result: MemoGuard reduces battery safety violations by 76.6% compared to similarity-only top-1 reuse and reduces fallback calls by 21.4% compared to always reasoning; on Jetson AGX Xavier with llama3.2:3b, avoids 3.67 s and 36.97 J of fallback-reasoning overhead per trial; repository: https://github.com/hetheiin/memoguard.

Conclusion: MemoGuard provides a safety-efficient design space for memory-based decision making in robots, reducing unsafe high-similarity episodes while lowering fallback computation and energy costs; demonstrated viability and released as open-source.

Abstract: Communication-limited robots in mission-critical scenarios such as disaster inspection and search-and-rescue must make reliable onboard decisions without access to remote operators or high-capacity reasoning services. Episodic memory reuse is an attractive low-cost fallback, but retrieval similarity does not guarantee execution validity, i.e., a retrieved action may match the current context yet be unsafe due to changed topology, insufficient battery margin, or unreliable prior outcomes. We call such high-similarity but execution-invalid episodes memory traps. This creates a safety-efficiency design space where similarity only reuse minimizes fallback cost but can be unsafe, while always invoking local reasoning improves safety at high computational and energy cost. This paper presents MemoGuard, a lightweight adaptive runtime that validates episodic memories against topology, resource, and outcome contracts before reuse, invoking fallback only when validation fails. In a graph-based corridor-inspection simulator, MemoGuard reduces battery safety violations by 76.6% over similarity-only top-1 reuse while reducing fallback calls by 21.4% over always reasoning. On an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Xavier with local llama3.2:3b fallback reasoning, this corresponds to 3.67 s and 36.97 J of avoided fallback-reasoning overhead per trial. We open-source MemoGuard at https://github.com/hetheiin/memoguard.

</details>


### [160] [AEGIS: Assay-Aware Protocol Validation and Runtime Monitoring for Open-Source Liquid Handling Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15620)
*Priyanka V. Setty,Arvind Ramanathan,Ian Foster,Rick Stevens*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AEGIS is a two-layer guardian for open-source OT-2 liquid handlers that combines pre-flight assay-rule validation with runtime visual monitoring to detect protocol-invariant violations and physical execution failures, achieving high accuracy and low cost while remaining open-source.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-source, low-cost liquid handlers run open-loop and lack pressure-based monitoring, risking silent protocol violations and runtime failures; there is a need to unify pre-flight, assay-aware validation with runtime, data-driven monitoring to improve reliability of autonomous labs.

Method: Layer 1 uses a curated, machine-readable assay rule database paired with an LLM to reason over OT-2 Python code, achieving high F1 across multiple assay families using several backends; Layer 2 trains a PCA-based world model on YOLO-cropped four-frame pipette trajectories to detect execution faults, with leakage-free leave-one-plate-out evaluation; a live demo validates no-tip and partial-dispense events, with a VLM self-vote gate. Cascade triage keeps VLM costs low, and the system is open-source.

Result: Layer 1 adjusted F1 = 0.97 on 24 protocols across five assay families; multiple ablations show superiority over rules-only and LLM-only baselines. Layer 2 average precision = 0.89, operating-point F1 = 0.71 (AUROC = 0.80) in a leave-one-plate-out setting; practical detection of no-tip failures and partial-dispense events during live OT-2 tests; small-pipette (p20) resolution limit explored (F1 = 0.47). Live demonstration reports deterministic no-tip detection and partial-dispense recall 5/5 with a self-vote gate; per-plate VLM cost ~ $1.63 vs $10.33 for always-on VLM. The system is open-source and claims to be the first to unify pre-flight assay-aware validation with runtime visual monitoring for an open-source liquid handler.

Conclusion: AEGIS demonstrates a practical, deployment-faithful, low-cost framework that integrates pre-flight assay-aware validation with runtime visual monitoring for open-source liquid handlers, outperforming ablations and offering a transparent, open-source solution for safer autonomous experimentation.

Abstract: Self-driving laboratories increasingly rely on low-cost liquid handlers such as the Opentrons OT-2, which ship without the pressure-based aspiration monitoring of Hamilton or Tecan systems and are typically run open-loop. Two failure modes go undetected: protocols that are syntactically valid but violate assay-specific invariants (e.g., tip reuse between a PCR template and a no-template control), and physical execution failures (partial dispense, air bubbles, missing tips) at runtime. We present AEGIS, a two-layer guardian for both. Layer 1 pairs a curated machine-readable assay rule database with an LLM that reasons over OT-2 Python code, reaching an adjusted F1 of 0.97 on a 24-protocol benchmark across five assay families and beating rules-only and LLM-only ablations across five backends; a free open-weight model ties the best proprietary one, so no paid API is required. Layer 2 fits a PCA world model to YOLO-cropped four-frame pipette trajectories; under a leakage-free leave-one-plate-out evaluation it reaches average precision 0.89 and operating-point F1 0.71 (AUROC 0.80), a deployment-faithful number that matches the live demonstration, and we characterize the small-pipette (p20) resolution limit (F1 0.47). A live demonstration on a physical OT-2 (five replicates per condition) catches planted no-tip failures deterministically and partial dispense on coloured dyes, with an always-VLM self-vote gate lifting partial-dispense recall to 5/5; transparent water is a principled limit of any front-view-only monitor, which AEGIS surfaces as low-confidence VLM reasoning rather than a wrong verdict. Cascade triage holds VLM cost near $1.63 per plate versus $10.33 for an always-VLM baseline. AEGIS is open source and, to our knowledge, the first system to unify pre-flight assay-aware validation with runtime visual monitoring for an open-source liquid handler.

</details>


### [161] [Think at 5 Hz, Act at 20 Hz: Asynchronous Fast-Slow Vision-Language-Action Inference for Closed-Loop Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15621)
*Yun Li,Jiachen Gong,Simon Thompson,Ehsan Javanmardi,Qunli Zhang,Zifan Zeng,Shiming Liu,Peng Wang,Zixuan Guo,Manabu Tsukada*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A fast-slow architecture for real-time autonomous driving that uses a slow, frozen vision-language backbone as a scene cache and a fast lightweight action expert for per-tick control, delivering fresh control every 50 ms and strong improvements in route completion, safety, and transferability with modest compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the latency gap when using large language models for end-to-end driving by separating perception/scene reasoning (slow) from reactive control (fast) and ensuring fresh use of observations at high control rates, avoiding the need to replay old commands.

Method: Propose a two-system pipeline: a frozen 7B vision-language backbone (slow) that digests navigation instructions and visual history and exposes a per-layer key-value cache as the scene representation; a lightweight action expert (fast) that, at every simulation tick, attends to the cache and current frame to regress waypoints in a single forward pass. Train the expert with randomized staleness to align with asynchronous execution. Evaluate on LangAuto-Short routes in CARLA, and test zero-shot transfer to unseen towns.

Result: Fresh control at 50 ms ticks; route completion rises from 37.0 to 94.0 versus a frame-skipping baseline. Frame-skip ablation shows the expert contributes to driving score and per-tick freshness boosts completion from 82.1 to 94.0 and reduces red-light violations by ~1/3. Zero-shot transfer to two unseen towns yields 84-94% route completion (baseline 31-41%). Open-loop waypoint error reduced by ~4× compared to the backbone's action head. per-tick computation is ~32 ms on a single consumer GPU and independent of history length.

Conclusion: The fast-slow architecture effectively decouples perception/scene reasoning from reactive control, enabling fresh, stable end-to-end driving with LLM-based components, substantial improvements in route completion and safety, and robust zero-shot transfer with modest compute.

Abstract: Large language models bring instruction following and scene reasoning to end-to-end driving, but their inference latency collides with the control rate a vehicle requires. Existing closed-loop agents hide this gap by invoking the model on alternate simulation ticks and replaying the previous command in between, so half of all control outputs ignore the newest observations. We present a fast-slow architecture that removes this compromise. A frozen 7B vision-language backbone acts as the slow system, digesting navigation instructions and visual history at low frequency while exposing its per-layer key-value cache as a standing representation of the scene. A lightweight action expert acts as the fast system, attending to this cache and to the current camera frame at every simulation tick to regress waypoints in a single forward pass. Since the cache lags behind the world at deployment, we train the expert under randomized staleness, aligning training with asynchronous execution. On LangAuto-Short routes in CARLA, our system produces fresh control at every 50 ms simulation tick and lifts route completion from 37.0 to 94.0 over the frame-skipping baseline. A frame-skip ablation with the same expert separates the two factors at work: the expert raises the driving score on its own, while per-tick freshness raises completion from 82.1 to 94.0 and cuts red-light violations by a third. Trained on a single town, the expert transfers zero-shot to two unseen towns, holding 84-94% route completion where the baseline reaches 31-41%. It reduces open-loop waypoint error by nearly a factor of four compared to the backbone's own action head, at a per-tick model cost of 32 ms that is independent of history length on a single consumer GPU.

</details>


### [162] [Scalable Open-Source Visuotactile Sensor for 6-Axis Contact Wrench Estimation in Tensegrity Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15633)
*Wenzhe Tong,Jonathan Mi,Xili Yi,Nima Fazeli,Xiaonan Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A scalable, open-source visuotactile sensor for tensegrity robots enabling six-axis wrench estimation via a tactile-to-wrench neural network, featuring a gyroid-bonded elastomer–TPU interface and validated on static/dynamic tests and a 12 kg robot; open-source.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable reliable tactile feedback and contact-aware proprioception for tensegrity robots, addressing challenges of modularity, adhesion, and scalability in sensing.

Method: An endcap visuotactile sensor comprising an elastomeric shell, a 3D-printed TPU interface, and a rigid base with an embedded camera and LED ring. A gyroid-infill bonding technique bonds elastomer to TPU without adhesives to create a durable, lightweight, modular interface. A tactile-to-wrench neural network maps shear vector fields to six-dimensional force/torque measurements; system is open-source.

Result: Quantitative: MSE of 0.1531 on static validation data with out-of-domain generalization under dynamic motion. Qualitative: successful full-system integration on a 12 kg tensegrity robot with reliable ground-contact identification.

Conclusion: The work advances tactile feedback for tensegrity robots by delivering a scalable, low-cost, and physically interpretable sensing solution with open-source availability, enabling contact-aware proprioception and state estimation for large-scale structures.

Abstract: This paper presents a scalable, open-source visuotactile sensing system for tensegrity robots that enables six-axis wrench estimation and contact detection. The proposed endcap sensor integrates an elastomeric shell, a 3D-printed thermoplastic polyurethane (TPU) interface, and a rigid base housing an embedded camera and LED illumination ring. A novel gyroid-infill bonding technique is introduced to form a durable elastomer-TPU interface without adhesives, yielding a lightweight and modular design compatible with large-scale tensegrity structures. A tactile-to-wrench neural network maps shear vector fields to six-dimensional force and torque measurements. Experimental results demonstrate accurate and stable wrench estimation with a mean squared error (MSE) of 0.1531 on static validation data and out-of-domain generalization under dynamic motion. Furthermore, full-system integration on a 12 kg tensegrity robot confirms the sensor's ability to reliably identify ground contacts. The system substantially improves the practicality of tactile feedback for tensegrity robots, offering a low-cost, reproducible, and physically interpretable pathway toward contact-aware proprioception and state estimation. Open source files are available at \href{https://github.com/Jonathan-Twz/tensegrity-gelfoot}{github.com/Jonathan-Twz/tensegrity-gelfoot}

</details>


### [163] [IMBench: A Benchmark for Intuitive Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15641)
*Anurag Maurya,Sukhvansh Jain,Prajwal Avhad,Gautham Balachandran,Ziyi Zhou,Atharva Kshirsagar,Satyam Singh,Bowen Li. Rishabh Mukund,Ritul Singh,Jatin Vira,Suvonil Chatterjee,Devesh K. Jha*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: IMBENCH introduces a 35-task benchmark (14K trajectories) to evaluate intuitive manipulation—the integrated capability to perceive, reason about physics, plan actions, and iteratively execute under explicit constraints—revealing gaps in current models' ability to produce executable plans and satisfy constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humans seamlessly integrate perception, physical reasoning, and motor control to solve manipulation tasks and adapt to new scenes and rules. Existing benchmarks separate reasoning from execution or only assess policy performance, leaving intuitive manipulation under-evaluated. IMBENCH seeks to close this gap by evaluating integrated capabilities.

Method: Design of IMBENCH: a benchmark spanning perception, physical reasoning, action generation, and iterative execution. It includes 35 tasks with contact-rich manipulation, tool use, and multi-stage dependencies, plus scalable tools for generating diverse scenarios. The dataset comprises ~14,000 filtered trajectories, enabling evaluation of inference of task-relevant physical structure and generation of feasible action sequences under explicit constraints.

Result: Experiments report a consistent gap: vision–language models show partial physical reasoning but fail to produce executable plans; vision–language–action models struggle to satisfy task constraints and generalize across scenarios. This identifies intuitive manipulation as a missing axis in current foundation models and generalist policies, positioning IMBENCH as a step toward evaluating and enabling more integrated adaptive physical intelligence.

Conclusion: IMBENCH provides an integrated evaluation framework to advance physical intelligence by linking perception, reasoning, and action under realistic constraints; it highlights deficiencies in current models and offers a pathway to develop more capable, adaptable robotic systems.

Abstract: Humans combine reasoning and motor control to solve complex manipulation tasks under diverse constraints. They build an understanding of the physical world that helps them convert reasoning into actions and quickly adapt to new scenes, tasks, and rules. We refer to this capability as intuitive manipulation. Existing benchmarks fail to capture this integration: they evaluate physical reasoning in isolation from execution, or measure policy performance without requiring explicit reasoning. We introduce IMBENCH, a benchmark designed to evaluate intuitive manipulation as an integrated capability spanning perception, physical reasoning, action generation, and iterative execution. Our tasks require models to infer task-relevant physical structure and generate feasible action sequences under explicit constraints, including contact-rich manipulation, tool use, and multi-stage dependencies. We introduce a benchmark of 35 tasks, 14K filtered trajectories, and scalable tools for generating diverse scenarios. Experiments reveal a consistent gap: vision language models show partial physical reasoning ability but fail to produce executable plans, while state-of-the-art vision-language-action models struggle to satisfy task constraints and generalize across scenarios. These results identify intuitive manipulation as a missing axis in current foundation models and generalist robot policies, and position IMBENCH as a step toward evaluating and enabling more integrated, adaptive physical intelligence.

</details>


### [164] [Difference-Based Relational Learning for Zero-Shot Object-Goal Visual Navigation With Direct Sim-to-Real Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15642)
*Guolei Qi,Feitian Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces Temporal Difference-Relational Network (T-DRN) for robust zero-shot sim-to-real object-goal navigation by learning domain-invariant relational features and leveraging a dual-frame temporal buffer, with validation in AI2-THOR and on a real robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-shot DRL navigation struggles with the sim-to-real gap due to object appearance variation and narrow camera FoV; there is a need for domain-invariant representations and temporal continuity to maintain object identity across frames.

Method: Proposes a Siamese difference-based feature extractor to compute relational differences between the target object and observed scene, producing domain-independent representations, combined with a dual-frame temporal buffer that preserves short-term object continuity under narrow FoV; end-to-end DRL framework trained for object-goal navigation; evaluated in AI2-THOR and on a physical wheeled robot.

Result: T-DRN yields improved zero-shot generalization (higher success rates) compared to strong baselines in simulation; robust performance demonstrated on a real robot, indicating feasible direct sim-to-real transfer under real sensing and actuation constraints.

Conclusion: The Temporal Difference-Relational Network offers a robust approach to bridging the sim-to-real gap in zero-shot object-goal navigation by combining domain-invariant relational features with short-term temporal continuity, validated both in simulation and on a real robot, suggesting broad applicability to real-world robotics.

Abstract: End-to-end deep reinforcement learning (DRL) for zero-shot object-goal visual navigation remains challenged by the sim-to-real gap, particularly variations in object appearance and restricted camera field-of-view (FoV). This letter proposes a Temporal Difference-Relational Network (T-DRN) for robust zero-shot sim-to-real transfer. T-DRN combines a Siamese difference-based feature extractor, which computes relational difference between the target and observed objects to produce domain-independent representations, with a dual-frame temporal buffer that preserves short-term object continuity under narrow FoV. Extensive experiments in AI2-THOR demonstrate that T-DRN improves zero-shot generalization in terms of success rates over strong baselines. Furthermore, T-DRN is systematically validated on a physical wheeled robot, demonstrating robust performance under real sensing and actuation constraints and supporting the feasibility of direct sim-to-real transfer.

</details>


### [165] [Learning a System-Level Surrogate for Hydraulic Excavators: A Simulation-to-Real LSTM Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15656)
*Shuai Wang,Shen Wang,Qiang Wang,Muguo Du,Donghai Shi,Chenyu Wang,Xiaofeng Tao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A simulation-to-real framework using LSTM to learn a system-level surrogate of an autonomous excavator's closed-loop dynamics, validated in MuJoCo and on real hardware, with adaptive Kalman-filter state estimation to handle noisy measurements; surrogate achieves high fidelity in angular velocity and long-horizon trajectories and can act as a drop-in model for simulation and physical systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome limited access to physical machines and high cost of real-world experimentation in excavation automation; enable scalable development via a data-driven surrogate.

Method: Treat excavator as an input-output operator. Train LSTM surrogate to reproduce closed-loop behavior under identical control inputs. Validate in MuJoCo, transfer to real excavator. Use adaptive Kalman filtering for consistency-aware state estimation to mitigate measurement inconsistencies.

Result: High-fidelity reproduction of angular velocity and long-horizon trajectories under closed-loop autoregressive evaluation; successful simulation-to-real transfer; surrogate suitable as a drop-in for both simulation and physical systems.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a scalable, efficient path for developing excavation automation algorithms by learning a system-level surrogate that can operate across simulation and real hardware.

Abstract: Developing autonomous hydraulic excavators is constrained by limited access to physical machines and the high cost of real-world experimentation. This paper proposes a simulation-to-real framework for learning a system-level digital surrogate using Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks. Instead of modeling internal dynamics, the excavator is treated as an input-output operator, and the surrogate is trained to reproduce its closed-loop behavior under identical control inputs. The approach is first validated in a MuJoCo simulation environment and then transferred to a real excavator. To address measurement inconsistencies in real-world data, a consistency-aware state estimation method based on adaptive Kalman filtering is introduced. Experimental results demonstrate that the learned surrogate achieves high fidelity in both angular velocity and long-horizon trajectory reproduction under closed-loop autoregressive evaluation. These results confirm that the proposed model can serve as a drop-in surrogate for both simulation and physical systems, enabling scalable and efficient development of excavation automation algorithms.

</details>


### [166] [Continuously Stable Structure through Plastic Deformation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15659)
*Junlong Xiao,Yaoqiang Pan,Xuan Zhang,Michael Yu Wang,Chao Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CSSPD yields a continuously stable soft gripper via plastic deformation and kirigami stiffening, enabling energy-free passive holding and dynamic stability; bioinspired paw pad and sensing enable rapid grasping; demonstrates 16 N passive grip, stability under 400 m/s^2, and passive perching, rivaling pneumatic actuation at 0.3 MPa.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the stability-compliance trade-off in soft robots, especially soft grippers, under dynamic disturbances and accelerations in real-world tasks.

Method: Integrate a plastic-deformation-based stable structure (CSSPD) into a soft gripper; employ a kirigami-patterned metal layer for tunable stiffness; develop a bioinspired paw pad; formulate a mathematical model and optimize the kirigami geometry; validate via experiments including passive holding force, dynamic stability under pulsed accelerations, pneumatic-actuation comparison, and passive perching tests.

Result: The gripper achieves a passive holding force of up to 16 N without energy input; performance is comparable to pneumatic actuation at 0.3 MPa; remains stable under pulsed accelerations up to 400 m/s^2; can passively perch on tree branches for extended periods without power.

Conclusion: CSSPD enables energy-free continuous stability in soft grippers and, with bioinspired sensing-enhanced paw pads, supports rapid, robust grasping; promising for mobile robotics; further work should address durability, fatigue under cyclic loading, reconfigurability, and sensing integration.

Abstract: Soft robots have seen widespread adoption in interactive tasks due to their inherent compliance and adaptability. However, these advantages often come at the cost of stability, posing challenges in a dynamic environment. This limitation is especially critical in soft grippers, where instability under acceleration or external disturbances can result in grasp failure. In this study, we present a continuously stable structure through plastic deformation (CSSPD), integrated into a soft gripper. By leveraging the mechanism of plastic deformation, the gripper maintains continuous configurations without energy input, while the added stiffness ensures both static and dynamic stability. We introduce a bioinspired paw pad that significantly enhances stability and enables sensing-based rapid object grasping. Then we develop the mathematical model and optimize the kirigami structure of the metal layer. Experimental results show that the gripper can sustain a passive holding force of up to 16 N without energy input, achieving performance comparable to pneumatic actuation at 0.3 MPa. When combined with pneumatic actuation, it remains stable under pulsed accelerations of up to 400 m/s^2. It can also passively perch on tree branches for extended periods without power, demonstrating promise for mobile robotic applications.

</details>


### [167] [A Generative Partially Specified Finite State Machine Approach to Complex Behaviour Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15674)
*Kalana Ratnayake,Michael Pritchard,David Hinwood,Maleen Jayasuriya,Damith Herath*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A novel GPSFSM neurosymbolic architecture enables generative, interpretable behavior planning for robots by leveraging FSM structure and LLMs; paired with a ROS2 stack (Fabric, Capabilities2, PromptTools) for asynchronous, configurable execution. Demonstrates higher plan-generation success than BTGenBot, especially zero-shot, with competitive latency; open-source implementation released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots operating in dynamic environments require planning that is reactive, interpretable, and adaptable. While LLMs have been used with Behaviour Trees, FSMs—though efficient and widespread—have not been leveraged for generative planning. The work aims to combine the interpretability and structure of FSMs with generative capabilities to improve robustness and reusability in robotics, and to provide robot-agnostic semantic capability descriptions.

Method: Introduce Generative Partially Specified FSM (GPSFSM) architecture and Fabric FSM engine to parse/validate/execute plans with control structures: Sequential, Recovery, Parallel-Any, Parallel-All. Extend ROS2 Capabilities2 with an asynchronous event system for behavior chaining and runtime parameter injection. Provide PromptTools for a ROS 2 interface to local/cloud LLMs with prompt buffering for dynamic, asynchronous task/context composition. Evaluate on navigation tasks, comparing plan-generation success rates and latency against BTGenBot and frontier LLMs; demonstrate capability to generate complex behaviours; release open-source ROS2 stack.

Result: GPSFSM achieves consistently higher plan-generation success rates than BTGenBot, especially in zero-shot scenarios where BTs struggle; planning latency is comparable to or lower than frontier LLMs; demonstrates generation of complex behaviours; open-source ROS2 stack released for reproducibility and practical adoption.

Conclusion: The GPSFSM framework advances generative planning for robotics by marrying symbolic FSM semantics with generative models, delivering interpretable, adaptable behavior planning with reactivity. The accompanying ROS2 stack enables practical deployment, and the approach shows promise in zero-shot and complex-task scenarios. Future work could explore scalability to larger task sets, more diverse environments, and deeper integration with other planning paradigms.

Abstract: Autonomous robots operating in dynamic environments require behaviour planning systems that combine reactivity, interpretability, and adaptability. While Large Language Models have been successfully integrated with Behaviour Trees for dynamic replanning, Finite State Machines, despite their widespread adoption and computational efficiency, remain unexplored for generative approaches. We propose a Generative Partially Specified Finite State Machine (GPSFSM) neurosymbolic architecture that utilises the symbolic and semantic structure of FSMs, including states and event-triggered transitions, to implement Behaviour Planning. This paper introduces the first GPSFSM framework for robotics, featuring Fabric, an FSM engine that parses, validates, and executes behaviour plans that contain Sequential, Recovery, Parallel-Any, and Parallel-All control structures. We extend the Capabilities2 package in ROS2 with an asynchronous event system for behaviour chaining and runtime parameter injection for configurable execution, addressing the ad-hoc function representations that limit current generative systems. PromptTools provides a unified ROS 2 interface to local and cloud LLMs, with prompt buffering, enabling dynamic asynchronous composition of task and context information. Together, these components enable standardised semantic capability descriptions for robot-agnostic development. Experimental evaluation on navigation tasks demonstrates that our GPSFSM approach achieves consistently higher plan-generation success rates than the state-of-the-art BTGenBot system, particularly excelling in zero-shot scenarios where BTs typically struggle, while maintaining comparable or lower planning latency to frontier LLMs. We also demonstrate that our system can generate complex behaviours. We release an open-source ROS2 stack that makes generative FSM planning practical and reproducible for robotic systems.

</details>


### [168] [RAVEN: Reinforcement-Adaptive Visibility-Graph Planning for Robust Humanoid Navigation with Collision-Free MPC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15701)
*Ruochen Hou,Shiqi Wang,Beom Jun Kim,Hanzhang Fang,Mehak Singal,Dennis W. Hong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RAVEN uses reinforcement learning to adapt obstacle inflation in a visibility-graph planner with an MPC tracker, yielding robust humanoid navigation under delays and sensing noise, without full end-to-end learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon planning with safety constraints is essential for dynamic humanoid navigation; classical visibility-graph + MPC is efficient but sensitive to model errors, control delays, and state-estimation noise, requiring manual tuning.

Method: A hierarchical RL–MPC framework where RL adjusts the geometric construction of the visibility-graph (e.g., obstacle inflation and related parameters) to reshape free-space geometry and alter global path topology; a collision-free MPC layer tracks the planned trajectory under velocity bounds and obstacle avoidance; training uses realistic delays and observation noise to learn robust planning adaptations while preserving explicit planning and constrained optimization.

Result: Compared to a manually tuned visibility-graph MPC baseline and a pure RL policy, RAVEN reduces overshoot near obstacles, improves robustness in narrow passages, and yields more reliable navigation under delay and observation noise.

Conclusion: Reinforcement-adaptive graph construction combined with constrained MPC provides an effective, interpretable alternative to end-to-end learning for robust humanoid navigation.

Abstract: Humanoid navigation in dynamic environments requires long-horizon planning while respecting short-horizon dynamic and safety constraints. Classical visibility-graph planners combined with model predictive control (MPC) can efficiently generate collision-free trajectories, but their performance depends on manually tuned parameters and accurate system modeling. In real robotic systems, control delays, state-estimation noise, and locomotion uncertainties can cause overshoot and constraint violations even when the nominal path is geometrically optimal. We propose RAVEN, a hierarchical reinforcement learning (RL)-MPC framework for robust humanoid navigation. Unlike prior approaches that use learning to tune cost weights or replace planning entirely, RAVEN employs RL to adapt the geometric construction of a visibility-graph planner by modifying obstacle inflation and related graph parameters. By directly reshaping the free-space geometry, the learned planner alters the topology of the global path to compensate for delay and tracking imperfections. A collision-free MPC layer then tracks the planned trajectory while explicitly enforcing velocity bounds and obstacle-avoidance constraints. By training under realistic delays and observation noise, RAVEN learns planning adaptations that improve robustness while retaining explicit long-horizon geometric planning and constrained optimization, in contrast to end-to-end learning approaches. We evaluate RAVEN against a manually tuned visibility-graph MPC baseline and a pure RL navigation policy. Results demonstrate reduced overshoot near obstacles, improved robustness in narrow passages, and more reliable navigation under delay and noise. These findings indicate that reinforcement-adaptive graph construction combined with constrained MPC provides an effective and interpretable alternative to end-to-end learning for robust humanoid navigation.

</details>


### [169] [Implicit Virtual Leader: Decentralized Vision-Only Relative Pose Estimation for Multi-Robot Formations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15708)
*Shiyuan Yang,Zelin Wang,Zhijia Tao,Yilin Wang,Zhengyu Hou,Xiaosong Kong,Borong Zhang,Yip Fun Yeung,Yuankai Luo,Sharon Lee,Qingbiao Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Vision-only, decentralized formation control using Graph Neural Networks with an implicit virtual leader (IVL) learned from monocular images and inter-robot communication; includes uncertainty estimation (aleatoric via heteroscedastic GNLL and epistemic via MC Dropout); outperforms traditional GPS-dependent methods while generalizing across heterogeneous platforms and formation sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Eliminate single points of failure and error propagation in classical leader-follower formations, especially in GPS-denied environments that rely on absolute localization sensors.

Method: A fully decentralized framework that uses Graph Neural Networks to implicitly learn a virtual leader frame (IVL) from monocular vision and inter-robot communication. The model includes a heteroscedastic GNLL head for Aleatoric uncertainty and employs MC Dropout for Epistemic uncertainty. Systematic evaluation is conducted on both simulation and real-world datasets.

Result: Competitive pose estimation accuracy. Natural generalization to heterogeneous robot platforms and varying formation sizes. Demonstrated robustness through uncertainty estimation and cross-domain evaluation.

Conclusion: The proposed vision-only, decentralized approach with an implicitly learned IVL and uncertainty quantification provides robust relative pose estimation for multi-robot formations, enabling flexible, GPS-denied operation without reliance on a physical leader.

Abstract: Classical leader-follower formation control suffers from single points of failure and error propagation, and relies on absolute localization sensors that are ill-suited for GPS-denied environments. We address these limitations by introducing a fully decentralized, vision-only relative pose estimation framework based on Graph Neural Networks (GNNs). The key idea is the implicit virtual leader (IVL): a non-physical formation reference frame that is not tied to any individual robot but is implicitly learned within the GNN using only monocular images and inter-robot communication. We attach a heteroscedastic GNLL head for aleatoric uncertainty and MC~Dropout for epistemic uncertainty, and conduct a systematic comparison across simulation and real-world test sets. Our framework achieves competitive pose estimation accuracy and generalizes naturally to heterogeneous robot platforms and varying formation sizes.

</details>


### [170] [AC-VLA: Robust Out-of-Distribution Action Execution via Compositional Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15714)
*Xiaojiang Peng,Kai Peng,Jie Lu,Zheng Lian,Zitong YU,Xiaobo Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AC-VLA introduces a plug-and-play compositional learning framework for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models to improve compositional out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization. It combines an LLM-driven instruction decomposer with a proprioceptive trajectory aligner to create dense sub-task supervision and mixed training on full demonstrations and decomposed data. It also employs a state-conditioned asymmetric masking strategy that suppresses wrist-view inputs during closed-gripper phases to enforce global semantic grounding. The framework is architecture-agnostic and can be integrated into any VLA backbone. Instantiated on π0.5 and evaluated on LIBERO and LIBERO-OOD, AC-VLA yields approximately 28 percentage points absolute improvement on compositional OOD tasks while preserving near-perfect in-distribution performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models excel at end-to-end robotic manipulation but struggle with out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization when familiar sub-tasks are recombined in unseen configurations. The authors identify two mutually reinforcing failure modes: trajectory overfitting (overfitting to holistic trajectory patterns rather than sub-skill semantics) and perceptual shortcut (action tokens over-rely on wrist-view textures at the expense of global spatial grounding). These motivate a compositional, architecture-agnostic remedy that can be plugged into existing VLA systems.

Method: Two architecture-agnostic components. (i) Compositional learning module: an LLM-driven instruction decomposer and a proprioceptive trajectory aligner generate dense sub-task supervision. The model is trained with a mixture of complete demonstrations and decomposed data to incentivize compositional generalization. (ii) State-conditioned asymmetric masking: during closed-gripper phases, wrist-view inputs are suppressed to force reliance on global spatial grounding rather than local textures. The approach is designed to be integrated without modifying the VLA backbone and was instantiated with the π0.5 backbone, evaluated on LIBERO and LIBERO-OOD.

Result: AC-VLA achieves ~28% absolute improvement on compositional OOD tasks on LIBERO/LIBERO-OOD benchmarks while maintaining near-perfect in-distribution performance for the π0.5 backbone.

Conclusion: AC-VLA provides a practical, plug-and-play pathway to improve compositional generalization in VLA models without architectural changes. Its two components address core failure modes—trajectory overfitting and perceptual shortcut—through dense sub-task supervision and global grounding. The results suggest robust gains on compositional OOD with minimal impact on ID accuracy, though dependences on LLM-derived decompositions and masking strategies could influence performance across backbones and tasks.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models excel at end-to-end robotic manipulation but struggle with out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization when familiar sub-tasks are recombined in unseen configurations. We identify two mutually reinforcing failure modes: \emph{trajectory overfitting}, where models overfit to holistic trajectory patterns rather than compositional sub-skill semantics; and \emph{perceptual shortcut}, where action tokens over-rely on wrist-view textures at the expense of global spatial grounding. To address both, we introduce \textbf{AC-VLA}, a plug-and-play Action Compositional learning framework comprising two architecture-agnostic components: \textbf{(i)} a compositional learning module that uses an LLM-driven instruction decomposer and a proprioceptive trajectory aligner to generate dense sub-task supervision, followed by mixed training on complete demonstrations and decomposed data to endow the model with compositional generalization; and \textbf{(ii)} a state-conditioned asymmetric masking strategy that suppresses wrist-view inputs during closed-gripper phases, enforcing global semantic grounding. All components are architectural modification-free and directly integrable into any VLA backbone. Instantiated on $π_{0.5}$ and evaluated on LIBERO and LIBERO-OOD benchmarks, AC-VLA achieves a ~28% absolute improvement on compositional OOD tasks while maintaining near-perfect in-distribution performance.

</details>


### [171] [A Task-Space Receding Horizon Controller for Fast Collision Avoidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15733)
*Mattia Penzotti,Marco Controzzi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A task-space receding-horizon controller with a short contact-consistent rollout to generate a non-penetration terminal reference and a single-input smooth minimum-acceleration move toward that reference, enabling real-time collision avoidance in dynamic clutter; demonstrates stability and sim-to-real performance with 40-DOF and 6-DOF experiments and favorable comparisons to MPC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time collision avoidance for manipulators requires fast responses with lookahead; full MPC is expensive; horizon-free methods lack foresight in dynamic clutter.

Method: Initialize from a closed-loop inverse-kinematics regulation law; perform a contact-consistent rollout using an iterative dynamics solver on inflated convex robot/obstacle geometries to shape a terminal non-penetration reference; compute only the first input of a smooth minimum-acceleration transition toward that reference; analyze contact-inactive closed-loop and updates for moving obstacles; embodiments of moving obstacles; simulate on 40-DOF; hardware on 6-DOF; compare with dynamic optimization and MPC baselines.

Result: Demonstrates intermediate horizons balance anticipation and responsiveness; sim-to-real consistency without accurate inertial parameter estimation; improved success rates in dynamic clutter; real-time solve times preserved across tested regimes; favorable comparisons to baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed approach yields real-time, foresightful collision avoidance with non-penetration guarantees in dynamic clutter while being computationally efficient and scalable, validated by high-DOF simulations and hardware experiments.

Abstract: Real-time collision avoidance for robotic manipulators requires fast reactions to unexpected obstacle motion and lookahead to avoid becoming trapped by near-future constraints. Full model predictive control can provide this foresight, but its online cost may grow quickly with horizon length, model fidelity, and the number of active geometric constraints. Conversely, horizon-free reactive methods are computationally efficient but can be short-sighted in dynamic clutter. We present a task-space receding-horizon controller that uses a short contact-consistent rollout to generate a terminal kinematic reference satisfying internal non-penetration constraints, then computes only the first input of a smooth minimum-acceleration transition toward that reference. Starting from a closed-loop inverse-kinematics regulation law, the rollout is performed with an iterative dynamics solver operating on inflated convex robot and obstacle geometries, so that robot-obstacle contacts, dynamic obstacle motion, and self-collisions can shape the terminal reference without requiring full constrained trajectory optimization. We analyze the contact-inactive closed loop and show local exponential task-space regulation under standard regularity assumptions.
