For contacts activated inside the rollout, we characterize the corresponding discrete updates and bound the effect of moving obstacles on regular operating sets. Simulations on a 40-DOF multi-chain system show that intermediate horizons balance anticipation, responsiveness, and computational cost. Hardware experiments on a 6-DOF platform demonstrate consistent sim-to-real behavior without accurate inertial parameter estimation, and comparisons against dynamic optimization fabrics and model predictive control (MPC) baselines show improved success rates in dynamic clutter while preserving solve times compatible with real-time execution in the tested regimes.

</details>


### [172] [Towards Artificial Nerves: Biomimetic Optical-Fiber Tactile Sensing for Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15746)
*Laura E. Butcher,Chris J. Ford,Nathan F. Lepora,Efi Psomopoulou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: OptiTac is a biomimetic tactile sensor that maps mechanical pins in soft skin to optical fibers acting as artificial nerves, enabling high-resolution, distributed tactile sensing with interpretable, image-like processing to infer contact location, size, and shape.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the demand for tactile sensing with human-like adaptability and resolution to enable dexterous manipulation and safe robot–human interaction, while maintaining interpretability and scalable distribution of sensors.

Method: A soft skin with mechanical pins is paired each with an optical fiber serving as an artificial nerve. Tactile information is routed away from the sensing surface while preserving spatial resolution. Tactile data are treated as images, enabling simple analytical (non-deep-learning) methods to infer contact location, size, and shape.

Result: Demonstrates a architectural principle for routing tactile information away from the sensing surface without loss of spatial resolution, enabling interpretable and scalable tactile intelligence and supporting distributed tactile systems for future robots.

Conclusion: OptiTac establishes an artificial nerve-inspired sensing framework for interpretable robotic touch and outlines a scalable route toward distributed tactile systems and human-like tactile perception in next-generation robotic platforms.

Abstract: Robotic systems increasingly demand tactile sensing that approaches the adaptability and resolution of human skin to enable dexterous manipulation and safe interaction. OptiTac is a biomimetic tactile sensor that emulates the mechanoreceptor-to-nerve architecture of human touch by pairing each mechanical pin on a soft skin with an optical fiber acting as an artificial nerve. This design demonstrates an architectural principle for routing tactile information away from the sensing surface while preserving high spatial resolution, establishing a practical route toward distributed tactile sensing in future robotic systems. By treating tactile signals as images, simple analytical methods, rather than opaque deep-learning models, are used to infer contact location, size, and shape, providing interpretable and scalable tactile intelligence. This work demonstrates how evolutionary principles from biology can guide the development of artificial nerve systems for robots, offering a pathway toward human-like tactile perception in next-generation robotic platforms. More broadly, OptiTac establishes an artificial nerve-inspired sensing framework for interpretable robotic touch and a scalable route toward future distributed tactile systems.

</details>


### [173] [SkillNav: Score-Level Skill Intervention for Zero-Shot Object Goal Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15758)
*Ruijie Sang,Yiqun Duan,Pinhan Fu,Ruilin Wang,Wei Sui,Xianda Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SkillNav introduces a training-free, hierarchical skill framework for VLM-based navigation that writes to a writable curiosity-value map, enabling composable, zero-cost behavioral memory and priorities across three skill tiers, yielding state-of-the-art SPL and high success rates on MP3D and HM3D.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcomes single-frame reasoning and the need for token-heavy prompts by enabling cross-step behavioral awareness. Addresses spatial signal encoding (angles, map cells, coordinates) and provides a path for continual improvement without retraining.

Method: Three-tier skill system (soft scaling, lower-bound boost, hard override) writes into the navigator's curiosity-value map. Skills cooperate in a fixed composition order, enabling predictable priority. New skills can be registered without retraining the VLM. A minimal prompt channel provides category-level semantic hints, creating a dual-representation: spatial memory on the map and semantic memory in prompts.

Result: Training-free approach achieves new state-of-the-art SPL on MP3D (25.5), HM3D v0.1 (39.3), and HM3D v0.2 (43.2), with up to 6.0 absolute SPL improvement over the strongest prior method, and the highest success rate on HM3D v0.1 (69.7) and v0.2 (75.9).

Conclusion: SkillNav enables continual behavioral refinement via plug-in skills without retraining, normalizing cross-step navigation behavior, reducing token load, and delivering strong, scalable performance across multiple embodied navigation benchmarks.

Abstract: Vision-Language Model (VLM) agents have advanced zero-shot object-goal navigation, yet single-frame reasoning leaves them without the cross-step behavioral awareness an embodied navigator requires, producing recurring failures such as dead-end stalls, in-room loops, and circuitous approaches to detected targets. Prompt-based remedies inflate token budgets across multi-submodule episodes and still struggle to encode inherently spatial signals such as angles, map cells, and viewpoint coordinates. In this paper, we propose SkillNav, an extensible behavioral skill framework for VLM-based navigation that treats the curiosity value map already maintained by modern VLM navigators as a writable substrate on which composable skills inscribe behavioral memory at zero token cost. Skills are stratified into three tiers by their level of behavioral authority, namely soft scaling for proportional reweighting, lower-bound boost for region-level guarantees, and hard override for threshold-triggered forced actions, and cooperate across tiers under a fixed composition order that establishes a predictable, declared priority among skills. This design turns capability improvement into skill registration: new behaviors plug in without retraining the VLM or disturbing existing skills, opening a path for continual refinement. A minimal prompt channel complements the score-level skills with category-level semantic hints, yielding a dual-representation design in which spatial memory lives on the map and semantic memory in short prompts. Training-free, SkillNav establishes new state-of-the-art SPL across MP3D (25.5), HM3D v0.1 (39.3), and HM3D v0.2 (43.2), improving SPL by up to 6.0 absolute over the strongest prior method, and achieves the highest Success Rate on HM3D v0.1 (69.7) and v0.2 (75.9).

</details>


### [174] [Deployment-Ready UWB Localization for Industrial Ground Robots with Automatic Anchor Calibration and Terrain-Aware Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15807)
*Alexander Raab,Giulio Delama,Roland Jung,Stephan Weiss*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An end-to-end UWB localization pipeline for AMRs that auto-calibrates anchor positions and biases, and fuses UWB with proprioception via a bias-aware EKF, enabling robust indoor-outdoor AMR localization with reduced manual setup and cross-platform transferability; dataset released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Calibrating static anchors for UWB is labor-intensive and error-prone, and fusing UWB with onboard sensors for robust pose estimation is challenging, especially with limited LOS and sparse anchor coverage. An integrated, low-tuning solution is desired for industrial AMRs using pose priors.

Method: Develop an automatic anchor calibration stage to estimate anchor positions and range biases, followed by a localization stage that fuses UWB measurements with proprioceptive sensing in a bias-aware Extended Kalman Filter. The approach is designed to plug into existing AMR stacks and relies on surface-bound motion with initial pose priors. Evaluations include a warehouse-based logistics AMR and an independent forklift dataset to assess transferability and robustness under limited LOS and sparse anchor coverage.

Result: The method achieves accurate indoor positioning and smooth outdoor transitions, with improved pose consistency compared to an earlier estimator formulation. It demonstrates transferability to another platform and maintains performance with limited LOS and sparse anchors. The authors also release a warehouse dataset publicly.

Conclusion: Automatic anchor calibration plus bias-aware multimodal fusion enables reliable, accurate UWB-based localization for industrial AMRs with substantially reduced manual effort, and the approach generalizes across platforms and scenarios.

Abstract: Ultra-Wideband (UWB) ranging has become a viable option for industrial Autonomous Mobile Robot (AMR) localization due to improved accuracy and low cost. However, real-world deployments remain limited by two recurring challenges: calibrating static anchors can be time-consuming and error-prone, and integrating UWB with existing onboard sensors requires careful design to ensure robust and consistent pose estimation. Addressing these challenges, this paper presents an end-to-end pipeline that combines automatic anchor calibration with a generic multi-sensor estimator tailored to surface-bound vehicle motion. It targets existing AMR stacks in scenarios where robot pose priors are available for initialization. The calibration stage estimates anchor positions and range biases, while the localization stage fuses UWB with proprioceptive sensing in a bias-aware Extended Kalman Filter to improve consistency without extensive parameter tuning. Experiments on a commercial logistics AMR in a warehouse setting demonstrate accurate positioning indoors and across outdoor transitions, with improved consistency compared to an earlier estimator formulation. Evaluation on an independent forklift dataset further indicates transferability to other platforms. The method remains effective in test cases with limited line-of-sight and sparse anchor coverage. These results show that UWB localization can be deployed with substantially reduced manual effort while preserving the accuracy required for industrial AMRs. The collected warehouse dataset is made publicly available.

</details>


### [175] [Beyond Frontiers: Scene-Anomaly Guided Autonomous Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15828)
*Akash Kumbar,Abhinav Raundhal,Madhava Krishna*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SCAGE reframes exploration as geometric anomaly minimization to improve 3D mapping in unknown indoor environments, yielding high coverage and reconstruction quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional coverage-maximizing exploration ignores structural priors, causing inefficient trajectories and lower fidelity reconstructions. A framework that leverages learned indoor architecture priors can guide exploration toward region-level anomalies to improve mapping.

Method: Operates on unstructured 3D point clouds. Learns priors of standard indoor architecture; continuously compares live observations to priors to detect anomalies (e.g., fragmented walls, partial tables). Anomalies guide the robot to investigate from optimal viewpoints, targeting poorly reconstructed regions to enhance both coverage and mapping fidelity.

Result: Achieved superior volumetric coverage (~90% in all scenes) and higher 3D reconstruction quality compared with state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: Anomaly-guided exploration effectively couples spatial discovery with high-fidelity mapping, demonstrating the value of structural priors in autonomous exploration.

Abstract: Autonomous exploration of unknown 3D environments is traditionally driven by coverage-maximizing geometric heuristics. However, these methods typically determine exploration targets without considering the underlying structural context. This leads to inefficient trajectories often limiting the fidelity of the final 3D reconstruction. To bridge the gap between spatial coverage and reconstruction quality, we introduce a novel paradigm: reframing exploration as a geometric anomaly minimization problem. We present SCAGE: SCene Anomaly Guided Exploration, a novel autonomous exploration framework that operates directly on unstructured 3D point clouds. Instead of blindly chasing volumetric boundaries, we equip the robot with a foundational understanding of standard indoor architecture. As the robot navigates, it continuously evaluates its live 3D observations against these learned expectations. When the incoming geometry contradicts the learned priors of a typical indoor environment, such as a fragmented wall or a partial table, the system flags these regions as scene anomalies. These geometric inconsistencies act as a guiding signal, naturally drawing the robot to investigate and resolve these structural anomalies from optimal vantage points. By actively targeting poorly reconstructed regions rather than just empty space, our approach seamlessly couples spatial discovery with high-fidelity mapping. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that SCAGE achieves superior volumetric coverage (~90% in all scenes) and higher 3D reconstruction quality compared to state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [176] [Minimum Time Dubins Airplane Paths with Asymmetric Climb Rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15863)
*Jaeyoung Lim,Giuseppe Loianno*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Asymmetric Dubins airplane paths replace symmetric climb constraints with asymmetric climb rates, preserving time-optimality and yielding substantial performance gains in time-to-connectivity and planning feasibility, demonstrated by a simulated reduction in min time (71%) and faster median planning times (2.8×), plus a real-world flight validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the sub-optimality and conservatism caused by symmetric climb rate constraints in traditional Dubins airplane paths, enabling closer-to-vehicle performance and more feasible planning on rugged terrain.

Method: Introduce asymmetric climb rate constraints into the Dubins airplane model, recalculate time-optimality conditions, prove that optimality is preserved under asymmetry, and integrate the model into planning (e.g., sampling-based methods) with validation on random state connections and a real flight.

Result: Demonstrated large gains: up to 71% reduction in minimum time for connecting randomly generated states; 2.8× faster median solution time when used in rugged-terrain planning; successful real-world flight validation.

Conclusion: Asymmetric climb rates enhance both the theoretical optimality and practical performance of Dubins airplane paths, making them more capable and readily applicable to real-world fixed-wing flight planning.

Abstract: Dubins airplane paths approximate the limited maneuverability of fixed-wing vehicles with minimum curvature and climb rate constraints. However, the symmetric climb rate constraints result in sub-optimal paths and conservative vehicle performance. In this work, we propose asymmetric Dubins airplane paths, which consider asymmetric climb rates for climbing and descending. We revisit the time optimality conditions and show that the asymmetric flight path angle constraints preserve optimality. We show that by considering asymmetric climb rates, we can take advantage of full performance of the vehicle, reducing the minimum time by 71% for connecting randomly generated states. We also demonstrate that the added climb rate results in 2.8 times faster to find the median solution time when integrated into a sampling-based planning task on rugged terrain, due to the added feasibility. We further demonstrate the practicality of the approach with a real-world flight.

</details>


### [177] [Dynamics-Aware Meta-Imitation for Generalization to Unseen Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15880)
*Zhenduo Shang,Xiyao Liu,Bohan Li,Xudong Wang,Teng Ren,Lianqing Liu,Zhi Han*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DAMI is a dynamics-aware meta-imitation framework integrating meta-learning, a Visual-Motor Trajectory module, U2T fusion for multimodal inputs, and a task-conditioned feature modulation to generalize to unseen tasks via few-shot adaptation, trained on random complete reference demos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation learning suffers from data scarcity and poor generalization to unseen tasks; need a method that captures intrinsic task dynamics and leverages multimodal observations for rapid adaptation.

Method: Proposes a DAMI framework: meta-learning to construct a shared skill space; VMT module for spatio-temporal dynamics in latent space; U2T block for unpaired multimodal fusion; TCFM to modulate low-level 3D features; learning from random complete reference demos to capture underlying task logic.

Result: Demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on seen tasks and strong few-shot adaptation to unseen tasks in both simulation and real-world settings, outperforming baselines.

Conclusion: DAMI advances imitation learning generalization by modeling intrinsic dynamics and multimodal cues to enable rapid adaptation to novel tasks with minimal data.

Abstract: Imitation Learning aims to learn skills from extensive observations and demonstrations for robots, so it suffers from data scarcity and environment generalization. The existing methods predominantly focus on imitation from in-domain tasks and consequently struggle with generalization to unseen tasks. To bridge this generalization gap, we propose the \textbf{D}ynamics-\textbf{A}ware \textbf{M}eta-\textbf{I}mitation (DAMI) framework. By integrating meta-learning to construct a shared skill space, DAMI equips agents for rapid adaptation to novel tasks. We introduce the Visual-Motor Trajectory (VMT) module to capture complex spatio-temporal dynamics within the task latent space. Furthermore, we propose the Unpaired Unified Task (U2T) block to fuse unstructured multimodal observations. To coordinate these representations, we integrate a Task-Conditioned Feature Modulation (TCFM) mechanism customized for modulating low-level 3D features. By capturing intrinsic dynamics from a random complete reference demonstration, our framework learns the underlying task logic rather than memorizing static cues, ensuring effective generalization. Extensive experiments in both simulation and real-world settings demonstrate that our approach outperforms state-of-the-art baselines regarding direct inference on seen tasks and adaptation to unseen tasks via few-shot fine-tuning.

</details>


### [178] [Learning Reach-Avoid Task with Reinforcement Learning: Vectorized Simulation and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15935)
*Jonas Weihing,Shahram Eivazi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A realistic DRL benchmark for robotic-arm reach-avoid tasks using MuJoCo MJX and Brax, achieving high but varying success rates across UR5e and Franka Emika, and highlighting DRL limits in realistic settings; provides open-source benchmark and environment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether deep reinforcement learning can effectively learn reach-avoid policies in complex, realistic robotic environments beyond simplified tabletop scenarios, and establish a standardized benchmark.

Method: Develop a diverse, realistic reach-avoid benchmark using MuJoCo MJX; parallelize simulation and DRL algorithms with Brax; evaluate on UR5e and Franka Emika arms; report both reach and static reach-avoid performance.

Result: State-of-the-art success rates: 96.1% (UR5e) and 98.8% (Franka) for reach; 86.8% (UR5e) and 95.2% (Franka) for static reach-avoid. Demonstrates DRL performance drop in realistic settings and provides open-source environment and benchmarking code.

Conclusion: DRL alone may not reliably solve real-world reach-avoid tasks; further research is needed. The benchmark offers a standardized platform for robust evaluation and progress in this area.

Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) has a longstanding tradition in addressing the reach-avoid task problem, especially for controlling robotic arms. While this task serves as a baseline environment within the research community, the ability of DRL to effectively learn the each-avoid task in complex and realistic scenarios beyond simplified and restricted tabletop settings remains uncertain. In this paper, we present, for the first time, a comprehensive benchmark for the reachavoid task that accurately captures real-world complexities without simplifications. We demonstrate a diverse range of settings for robotic arm reach-avoid task, which can be used for evaluating DRL research. We achieved this by utilizing the MuJoCo MJX physics engine and parallelizing both the simulation environment and DRL algorithms using the Brax library. We achieved state-of-the-art results with success rates of 96.1% (UR5e) and 98.8% (Franka Emika Robot) for the reach task and 86.8% (UR5e) and 95.2% (Franka) for the static reachavoid task. Our results indicate that while in previous works DRL agents could solve, for example, a reach task in a simplified setting perfectly, their agents performance collapses when evaluated in realistic scenarios. Overall, this work identifies that additional research is still required to claim the successful resolution of the robotic arm reach-avoid task using DRL. The environment and benchmarking code is available as open source at the following link

</details>


### [179] [Embodied Active Learning under Limited Annotation and Navigation Budget for Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15974)
*Hadrien Crassous,Mohamed Yassine Kabouri,Minahil Raza,Joni Pajarinen,Riad Akrour*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Embodied active learning for object detection under navigation and annotation budgets; uses spatial consistency to identify informative samples; achieves best accuracy in simulation and real-world with YOLOv5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address domain shift of vision models deployed in new environments with limited labeling and navigation budgets; enable on-robot adaptation.

Method: Embodied batch active learning: an agent collects candidate samples within a navigation budget and assigns limited annotation budget to key images; leverages spatial consistency to flag inconsistent labels indicating potential model failures; evaluates several active learning objectives; retrains detector.

Result: Outperforms baselines in detection accuracy under the same budgets on AI2-THOR scenes and real-world Spot robot with YOLOv5; demonstrates effectiveness of spatial inconsistency as a guidance signal; open-source project.

Conclusion: Spatial inconsistency is a useful cue for data selection in embodied active learning; the approach enables adaptive detectors under tight resource constraints; potential for broader deployment with accessible code.

Abstract: This paper studies how to adapt a computer vision object detector to an unknown environment under both a robot navigation time and annotation budget constraint. Our approach selects informative robot trajectories and image samples to retrain the detector, explicitly targeting its failure cases. Formally, the approach is an embodied variant of batch active learning, where at each round an agent has a limited navigation budget to collect candidate samples and a limited annotation budget for the most relevant images. We leverage spatial consistency to identify images with inconsistent labels, which are likely to provide the greatest improvement to the vision model. We evaluate the approach using different active learning objectives on large scenes from the AI2-THOR simulator and on a real-world setup using a Boston Dynamics Spot robot with the real-time object detector YOLOv5. Through comparison against several baselines, our experimental results show that spatial inconsistency helps guide the agent and select relevant images without external supervision, achieving the highest detection accuracy at the end of the adaptation process under the same budget. The open-source project can be found at https://mkabouri.github.io/embodied-active-learning-od

</details>


### [180] [Data and Learning Where it Matters for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15982)
*Oliver Hausdörfer,Linus Schwarz,Gabor Marko,Christian Dietz,Timo Class,Luka Hofer,Jim Yun-Jin Li,Johannes Hechtl,Ralf Römer,Angela P. Schoellig*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: End-to-end learned policies on large datasets remain brittle for contact-rich, high-precision tasks; the paper advocates targeted data collection for the critical contact-rich segment, using automated data collection plus offline RL and traditional planning for free-space, achieving 96% success across four tasks with 2–2.5 hours of data, and strong OOD robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address brittleness and poor generalization in learned policies by introducing structure in data collection, separating the task into a critical contact-rich segment and simpler free-space motion, leveraging planning for the latter.

Method: Automated data collection focused on the contact-rich segment; offline deep reinforcement learning for that segment; traditional planning for free-space motion; no teleoperator skills required; no online policy updates; evaluation on four real-world tasks with 2–2.5 hours data.

Result: Average success rate of 96% vs 55% strongest baseline; strong performance in out-of-distribution scenarios; data-efficient (minutes/hours); real-world tasks; avoids reliance on teleoperator; demonstrates efficacy of targeted data collection and offline RL.

Conclusion: Targeted data collection for contact-rich tasks can enable high-accuracy control in precision applications; reduces need for operator skill and online adaptation; supports generalization to OOD; suggests a practical data-collection paradigm for robotics tasks requiring precision.

Abstract: Learned policies trained end-to-end on large datasets often remain brittle in high-precision tasks and struggle with generalization. We find that these limitations largely stem from a lack of structure and focus in data collection. Our key insight is to leverage dense data collection only for the critical segment of contact-rich tasks and to rely on traditional planning during simple free-space motion. We propose an automated data-collection scheme in combination with offline deep reinforcement learning for the critical segment of the task, eliminating reliance on a teleoperator's skill and on online policy updates. Across four challenging real-world tasks, using only 2 to 2.5 hours of autonomous data collection, we achieve an average success rate of 96%, compared to the strongest baseline at 55%. Notably, performance remains high in out-of-distribution scenarios where end-to-end approaches struggle. Our results pave the way for targeted data collection for contact-rich tasks and for high success rates in precision applications.

</details>


### [181] [A Morphing-Designed Hexarotor Prototype combining Practical Resilience and Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16002)
*Murat Bronz,Mahmoud Hamandi,Elgiz Baskaya,Chiara Gabellieri,Antonio Franchi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Hexarotor prototype 'Opti-Hexa' morphs propeller geometry to achieve single-propeller failure resilience with energy efficiency comparable to a standard star-shaped baseline; a geometric viability region exists, and the complete hardware/software are released as open-source.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the resilience-efficiency trade-off in multirotor designs by enabling continuous morphing of rotor geometry and validating it with data-driven power modeling; promote reproducibility via open hardware/software.

Method: Develop a morphing hexarotor platform and vary the angles between adjacent propellers across a continuous range. Build a data-fitted empirical power model and evaluate resilience by comparing position accuracy and rotational kinetic energy during propeller failure to nominal hovering.

Result: Identified a geometric viability region where resilience to single-propeller failure is achieved without the aerodynamic efficiency losses typical of resilient designs; the energy efficiency matches that of a standard star-shaped prototype; all hardware and software are released open-source.

Conclusion: Morphing rotor geometry can simultaneously provide practical resilience and energy efficiency, with a defined geometric region enabling this trade-off; open-source platform facilitates further research.

Abstract: This work demonstrates experimentally the existence of a hexarotor prototype, termed Opti-Hexa, that simultaneously achieves practical resilience to single-propeller failures and energy efficiency comparable to a standard Star-shaped prototype with the same size, weight, hardware and software. Leveraging a novel open-source morphing platform, we investigate the trade-offs across a continuous range of geometries by varying the angles between adjacent propellers. We study practical efficiency through a data-fitted empirical power model and evaluate practical resilience by comparing the position accuracy and rotational kinetic energy during failure to those observed under nominal hovering conditions. Our experiments confirm the existence of a geometric viability region for this specific morphing platform, where resilience is ensured without the aerodynamic efficiency losses typically associated with practically resilient designs found in the state of the art. The complete hardware and software of the morphing platform are released to support further research.

</details>


### [182] [Let the Body Follow: Coupled Egocentric Control for Whole-Body Robot Teleoperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16095)
*Tsung-Chi Lin,Yichen Xie,Chien-Ming Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A body-following teleoperation framework maps operator head and hand motions to torso/base adjustments, improving whole-body teleoperation efficiency and perceived workload over a baseline interface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce cognitive and motor load in whole-body teleoperation by offloading torso/base control to automatic responses to the operator's natural head and hand movements, enabling more intuitive, gaze- and hand-driven interaction.

Method: Introduce coupled egocentric control where the robot’s torso and base automatically respond to head and arm motions: head pitch adjusts torso height, head yaw drives base rotation, end-effector height adjusts torso motion, and end-effector workspace boundaries trigger base translation. Evaluation conducted via a user study with a TIAGo mobile manipulator on home-care-inspired tasks, comparing against a baseline hybrid interface.

Result: Compared with the baseline, the approach improves object manipulation efficiency, reduces button-based control effort and arm singularities, lowers mental demand and overall workload, and increases ease of use, ease of learning, confidence, and user preference for torso and base control.

Conclusion: Coupled egocentric control provides a more intuitive, scalable solution for whole-body teleoperation by leveraging body-following semantics, reducing explicit control commands for torso/base, and enhancing user experience and task performance in home-care tasks.

Abstract: Whole-body teleoperation requires users to coordinate perception, manipulation, posture, and mobility across multiple robot components. This coordination is difficult because users must simultaneously control the robot's head, arms, torso, and base while maintaining task awareness and avoiding kinematic or environmental constraints. In this paper, we propose coupled egocentric control, a body-following teleoperation approach in which the robot's torso and base automatically respond to the operator's head and arm motions. Rather than requiring explicit touchpad commands for every torso or base adjustment, the system lets users focus on gaze and hand control: head pitch adjusts torso height, head yaw drives base rotation, end-effector height adjusts torso motion, and end-effector workspace boundaries trigger base translation. We evaluate this approach in a user study on whole-body teleoperation of a TIAGo mobile manipulator for home-care-inspired tasks. Compared with a baseline hybrid interface, coupled egocentric control improves object manipulation efficiency, reduces button-based control effort and arm singularities, lowers mental demand and overall workload, and increases ease of use, ease of learning, confidence, and user preference for torso and base control.

</details>


### [183] [BayesContact: Uncertain Pose Estimation via Visuo-Tactile Proposals and Simulation-based Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16123)
*Aditya Kamireddypalli,Matias Mattamala,Joao Moura,Russell Buchanan,Sethu Vijayakumar,Subramanian Ramamoorthy*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: BayesContact uses simulation-based inference to fuse depth with force/torque-derived contacts for visuo-tactile pose estimation in peg-in-hole, maintaining a particle belief and using forward models to compute observation likelihoods; active probing guides disambiguation; shows ~30% improvement over vision-only methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Limitations of depth-only sensing and costly offline retraining for contact-rich manipulation; need a data-efficient, generalizable framework that leverages visuo-tactile cues and simulation to adapt to new geometries.

Method: Particle-based belief over pose; for each pose, a renderer predicts depth; a physics simulator predicts contact outcomes under guarded probing; likelihoods are computed against real depth and contact observations to update the posterior; information-gain-based probing selects actions to disambiguate; results validated in simulation and on real robots.

Result: BayesContact improves pose observability and insertion success by about 30% compared to vision-only inference across varied geometries and real-robot experiments.

Conclusion: A simulation-based visuo-tactile inference framework with active probing offers robust, data-efficient pose estimation for peg-in-hole tasks and reduces reliance on offline retraining; the approach generalizes to other contact-rich manipulation problems.

Abstract: Contact-rich manipulation requires pose estimates that are often more accurate than what depth-only sensing provides. Existing methods, relying on vision and contact, employ costly offline training procedures that need to be retrained for new environments and geometries. We propose BayesContact, a Simulation-Based Inference framework for visuo-tactile pose estimation in peg-in-hole insertion. BayesContact maintains a particle belief over object pose and fuses depth observations with force/torque-derived contact evidence. We employ simulation based forward models to approximate these observation likelihoods. For each pose hypothesis, a renderer predicts depth measurements and a physics simulator predicts contact outcomes under guarded probing actions; both are scored against real observations to update the belief. The resulting multimodal belief also enables information-gain-based probing for active disambiguation. Across simulated geometries and real-robot experiments, BayesContact improves pose observability and insertion success over vision-only inference by 30%

</details>


### [184] [A New Implementation of NeoSLAM and a Comparative Evaluation with RatSLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16143)
*Joao Victor T. Borges,Fabio Coelho,Paulo Padrao,Jose Fuentes,Ramon R. Costa,Liu Hsu,Leonardo Bobadilla*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ROS2-based modular rewrite of NeoSLAM achieves real-time throughput with map reconstruction quality comparable to RatSLAM across three datasets and varying conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address real-time performance and modularity for NeoSLAM while providing a fair comparison to RatSLAM across diverse environments.

Method: Rewrite NeoSLAM into a modular ROS2-based architecture and evaluate its real-time execution on three datasets under varying environmental conditions, comparing against RatSLAM with respect to throughput, mapping consistency, and trajectory reconstruction.

Result: The new NeoSLAM achieves higher processing throughput enabling real-time operation; map reconstruction performance is comparable to RatSLAM; some differences in mapping consistency and trajectory reconstruction observed across datasets.

Conclusion: A ROS2-based modular NeoSLAM is practically viable, delivering real-time performance with map-quality on par with RatSLAM and robust across diverse environments.

Abstract: This paper presents a new implementation of the NeoSLAM algorithm. The proposed version is a complete rewrite of NeoSLAM into a modular architecture using modern frameworks that, together, enable real-time execution with minimal discarding of input data. This work also provides a comparative evaluation between NeoSLAM and RatSLAM across three datasets under varying environmental conditions. The experimental results highlight differences in mapping consistency and trajectory reconstruction, demonstrating the effectiveness and practical applicability of the proposed ROS2-based implementation. The results indicate that the new NeoSLAM outperforms the original in terms of processing throughput for real-time applications and achieves comparable performance to RatSLAM in terms of map reconstruction across the evaluated datasets.

</details>


### [185] [VTLoc: Learning-based Tactile Contact Localization in Visual Point Clouds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16146)
*Zhiyuan Wu,Zhuo Chen,Shan Luo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VTLoc is a visual-tactile framework that localizes touch points on object surfaces by aligning tactile readings with a 3D visual point cloud, using a geometric multi-modal alignment module and an iterative updater; evaluated on 100 real-world objects, it reduces local-to-global ambiguity in single-touch contact localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the challenge of accurate spatial alignment between tactile data and 3D visual geometry, enabling precise contact localization by fusing vision and touch, which provides global context and local precision respectively.

Method: VTLoc introduces two components: (1) a geometric multi-modal alignment module that reconstructs a pseudo-point cloud from fused visual-tactile features and aligns it with the visual point cloud to enforce spatial consistency; (2) an iterative localizing updater that refines the contact location through successive refinements using fused features, operating on a 3D point cloud as visual input and a tactile reading.

Result: The approach improves single-touch contact localization by reducing local-to-global correspondence ambiguity, demonstrated on a new benchmark of 100 real-world objects.

Conclusion: Explicit cross-modal alignment and iterative refinement between vision and touch can enhance contact localization, enabling more reliable tactile-vision fusion for robotic perception and manipulation.

Abstract: Vision and touch are complementary modalities essential for robotic perception and manipulation. While vision provides global object context, touch offers precise local information at contact points. Integrating these modalities for contact localization, i.e., predicting the location of touch on an object's surface, poses significant challenges due to the need for accurate spatial alignment between tactile data and visual geometry. To address this challenge, we propose VTLoc, a novel visual-tactile framework that localizes contact points from tactile readings using a 3D point cloud as visual input. VTLoc introduces two key components: a geometric multi-modal alignment module, which reconstructs a pseudo-point cloud from fused visual-tactile features and aligns it with the visual point cloud to enforce spatial consistencies across modalities; and an iterative localizing updater, which iteratively refines the predicted contact location using fused visual-tactile features. Evaluated on a new benchmark of 100 real-world objects, VTLoc improves single-touch contact localization by reducing local-to-global correspondence ambiguity.

</details>


### [186] [Vision-Language-Motion Maps: An Open-Vocabulary, Uncertainty-Aware, Queryable Motion Attribute for 3D Scene Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16173)
*Dibyendu Ghosh,Ayushi Shakya*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLMM proposes an open-vocabulary 3D map where each element stores a fused semantic movability prior, observed cross-frame motion, and per-element uncertainty, enabling natural-language queries about what moves, what could move, and what stays still.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-vocabulary 3D maps currently answer questions about what/where but struggle with dynamic behavior. There is a need to combine semantic priors with observed motion and quantify uncertainty to support motion queries under language guidance.

Method: Introduce Vision-Language-Motion Maps (VLMM): each map element carries a fused motion attribute (semantic movability prior from VLM/LLM, observed motion, and uncertainty). Queries become attribute filters distinguishing moving, could-move, and static. Validated via ablations on AI2-THOR simulator (exact ground truth across three scene types) and real RGB-D sequences (TUM, Bonn). Isotonic calibration applied to obtain ECE ~0.10.

Result: Ablation shows semantic-only baselines fail motion queries; motion fields alone cannot replace semantic priors or observed motion. On real data, the uncertainty channel improves moving-vs-static AP and reduces false motion flags, robust to pose noise. Raw confidences are not calibrated, but isotonic calibration yields ECE ~0.10.

Conclusion: VLMM represents a comprehensive, open-vocabulary, language-queryable 3D map with fused prior and observed motion plus per-element uncertainty, addressing gaps left by prior maps that lack at least one of these properties.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary 3D maps let robots answer language queries about what and where, but they assume a static world and cannot answer queries about how scene elements behave. We introduce Vision-Language-Motion Maps (VLMM), an open-vocabulary, natural-language-queryable 3D map in which each element carries a fused motion attribute: a VLM/LLM semantic movability prior combined with geometrically observed cross-frame motion, together with a per-element uncertainty. Queries reduce to attribute filters that distinguish what has been seen to move, what could move but has not, and what stays still. On a controlled simulator benchmark with exact ground truth (AI2-THOR, three scene types) we show through ablation that the schema fields are non-substitutable: a semantic-only baseline fails motion queries even with strong features, and neither motion field substitutes for the other (the prior cannot answer "what is moving," observed motion cannot answer "what could move"). On real dynamic RGB-D (TUM and Bonn, six sequences) we show the uncertainty channel-our key difference from prior fused-motion work-consistently improves moving-vs-static average precision and reduces false motion flags, and is robust to estimated (noisy) poses. The raw confidence is not calibrated, but post-hoc isotonic calibration reaches an expected calibration error of 0.10. VLMM is a representation contribution: the closest prior maps each lack at least one of the four properties-open-vocabulary, language-queryable, fused prior-and-observed motion, and per-element uncertainty-that our combination provides.

</details>


### [187] [Handroid: Bridging Dexterous Hand and Humanoid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16187)
*Ruogu Li,Chenyang Ma,Sikai Li,Zhenyu Wei,Yunchao Yao,Haochen Shi,C. Karen Liu,Shuran Song,Mingyu Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Handroid is a compact desktop-scale robot that reuses a single 27-DoF body as either a dexterous hand or a humanoid, enabling both manipulation and locomotion within a unified control/learning framework; validated on dexterous manipulation, RL-based locomotion, keyframe deployment, and long-horizon embodiment reconfiguration tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge dexterous manipulation and whole-body locomotion within a single, compact, and reproducible platform, enabling cross-embodiment learning and flexible morphology.

Method: A single 27-DoF electromechanical body reconfigurable into two embodiments: a 20-DoF dexterous hand and a 12-DoF lower-limb humanoid, plus a unified control/learning framework supporting hand teleoperation, dexterous grasping, in-hand manipulation, locomotion, gait generation, and interactive motion authoring.

Result: The system is validated through real-world dexterous manipulation, reinforcement-learning-based locomotion, keyframe motion deployment, and a long-horizon task involving embodiment reconfiguration, locomotion, docking, and dexterous pick-and-place, demonstrating a compact, reproducible platform for morphology-reconfigurable robotics and cross-embodiment learning.

Conclusion: Handroid establishes a compact, morphology-reconfigurable platform that unifies manipulation and locomotion capabilities, enabling cross-embodiment robot learning and advancing morphology-reconfigurable robotics.

Abstract: Dexterous hands and humanoid robots are typically developed as distinct embodiments: the former enable contact-rich manipulation at the object scale, whereas the latter provide mobility and whole-body interaction in human-centered environments. We introduce \textbf{Handroid}, a desktop-scale dual-embodiment robot that integrates both capabilities within a single reconfigurable platform. Handroid reuses one 27-DoF electromechanical body as either a dexterous hand or a desktop humanoid, measuring 0.33 m in height and 2.05 kg in weight. In the dexterous hand embodiment, 20 DoFs form an anthropomorphic hand closely matching the kinematic structure of the human hand. In the humanoid embodiment, the same articulated modules are reconfigured into a humanoid with a head, arms, and legs, including a 12-DoF lower-limb structure for locomotion and whole-body motion. Handroid further provides a unified control and learning framework supporting hand teleoperation, dexterous grasping, in-hand manipulation, humanoid locomotion, gait generation, and interactive motion authoring. We validate the platform through real-world dexterous manipulation, reinforcement-learning-based locomotion, keyframe motion deployment, and a long-horizon task involving embodiment reconfiguration, locomotion, docking, and dexterous pick-and-place. These results position Handroid as a compact and reproducible platform for advancing morphology-reconfigurable robotics and cross-embodiment robot learning.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [188] [GraphDx: A Cost-Aware Knowledge-Enhanced Multi-Agent Framework for Sequential Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15280)
*Shaoting Tan,Ning Liu,Yuntao Du,Shuyue Wei,Wu Shuai,Qian Li,Yanyu Xu,Wei Zhang,Lizhen Cui,Haitao Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GraphDx proposes a knowledge-enhanced Medical Diagnosis Knowledge Graph (MDKG) with a three-agent collaborative framework to improve sequential diagnosis: higher accuracy and substantially lower testing costs across MedQA and MIMIC-IV, robust to multiple LLM backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs encode broad medical knowledge but struggle with systematic, cost-aware reasoning in sequential diagnostics, often leading to unnecessary testing. A knowledge-augmented, interpretable framework is needed to balance diagnostic accuracy with resource costs.

Method: Automatically construct MDKGs with quantized typicality, action-centric topology, and dual-objective attributes for diagnostic relevance and cost-sensitivity. Deploy three collaborative agents: Perception (language understanding/generation), Reasoning (deterministic evidence scoring and cost-aware planning on the MDKG), and Decision (language tasks). Evaluate across MedQA and MIMIC-IV on three LLM backbones (DeepSeek-V3, Kimi-k2, Llama-3.3).

Result: Diagnostic success rates rise from 50–68% to 79–93% across datasets and backbones; test costs reduced by 20–54%, indicating improved accuracy with resource efficiency and interpretability.

Conclusion: GraphDx presents a robust, economical, and interpretable framework that integrates knowledge graphs with cost-aware, multi-agent reasoning to enhance automated clinical diagnosis.

Abstract: Sequential diagnosis requires balancing diagnostic accuracy against resource costs through iterative information gathering. Existing Large Language Model (LLM) approaches exhibit a critical knowledge-reasoning gap: despite encoding extensive medical knowledge, they struggle to reason systematically under cost constraints, often resorting to excessive testing. We propose GraphDx, a knowledge-enhanced framework with two core innovations. First, we design an automated pipeline that leverages LLMs to construct Medical Diagnosis Knowledge Graphs (MDKGs) with quantized typicality, action-centric topology, and dual-objective attributes for both diagnostic relevance and cost-sensitivity. Second, we introduce three collaborative agents (Perception, Reasoning, and Decision) where the Perception and Decision Agents handle language understanding and generation, while the Reasoning Agent performs deterministic evidence scoring and cost-aware planning on the MDKG. Experiments on MedQA and MIMIC-IV across three LLM backbones (DeepSeek-V3, Kimi-k2, Llama-3.3) show that GraphDx improves diagnostic success rates from 50--68% to 79--93% while reducing test costs by 20--54%, providing a robust, economical, and interpretable solution for automated clinical diagnosis.

</details>


### [189] [Causal-Audit: Explicit and Auditable Graph-based Reasoning via Target-Aware Causal Chain Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15281)
*Su Lan,Xuefei Yin,Yanming Zhu,Alan Wee-Chung Liew*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes an explicit, auditable causal reasoning framework for context-free intervention-based QA using a four-stage, graph-based approach with target-aware graph construction and path-level evidence aggregation, achieving robust, interpretable results that outperform prior LLM-based methods on three benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the opacity and fragility of implicit, language-level causal reasoning in existing LLM-based QA, especially under interventions and context-free settings, by delivering an auditable, structured causal reasoning process.

Method: Structured four-stage reasoning over an explicit causal graph, with (1) target-aware graph construction that centers the target variable to suppress irrelevant variables, (2) modular stage-wise reasoning, and (3) path-level evidence aggregation that combines multiple causal paths with reinforcing and counteracting effects.

Result: Empirically outperforms existing LLM-based methods on three benchmarks, while providing interpretable and auditable causal reasoning traces.

Conclusion: The proposed framework delivers explicit, auditable causal reasoning for context-free intervention-based QA, improving robustness and interpretability over end-to-end LLM approaches.

Abstract: Causal and intervention-based question answering is fundamental to advancing large language models (LLMs) toward reasoning beyond surface-level correlations and understanding underlying causal mechanisms. However, existing LLM-based methods often rely on implicit language-level reasoning, resulting in opaque causal assumptions, unverifiable reasoning paths, and fragile predictions under complex interventions, particularly in context-free settings. In this paper, we propose an explicit and auditable causal reasoning framework for context-free intervention-based question answering. Our method formulates causal inference as structured reasoning over an explicit causal graph through four modular stages, rather than implicit end-to-end prediction. A key innovation is a target-aware causal graph construction strategy that treats the target variable as a core constraint during graph expansion, effectively suppressing irrelevant variables, spurious causal relations, and reasoning noise. We further introduce a path-level causal evidence aggregation mechanism that combines multiple causal paths while modeling both reinforcing and counteracting effects, enabling robust decision-making beyond single-chain reasoning. Extensive experiments on three benchmarks demonstrate that our framework consistently outperforms existing LLM-based methods while providing interpretable and auditable causal reasoning traces.

</details>


### [190] [Cura 1T: Specialized Model for Agentic Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15314)
*actAVA AI,:,Haolin Chen,Leon Qi,Steve Brown,Deon Metelski,Tao Xia,Joonyul Lee,Qixuan Wang,Kevin Riley,Frank Wang,Weiran Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Cura 1T demonstrates effective multi-task healthcare capability via a data-centered, human-gated self-evolution loop, achieving top-tier benchmarks across healthcare tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for a unified, high-stakes healthcare LLM that can handle patient consultation, clinical reasoning with text and images, interactive diagnosis, and EHR tool use. Existing models often excel at narrow tasks but degrade when updated, leaving a gap for integrated healthcare workflows.

Method: A training agent plans target capabilities, trains the model, evaluates benchmark trajectories, and refines the data mixture from observed failures in iterative evolution rounds. The loop emphasizes data-centered improvements using synthetic and curated examples rather than single generic medical-data updates.

Result: Cura 1T ranks at or near the top among frontier baselines on the healthcare evaluation suite and remains competitive on out-of-domain reasoning and agentic benchmarks.

Conclusion: A data-centered self-evolution loop can effectively broaden multi-task healthcare capabilities while mitigating task interference, outperforming broad medical-data updates and sustaining performance across diverse healthcare benchmarks.

Abstract: Healthcare spans high-stakes communication, expert reasoning, and workflow execution, yet specialized LLMs that cover these use cases together remain limited. A healthcare model must handle patient consultation, clinical reasoning over text and images, interactive diagnosis, and electronic health record (EHR) tool use. These capabilities fail in different ways, and a narrow update for one task can degrade another. We present Cura 1T, a healthcare-specialized LLM trained through a human-gated self-evolution loop. In each evolution round, a training agent plans a target capability, trains the model, evaluates benchmark trajectories, and refines the data mixture from observed failures. This data-centered loop improves the model through targeted synthetic and curated examples rather than a single generic medical-data update. Across the healthcare evaluation suite, Cura 1T ranks at or near the top among frontier baselines, while remaining competitive on out-of-domain reasoning and agentic benchmarks.

</details>


### [191] [AnovaX: A Local, Multi-Agent Voice Assistant with LLM Planning, Typed Executors, and Adaptive Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15367)
*Raunak B Sinha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A local-first desktop assistant architecture that runs entirely on-device, using a Gemini-powered planner to emit JSON tool plans, a multi-agent orchestrator with per-tool agents, and a recovery loop; includes a local Flask server for phone control and streaming. Demonstrates feasibility of a legible, few-thousand-line assistant that can open apps, type, search, coordinate actions, and be controlled from a phone without LLM keyboard input.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address privacy, latency, and reliability concerns of cloud-based desktop assistants by keeping all processing on-device and using the desktop as the action surface; show practical architecture for multi-agent coordination, safety, and recovery.

Method: Single Python process integrating: wake-word gate, speech pipeline, LLM planner (Gemini) producing a JSON plan of tool calls, a whitelist/denylist safety layer, a multi-agent orchestrator that maps plans to typed child agents (e.g., AppAgent, TypingAgent, BrowserAgent, and six others) with per-agent timeouts, retries, and shared-resource locks; a recursive MetaAgent enabling one-level sub-goaling back to the planner (capped at two levels of nesting); a recovery loop with a compact ReAct-style prompt and speculative execution of read-only tools to hide latency; a companion Flask server exposing a phone-friendly remote over local WiFi, mirroring agent lifecycle events and streaming the laptop screen via MJPEG; demonstration focuses on end-to-end capability rather than competing with Siri/Alexa.

Result: Prototype-level demonstration showing end-to-end local-first operation: opens apps, types into them, runs searches, coordinates concurrent actions, recovers from single-step failures, and can be controlled entirely from a phone without the LLM touching the keyboard; architecture emphasizes legibility and bounded complexity (few-thousand lines).

Conclusion: The work argues that a legible, local-first desktop assistant is feasible and practical, reducing cloud dependency while enabling complex automation through structured multi-agent planning and execution, with design choices such as bounded nesting, safety guards, recovery mechanisms, and remote access serving as a blueprint for privacy-preserving personal assistants.

Abstract: Desktop voice assistants are still dominated by cloud pipelines that ship raw audio off the machine and expose a fixed set of skills. We describe AnovaX, a small local-first assistant that runs entirely on the user's computer and treats the desktop itself as its action surface. A single Python process wires together a wake-word gate, a speech pipeline, an LLM planner (Gemini) that emits a JSON plan of tool calls, a whitelist-and-denylist safety layer, a multi-agent orchestrator that translates each plan into typed child agents on a bounded thread pool, and an adaptive recovery loop that takes over whenever a core step fails. Every tool corresponds to a specialized agent class (AppAgent, TypingAgent, BrowserAgent and six others) with its own timeout, retry policy, and shared-resource locks. A recursive MetaAgent lets the planner delegate a sub-goal back to itself, capped at two levels of nesting. The recovery loop uses a compact ReAct-style prompt and hides Gemini's latency behind speculative execution of read-only tools. A companion Flask server exposes a phone-friendly remote over the local WiFi, mirrors every agent lifecycle event to the phone in real time, and streams the laptop's screen back over MJPEG so the user can watch remote commands land as they run. The point of the project is less to compete with Siri or Alexa than to show that a legible, few-thousand-line assistant is enough to open apps, type into them, run searches, coordinate concurrent actions, recover from single-step failures, and be driven entirely from a phone in another room -- without the LLM ever touching the keyboard.

</details>


### [192] [Precise but Uncoupled: Reviewer Precision Does Not Guarantee Critique Uptake in Multi-Agent Math Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15388)
*Chih-Hsuan Yang,Jingyan Jiang,Vikram Vasudevan,Cheng-Hau Yang,Huihuo Zheng,Le Chen,Eliu A. Huerta,Venkatram Vishwanath,Ian T. Foster,Rajeev Thakur*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Peer-discussion-based critique improves hard problem solving more than a planner-executor-reviewer setup; reviewer precision alone does not ensure helpful critiques, and critique uptake governs gains. Evaluations can overstate system quality if critiques are not acted upon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether a dedicated reviewer stage improves problem solving in hierarchical math/CS agents and to distinguish the impact of reviewer quality from the uptake and use of critiques.

Method: Experiments on 4,181 verifier-grounded Omni-MATH problems using matched GPT-OSS-120B actors. Compare a planner-executor-reviewer (PER) pipeline against broadcast-style peer discussion across problem tiers. Measure reviewer precision, the likelihood that evaluator-verified critique changes the next candidate, and how reviewer guidance is integrated. Examine effects of explicit acknowledgment and embedding reviewer cues in the solver’s working context. Analyze results across difficulty tiers to separate reviewer quality from critique uptake.

Result: Gains appear from tier 4 onward; broadcast-style peer discussion achieves higher final accuracy than PER in the harder regime. PER’s reviewer is more precise (0.861 vs. 0.644) but evaluator-verified useful critique is far less likely to change the next candidate and yields lower reviewer-guided repair. Reviewer detection quality and critique uptake are empirically separable. Forcing explicit acknowledgment lowers final accuracy, while embedding reviewer guidance in the solver’s context partially improves follow-through but does not close the gap.

Conclusion: Reviewer-centric evaluation can overstate system quality: a protocol may detect errors well yet fail to solve more problems if it does not act on those critiques. Effective design requires critiqued feedback to be integrated into subsequent solving steps, and metrics must account for critique uptake, not just reviewer precision.

Abstract: Many math- and science-oriented agent systems use hierarchical designs with specialized reviewer roles, assuming that a dedicated review stage should help turn wrong candidates into correct ones. We test this assumption on 4,181 verifier-grounded Omni-MATH problems using matched gpt-oss-120b actors. Collaboration adds little on the easiest tiers, but from tier 4 onward the gains open sharply; in this harder regime, broadcast-style peer discussion reaches higher final accuracy than a planner-executor-reviewer pipeline (PER). We ask whether this gap is explained by reviewer quality or by whether critique changes the next answer the protocol carries forward. It is not explained by reviewer precision alone: PER's reviewer is more precise than broadcast's (0.861 vs. 0.644), yet evaluator-verified useful critique is much less likely to change the next candidate and produces lower reviewer-guided repair. These results show that reviewer detection quality and critique uptake are empirically separable. Within matched PER interventions, forcing explicit acknowledgment lowers final accuracy, while embedding reviewer guidance directly in the solver's working context partially improves follow-through without closing the gap. Overall, reviewer-centric evaluation can overstate system quality: a protocol may spot errors well yet still fail to solve more problems if it does not act on those critiques.

</details>


### [193] [DrawingVQA: A Real-World Benchmark for Multi-Depth Visual-Textual Reasoning on Construction Drawings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15418)
*Yoonhwa Jung,Junryu Fu,Mani Golparvar-Fard*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduction of DrawingVQA benchmark for evaluating MLLMs on real-world construction drawings; 33 drawings, 92 QA pairs; three reasoning depths; dual categorization framework mapping to 7 engineering and 4 MLLM capabilities; results show large gap to experts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Construction drawings uniquely fuse abstract geometry, symbolic notation, tabular data, annotations, and domain-specific text. This creates a challenging multimodal domain not well covered by benchmarks based on natural images, hindering evaluation of AI systems intended to integrate with engineering workflows.

Method: Curated dataset of 33 Issued for Construction drawings and 92 expert question-answer pairs. Defines three reasoning depths—perceptual understanding, contextual interpretation, and domain-expert reasoning—and presents a dual categorization framework that maps performance to seven construction-engineering capabilities and four MLLM capabilities.

Result: State-of-the-art MLLMs exhibit a substantial gap relative to human experts, especially at higher reasoning depths, indicating current models struggle with domain-specific multimodal reasoning in construction drawings.

Conclusion: The benchmark provides a foundation for domain-specialized multimodal reasoning, enabling progress toward integrating AI-driven understanding with real-world engineering workflows.

Abstract: We introduce DrawingVQA, the first benchmark designed to evaluate multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on real-world construction drawings -- a core media in architecture, civil, and many other engineering practices. Unlike natural images or schematic floor plans, construction drawings fuse abstract geometry, symbolic notation, tabular data, annotations, and domain-specific text, forming a uniquely complex visual-textual domain core to engineering workflows. DrawingVQA bridges this gap with 33 "Issued for Construction" drawings and 92 expertly curated question-answer pairs, spanning three reasoning depths: perceptual understanding, contextual interpretation, and domain-expert reasoning. To evaluate model capabilities, we present a dual categorization framework to jointly analyze performance across seven construction-engineering and four MLLM capability dimensions -- the first to explicitly map engineering workflows to AI reasoning competencies. Evaluations of state-of-the-art MLLMs reveal a substantial gap between model and expert performance, particularly at higher reasoning depths. This benchmark lays a foundation for domain-specialized multimodal reasoning to allow for advancement on integration of AI-driven understanding and real-world engineering workflows.

</details>


### [194] [Do Coding Agents Need Executable World Models, Simplification, and Verification to Solve ARC-AGI-3?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15439)
*Sergey Rodionov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Four nested Codex-based agents dissect which ARC-AGI-3 components drive performance; model strength and reasoning effort dominate, with component effects varying by setting. Verification yields top results but is resource-intensive; hold-out generalization remains untested beyond the public data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To attribute ARC-AGI-3's performance to individual ideas (world modeling, scheduled simplification, and replay verification) by constructing four nested Codex-based agents and evaluating across models and reasoning efforts.

Method: Construct four nested agents: textual baseline; flexible-interface executable world model without replay verification; same with scheduled simplification; fixed-interface verification retaining simplification and requiring exact reproduction of observations. Evaluate across gpt-5.4 and gpt-5.5 (high and xhigh reasoning) on ARC-AGI-3 games; exploratory follow-ups with textual and verification variants using gpt-5.6-sol at xhigh and max.

Result: Across settings, stronger models and greater reasoning effort consistently improve performance; differences among variants are small within a given model-effort setting; the impact of components varies by setting; requiring persistent executable deliverable is not universally beneficial (textual variant sometimes outperforms flexible-interface executable variant in gpt-5.5); simplification improves in three of four model-effort settings; the complete verification treatment ranks first in all four settings but with high resource use; in the gpt-5.6-sol follow-up, verification fully solves all public games at both reasoning efforts, ~99% RHAE, and uses <50% of human baseline actions, though postdated and hold-out generalization remains untested.

Conclusion: Model capability and reasoning effort are the primary performance drivers; component effects are context-dependent. Verification can saturate performance on public data but may not generalize to hold-out cases. Future work should test on hold-out data, analyze resource costs, and explore generalization and transfer to other problem domains.

Abstract: Our previous ARC-AGI-3 agent bundled executable world modeling, scheduled simplification, and exact replay verification, leaving unclear which idea accounted for its performance. We address this attribution question with four nested Codex-based agents: a textual baseline; a flexible-interface executable world model without replay verification; the same executable model with scheduled simplification; and a fixed-interface verification treatment that retains simplification and requires exact reproduction of recorded observations. The main study evaluates all four agents with gpt-5.4 and gpt-5.5 at high and xhigh reasoning effort on the public ARC-AGI-3 games. Exploratory follow-ups evaluate the textual and verification variants with gpt-5.6-sol at xhigh and max. The most robust result is that every agent variant improves with a stronger model and with greater reasoning effort. Within each model-effort setting, differences among variants are smaller than anticipated, while the effects of individual components vary across settings. Requiring a persistent executable deliverable is not universally beneficial: the textual variant outperforms the flexible-interface executable variant in both gpt-5.5 settings. Simplification improves performance in three of the four model-effort settings, with the weakest setting as the only exception. The complete verification treatment ranks first in all four settings, although it uses substantially more resources. In the gpt-5.6-sol follow-up, the verification variant fully solves every public game at both reasoning efforts, achieves about 99% RHAE, and uses fewer than half the total actions of the human baseline. Because the model postdates these games and held-out performance remains untested, this result should be interpreted as saturation of the public set only.

</details>


### [195] [Beyond a Joke: Multi-Angle Reasoning for Detecting and Explaining Harmful Humor in Memes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15442)
*Shanhong Liu,Pai Chet Ng,De Wen Soh,Malika Meghjani,Konstantinos N. Plataniotis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAR-12 uses 12 humor/hate perspectives with role-aware attention and a prototype-based classifier for explainable meme understanding, achieving strong humor and hate detection and providing grounded explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memes often blend humor with harmful content, making interpretation complex and requiring reliable, interpretable reasoning; existing multimodal classifiers struggle to model interdependencies and provide explanation.

Method: Interpret each meme through twelve structured perspectives derived from humor and hate theories; apply a role-aware soft-gated attention mechanism to weight perspectives; use a prototype-based classifier for final prediction; synthesize explanations from perspective-specific reasoning and learned attention weights.

Result: On PrideMM and Memotion, MAR-12 achieves up to 80.3% accuracy in humor detection and 75.9% accuracy in hate detection, outperforming state-of-the-art methods; human and GPT-4-based evaluations indicate explanations are coherent and persuasive, especially when humor and harmful cues co-occur.

Conclusion: MAR-12 demonstrates that VLM-based meme understanding can be made explainable through structured perspectives and attention-guided reasoning, yielding accurate detection and transparent, context-grounded explanations.

Abstract: Internet memes intertwine visual cues, textual content, and cultural context, making them particularly challenging to interpret in scenarios where humor, sarcasm, and harmful intent coexist. These complexities highlight the need for explainable meme understanding systems that can provide reliable and structured reasoning to support both accurate classification and human interpretability. However, existing multimodal classifiers either overlook these interdependencies or provide only limited interpretability. In this paper, we introduce MAR-12, a novel framework that leverages Vision Language Models (VLMs) for meme detection and understanding in settings where humorous and hateful elements may coexist. The framework first interprets each meme through twelve structured perspectives derived from humor and hate theories. It then applies a role-aware soft-gated attention mechanism to learn how much each perspective should contribute, followed by a prototype-based classifier for the final prediction. Finally, explanations are synthesized using both perspective-specific reasoning and learned attention weights, ensuring transparent and context-grounded justifications. We evaluate MAR-12 on the PrideMM and Memotion datasets, where it achieves up to 80.3% accuracy for humor detection and 75.9% accuracy for hate detection, outperforming state-of-the-art approaches. Furthermore, both human and GPT-4-based evaluations confirm that MAR-12 produces coherent and persuasive explanations, particularly for memes in which humorous and harmful cues co-occur.

</details>


### [196] [From Black Box to Executable Logic: Explainable Reinforcement Learning through Prolog Expert Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15459)
*Eduardo C. Garrido-Merchán*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A three-stage post-hoc method converts a trained PPO policy into an executable Prolog program with a logic-engine execution, plus an edit-driven expansion loop that guarantees monotone improvement and termination. Theoretical guarantees include a return-loss bound and a finite-state/proposition fidelity analysis; empirical results show exact optimal returns on discrete tasks and strong but partial fidelity on certain continuous tasks, with clause-efficiency trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Make deep RL policies explainable by translating them into human-readable, verifiable, and editable logic programs while preserving performance where possible.

Method: 1) Extract a frozen PPO teacher; 2) Induce an ordered rule list via relational learning from its decisions and emit as a Prolog program run by a standard logic engine; 3) Expand/edit the rule base, accepting edits only if policy evaluation certifies a return increase. Theoretical guarantees include a return-loss bound in finite MDPs and monotone, terminating expansion; in the continuous setting, analyze propositional-threshold instantiation and lower bounds for oblique decision boundaries.

Result: Discretized task (two-room key-and-door, 16,944 reachable states): expanded Prolog matches exact optimal return in all seeds and beats the stochastic teacher under budget caps in all seeds. Continuous-control tasks: Acrobot (11 clauses) and CartPole (~97% return), LunarLander shows partial recovery consistent with an exponential lower bound on complexity; the Prolog program can substitute the network while staying within noise levels for some tasks.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a viable path to explainable RL through an executable logic-program representation with verifiable guarantees, achieving exact or near-exact fidelity on several tasks and elucidating the trade-offs between expressivity, clause count, and task structure (discrete vs continuous observations).

Abstract: A trained deep reinforcement learning policy is a black box, and we ask whether it can be made explainable by rewriting it as an executable logic program that reproduces its behaviour and that a person can read, a logic engine can run, and an optimizer can edit. We present a three-stage post-hoc transformation that extracts a frozen proximal policy optimization teacher, induces an ordered rule list from its decisions in the manner of classical relational learning, and emits the result as a Prolog program whose every decision is executed by an off-the-shelf logic engine; a subsequent expansion stage edits the rule base and accepts an edit only when policy evaluation certifies a return increase. We prove four guarantees. A return-loss bound makes the distilled program a machine-checkable certificate in a finite Markov decision process, and the expansion loop improves monotonically and terminates. For the continuous-observation setting we answer whether the conversion is possible at all: the propositional threshold instantiation converts the network to arbitrary fidelity as the resolution B grows, with disagreement O(1/B) and a return gap that closes at the same rate, and a matching lower bound shows the cost is exponential in the observation dimension for an oblique decision boundary. Empirically, on a two-room key-and-door task with 16,944 reachable states the expanded Prolog program attains exact optimal return in every seed and, in a budget-capped regime, exceeds the stochastic teacher on exact return in ten of ten seeds. On three continuous-control tasks the emitted program substitutes the network, matching the neural teacher within noise on Acrobot with eleven clauses and recovering about 97% of its return on CartPole, while on the finer-control LunarLander it recovers only partially, exactly the ceiling the exponential lower bound predicts.

</details>


### [197] [A Critical Analysis of Trustworthy AI Tools, Mark Frameworks, and the Implementation Chasms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15480)
*Michael Papademas,Xenia Ziouvelou,Kostas Karpouzis,Vangelis Karkaletsis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Critical diagnostic study of tools and trust-mark frameworks for trustworthy AI (TAI) using OECD data; reveals asymmetries in ethical focus, lifecycle coverage, stakeholders, and tool typology; highlights emphasis on fairness, transparency, and robustness while underemphasizing explainability, security, and sustainability; shows post-development bias; argues for lifecycle-spanning ethics and broader multi-stakeholder governance with actionable recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap between abstract AI ethics principles and practical implementation by empirically evaluating existing tools and trust marks to identify implementation gaps and inform more concrete governance.

Method: Empirical mapping and descriptive comparative analysis of a comprehensive OECD dataset of TAI tools and certifications; assess ethical focus, lifecycle coverage, stakeholder targets, and tool typology.
