Result: Identified significant asymmetries: emphasis on fairness, transparency, and robustness; limited attention to explainability, digital security, and environmental sustainability; concentration on post-development stages; insufficient guidance for design/data collection; underdeveloped education and policy engagement; governance dominated by technical/industry perspectives.

Conclusion: To bridge the theory-practice gap: broaden ethical objectives, embed ethics across the AI lifecycle, and foster broad multi-stakeholder participation; provide holistic, inclusive, and enforceable AI governance with concrete implementation guidance.

Abstract: As artificial intelligence (AI) systems increasingly impact society, ensuring their ethical and trustworthy deployment has become a global priority. While a myriad of high-level ethical guidelines have emerged, criticism persists that these frameworks remain abstract and lack concrete mechanisms for implementation. This paper conducts a critical analysis of tools and trust mark frameworks intended to operationalize trustworthy AI (TAI), drawing on a comprehensive dataset from the OECD. Through empirical mapping and descriptive comparative analysis, we identify significant asymmetries in ethical focus, lifecycle coverage, stakeholder targeting, and tool typology. Our findings show a strong emphasis on fairness, transparency, and robustness, with comparatively little attention paid to explainability, digital security, and environmental sustainability. Moreover, most tools and certifications concentrate on post-development stages, with limited guidance for early design or data collection phases. Educational initiatives and policy engagement are notably underdeveloped, suggesting that current TAI efforts are dominated by technical and procedural measures within industry contexts. We argue that bridging the persistent chasm between AI principles and practice requires expanding ethical objectives, embedding ethics across the AI lifecycle, and fostering broader multi-stakeholder participation. This study provides both a diagnosis of existing implementation gaps and actionable recommendations for advancing more holistic, inclusive, and enforceable AI governance

</details>


### [198] [Logic, Optimization, and Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15532)
*J. N. Hooker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey of how logic and optimization combine to enhance transparency in rule-based AI, covering probabilistic and nonmonotonic logics, many-valued logics, Bayesian/DS frameworks, and computational techniques like decision diagrams and Benders, with emphasis on explainability and future research directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transparency (reproducibility, explainability, trust, fairness) in AI motivates using logic for rule-based encoding and optimization for scalable inference; the paper surveys how these paradigms support transparent reasoning.

Method: Comprehensive survey of logic–optimization partnerships across domains (probabilistic logic, Bayesian logic, belief logics, Dempster–Shafer, nonmonotonic/default logic, many-valued logics, Boolean regression). It discusses computation of projections via decision diagrams and logic-based Benders decomposition, postoptimality analysis for explanations, and the role of optimization in answer set programming modulo theories; and outlines future research directions.

Result: Synthesizes how logic and optimization can jointly contribute to transparent, explainable AI. Maps techniques to transparency goals, identifies practical computation methods (projections, Benders, postoptimality analysis), and highlights ASP modulo theories as a deployment area; provides a cohesive view and gaps for future work.

Conclusion: Future work likely includes deeper integration across logical formalisms, scalable inference for large rule bases, enhanced explainability tools (postoptimality analysis, projections), probabilistic/many-valued logic under optimization, and broader adoption in trustworthy AI systems.

Abstract: Logic and optimization can, in combination, make valuable contributions to rule-based AI. Logic is the obvious medium for encoding a rule base and drawing inferences from it, while optimization provides a powerful technology for computing inferences. Their combination has taken on new relevance amid a growing concern for transparency in AI. which is important for reproducibility, explainability, trustworthiness, and fairness. Rule-based AI provides a natural solution to transparency that is becoming increasingly practical due to today's highly advanced optimization methods. This article surveys several areas of logic-optimization partnership, including probabilistic logic, Bayesian logic, belief logics and Dempster-Shafer theory, nonmonotonic (default) logic, many-valued logics, and inference of logical formulas from noisy data based on Boolean regression. It shows how to compute projections, the fundamental problem of both logic and optimization, using decision diagrams and logic-based Benders decomposition. It describes the use of postoptimality analysis to explain how conclusions are reached, further enhancing transparency, as well as the role of optimization in answer set programming modulo theories. The paper concludes by suggesting possible future research directions.

</details>


### [199] [SeerGuard: A Safety Framework for Mobile GUI Agents via World Model Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15550)
*Xue Yu,Bo Yuan,Pengshuai Yang,Kailin Zhao,Hong Hu,Junlan Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SeerGuard introduces pre-execution, instruction-level screening and action-level risk assessment for mobile GUI agents, enabled by a safety-augmented world model (SAWM) trained via multi-task learning to predict next states and assess safety. It shows substantial safety-utility gains and risk-cost reductions on a VL-Instruction model, and generalizes across GUI agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile GUI agents pose safety risks where a single erroneous action can cause irreversible consequences. Current safety mechanisms are reactive and lack pre-execution risk assessment.

Method: Pre-execution instruction-level screening and action-level risk assessment. Build SAWM by multi-task learning combining semantic next-state prediction with safety risk assessment. Evaluate on Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct with metrics safety-utility and risk-cost at ω=0.8 and α=0.8.

Result: SAWM enables high-level analysis to anticipate risks before execution, improving safety-utility from 0.191 to 0.596 and reducing risk-cost from 0.347 to 0.130 on the target model. Demonstrates generalization across diverse mobile GUI agents.

Conclusion: Pre-execution screening and action risk assessment, underpinned by SAWM, offer effective, generalizable safety improvements for mobile GUI agents, validating the proposed approach.

Abstract: Mobile graphical user interface (GUI) agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in automating complex tasks, yet they introduce critical safety risks where a single erroneous action can lead to irreversible consequences. Existing safety mechanisms are primarily reactive, lacking the ability to assess risks before execution. In this paper, we introduce SeerGuard, a consequence-aware safety framework designed to mitigate these risks through pre-execution instruction-level screening and action-level risk assessment. Specifically, the action-level assessment analyzes agent-proposed actions within current GUI states, anticipating likely outcomes to identify risks before they are executed. To enable these capabilities, we construct a unified safety-augmented world model (SAWM) via multi-task learning, integrating semantic next-state prediction with safety risk assessment. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SeerGuard generalizes effectively across diverse mobile GUI agents. On Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, it increases the safety-utility score from $0.191$ to $0.596$ at $ω=0.8$ and reduces the risk-cost score from $0.347$ to $0.130$ at $α=0.8$. Further analyses on our SAWM validate the effectiveness of the instruction-level screening, alongside the capability of action risk assessment and next-state prediction.

</details>


### [200] [MGDT: MLLM-Guided Diffusion Transformer with Relation-Adaptive Mixture-of-Experts for Multimodal Knowledge Graph Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15592)
*Xu Hou,Meiyu Liang,Wei Huang,Yawen Li,Zhe Xue,Wu Liu,Guanhua Ye,Lei Shi,Kangkang Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MGDT introduces an align-then-diffuse MKGC framework that uses a Relation-Adaptive Semantic Routing MoE to filter modalities, a frozen Multimodal Large Language Model as a semantic anchor to align representations, and a graph-conditioned diffusion transformer to generate missing entities; it outperforms strong baselines on three benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion-based MKGC models typically denoise on raw multimodal features, forcing a single denoiser to perform relation-aware cue selection, cross-modal alignment, and structure-aware entity generation. This leads to noisy, semantically inconsistent conditions and suboptimal completion. An align-then-diffuse strategy with relation-aware routing and semantic anchoring can reduce interference and improve accuracy.

Method: MGDT comprises: (1) a Relation-Adaptive Semantic Routing Mixture-of-Experts (RASR-MoE) that selects relation-relevant multimodal transformation paths and suppresses irrelevant modality interference; (2) a frozen Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) used as a semantic anchor to align routed multimodal representations into a unified latent space; (3) a Knowledge Graph Diffusion Transformer (KGDT) that performs graph-conditioned denoising generation in the aligned space to produce missing entity representations.

Result: Empirical evaluation on three benchmark datasets shows MGDT consistently outperforms strong baselines.

Conclusion: An align-then-diffuse MKGC framework with relation-adaptive routing and MLLM-guided alignment improves completion performance and robustness across datasets.

Abstract: Multimodal Knowledge Graph Completion (MKGC) requires inferring missing entities from structural, textual, and visual cues. Existing diffusion-based MKGC methods usually denoise directly on raw multimodal features. Such a design forces the denoiser to simultaneously perform relation-dependent cue selection, cross-modal semantic alignment, and structure-aware entity generation, which introduces noisy and semantically inconsistent conditions for diffusion and consequently leads to suboptimal completion performance. To address this limitation, we propose MGDT: MLLM-Guided Diffusion Transformer with Relation-Adaptive Mixture-of-Experts (MGDT), a novel MKGC framework built on an align-then-diffuse paradigm. MGDT first employs a Relation-Adaptive Semantic Routing Mixture-of-Experts (RASR-MoE) module to select relation-relevant multimodal semantic transformation paths and suppress irrelevant modality interference. MGDT then uses a frozen Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) as a semantic anchor to align the routed multimodal representations into a unified latent space and reduce cross-modal semantic heterogeneity. Finally, a Knowledge Graph Diffusion Transformer (KGDT) performs graph-conditioned denoising generation in the aligned space to produce the missing entity representation. Experiments on three benchmark datasets show that MGDT consistently outperforms strong baselines.

</details>


### [201] [Neuro-Symbolic AI for LEED compliance: Document-Centric Benchmarking, Deterministic Numeric Checking, and When Multimodal Hurts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15647)
*Aritro De,Juliana Felkner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A neuro-symbolic pipeline for LEED v4.1 BD+C screening using small locally hosted LMs and a numeric checker; 4B model gemma3:4b performs best among tested cores; full system yields 61.6% accuracy; adding images reduces performance; prompts influence results based on project documentation richness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the document-intensive burden of LEED v4.1 BD+C certification by combining a compact, local LLM with deterministic, rule-based checks to screen and verify compliance.

Method: Align project PDFs to LEED credit sections, retrieve evidence using credit-aware keywords, verify with a locally hosted 4B LLM (gemma3:4b) and apply a LEED-specific numeric checker for quantitative thresholds; evaluate on four university buildings (484 PDFs, 153 credit-level decisions); compare against an 8B model (llama3.1:8b); perform ablations (low-res drawings, prompts); analyze impact of ground-truth PASS rate on prompts.

Result: 4B text-only core verifier (gemma3:4b) achieves 67.3% accuracy, outperforming the 8B model; deterministic numeric checker fixes arithmetic errors, boosting EA-p2 accuracy from 50% to 100% and improving other credits with reliable value extraction; full neuro-symbolic setup reaches 61.6% overall accuracy, trailing the best text-only baseline due to extraction failures and conservative qualitative handling; low-resolution drawings (150–300 dpi) reduce accuracy; rubric prompts excel on documentation-rich projects, while chain-of-thought prompts excel on documentation-lean ones.

Conclusion: Within LEED v4.1 BD+C compliance verification over raw project docs, the pipeline and baselines offer a reproducible reference for accuracy and failure modes, indicating when neuro-symbolic integration helps or hinders and highlighting the importance of data quality and prompt strategy.

Abstract: LEED v4.1 BD+C certification remains a document-intensive process that requires reviewers to read hundreds of pages of project evidence and apply credit-specific threshold logic by hand. This paper investigates whether small, locally deployed language models can perform meaningful screening of LEED documentation and how deterministic symbolic components should share that work. A neuro-symbolic pipeline is introduced that aligns project PDFs to LEED credit sections, retrieves evidence with credit-aware keyword signatures, verifies compliance with a locally hosted 4-billion-parameter language model, and applies a LEED-specific numeric checker to quantitative thresholds. Experiments on four university buildings (484 PDFs, 153 credit-level decisions) show that a 4-billion-parameter model (gemma3:4b) is the strongest text-only core verifier, achieving 67.3% accuracy and outperforming a larger 8-billion-parameter model (llama3.1:8b) in this task. The deterministic numeric checker corrects arithmetic errors on key quantitative credits, moving EA-p2 from 50% to 100% accuracy and improving several other credits when required values are reliably extracted. At the same time, the full neuro-symbolic configuration achieves 61.6% overall accuracy, trailing the best text-only baseline due to extraction failures and conservative behavior on qualitative categories. Systematic ablations show that adding low-resolution drawing images (150-300 dpi) consistently reduces accuracy, and that prompt effectiveness depends on the building's ground-truth PASS rate: rubric prompts perform best on documentation-rich projects, while chain-of-thought prompts perform best on documentation-lean projects. Within the specific scope of LEED v4.1 BD+C compliance verification over raw project documentation, this pipeline and its baselines provide an initial reproducible reference point for both accuracy and failure modes.

</details>


### [202] [ToolVerse: Unlocking Massive Environments and Long-Horizon Tasks for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15660)
*Shuaiyu Zhou,Fengpeng Yue,Zengjie Hu,Yuanzhe Shen,Chenyang Zhang,feng hong,Cao Liu,Ke Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ToolVerse scales agentic RL for long-horizon, tool-rich reasoning by auto-building training environments from ~400 MCPs (~4500 tools), designing tasks via tool dependency graphs with Dynamic Unlocking Sampling to create the GUST dataset, and applying a Turn-Aware Relative Advantage credit assignment method; results show strong gains on agentic benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs excel in compact reasoning but struggle to maintain robustness in dynamic real-world environments that require complex tool integration and long-horizon planning; current benchmarks are inadequate for evaluating scalable, tool-rich agentic RL.

Method: 1) Build massive executable training environments from ~400 Model Context Protocols (MCPs) containing ~4500 tools. 2) Design long-horizon tasks via a tool dependency graph and the Dynamic Unlocking Sampling Algorithm to generate the GUST (Graph Unlocking Sampling Tasks) dataset. 3) Introduce Turn-Aware Relative Advantage to address credit assignment in long-horizon agentic RL. 4) Train and evaluate the framework on agentic benchmarks.

Result: Experimental results show a significant boost in long-horizon tool-use performance and robust reasoning for LLM-based agents in dynamic environments across agentic benchmarks.

Conclusion: ToolVerse provides a scalable, tool-integrated RL framework that enables robust long-horizon reasoning and execution in real-world, dynamic settings.

Abstract: While LLM agents demonstrate strong reasoning abilities in compact and well-defined scenarios, they struggle to maintain robustness and effectiveness when faced with large-scale, diverse, and dynamic real-world environments that demand seamless tool integration. To address this gap, we introduce ToolVerse, a comprehensive framework that scales up agentic RL environments and enables agents to perform complex long-horizon reasoning in Tool-Integrated Reasoning (TIR) tasks. First, ToolVerse automatically builds the massive executable agent training environments from nearly 400 real-world Model Context Protocols (MCPs) that contain about 4500 tools. Second, we propose a task design strategy based on a tool dependency graph, utilizing Dynamic Unlocking Sampling Algorithm to generate long-horizon tasks, and produce GUST (Graph Unlocking Sampling Tasks) dataset. Third, to alleviate the credit assigment problem in long-horizon agentic RL, we propose a fine-grained Turn-Aware Relative Advantage algorithm. We conduct extensive Agentic RL training using ToolVerse and evaluate our framework on serveral agentic benchmarks. Experimental results demonstrate that our framework significantly strengthens LLMs' capabilities in long-horizon tool use, achieving a marked performance boost and showcasing robust reasoning within dynamic environments.

</details>


### [203] [S1-Omni: A Unified Multimodal Reasoning Model for Scientific Understanding, Prediction, and Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15686)
*Jiahao Zhao,Junyi Liu,Lifeng Xu,Nan Xu,Qingli Wang,Qingxiao Li,Tianle Chen,Xiaoyu Wu,Yawen Zheng,Zikai Wang,Guanming Liu,Hequn Zhou,Jingyi Wang,Jingyuan Shu,Keqi Wang,Li He,Songyang Diao,Wenhui Xu,Xinyu Ren,Yaqin Fan,Yujin Zhou,Zhanao Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: S1-Omni is a unified multimodal reasoning model for science that integrates data representation, knowledge, and domain-specific decoding to handle 200 tasks, trained on S1-Omni-Corpus, and evaluated across 60 benchmarks with strong performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current scientific AI capabilities are fragmented across domain-specific models, tool-augmented LLMs, and LMs that struggle to jointly reason over heterogeneous data, scientific laws, and expert knowledge; a single coherent model could consolidate these capabilities.

Method: Three-core architecture: (1) unified representation by mapping natural-language instructions and scientific objects (e.g., CIF, SMILES, protein sequences, spectra, scientific images) into a shared space; (2) incorporation of scientific laws and expert knowledge in data construction and training to enable reasoning from evidence; (3) task-specific decoding for diverse applications (property prediction, spectrum-to-molecule generation, protein site/structure prediction, scientific image generation/editing). Training on S1-Omni-Corpus spanning 200 tasks with millions of reasoning samples; evaluation on 60+ scientific benchmarks.

Result: S1-Omni outperforms GPT-5.5 and Gemini-3.1-Pro on most benchmarks and matches or surpasses domain-specific models on several benchmarks.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates a practical path toward unified scientific modeling, showing that a single model can integrate heterogeneous data, laws, and knowledge to perform a wide range of science-related tasks with competitive performance.

Abstract: We present S1-Omni, a unified multimodal reasoning model for scientific understanding, prediction, and generation. AI for Science (AI4S) has advanced significantly through domain-specific models, tool-augmented LLMs, and scientific language models. However, model capabilities remain highly fragmented, limiting the joint modeling of heterogeneous data, scientific laws, and expert knowledge. S1-Omni addresses this gap by consolidating these capabilities into a single, coherent scientific reasoning model. The architecture of S1-Omni is built upon three core components: unified representation of scientific data, natural-world knowledge alignment, and decoding for domain-specific tasks. First, S1-Omni maps natural-language instructions and scientific objects, including CIF, SMILES, protein sequences, spectra, and scientific images, into a shared representation space. Second, it incorporates scientific laws and expert knowledge into data construction and training, enabling the model to reason from scientific evidence. Third, it performs task-specific decoding to support a broad range of applications, including property prediction, spectrum-to-molecular generation, protein site and structure prediction, and scientific image generation and editing. S1-Omni is trained on S1-Omni-Corpus, which covers 200 scientific tasks and contains millions of reasoning samples, and is evaluated on over 60 scientific benchmarks. It outperforms GPT-5.5 and Gemini-3.1-Pro on most benchmarks and matches or surpasses domain-specific models on several benchmarks. Overall, S1-Omni provides a practical path toward unified scientific modeling.

</details>


### [204] [Behavioral Controllability of Agentic Models for Information Extraction: From Fixed Workflows to Reflective Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15715)
*Lujia Zhang,Xingzhou Chen,Hongwei Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Evaluates agentic features (reflection, memory) in LLM-based PDF dataset extraction, comparing fixed workflows to reflective agents and an optimized S2 variant with richer PDF tools and dynamic tool selection, focusing on process-level behavior to understand when agentic components affect task completion and how failure modes inform design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether agent-level components (reflection, memory) provide observable advantages over fixed LLM workflows in complex information-extraction tasks.

Method: Compare three conditions: a fixed workflow baseline, reflective agent variants, and an optimized S2 agent using richer PDF tools with dynamic tool selection. Assess process-level behaviors (tool executions, retries, reflection, memory usage, runtime, failure recovery) and treat extraction coverage/field completeness as secondary outcomes.

Result: Agentic mechanisms can induce changes in system behavior; their impact on task completion is context-dependent, with some configurations improving or hindering performance. Failure modes reveal design flaws and motivate the optimized S2 configuration.

Conclusion: Process-focused evaluation under a common harness is essential to determine when reflection and memory are beneficial. Findings suggest adopting richer toolsets and dynamic tool selection for improved robustness and efficiency in agent-based information extraction.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly used for complex information-extraction tasks, yet it remains unclear whether agentic components such as reflection and memory lead to observable and controllable improvements over fixed LLM workflows. We study this question through conference-paper dataset extraction, where a system must identify datasets mentioned in scholarly PDFs and produce structured records. We compare a fixed workflow baseline with reflective agent variants and specify an optimized agent condition (S2) that extends the same task with richer PDF tools and dynamic tool selection. Our evaluation emphasizes process-level behavior--including tool execution, retries, reflection, memory use, runtime, and failure recovery--while treating extraction coverage and field completeness as secondary outcome measures. The paper characterizes when agentic mechanisms change system behavior, whether these changes improve task completion, and how the observed failure modes motivate an optimized agent design under the same evaluation harness.

</details>


### [205] [NeurOWL: An LLM-Based Neural-symbolic Framework for Incomplete OWL Ontology Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15776)
*Hui Yang,Jiaoyan Chen,Yiping Song,Renate Schmidt,Wen Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces NeurOWL, a neuro-symbolic framework for handling subsumption reasoning in incomplete OWL ontologies by verifying candidate subsumptions and abducting missing axioms, with LLMs and ontology embeddings, showing strong performance across domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world ontologies are often incomplete, making subsumption reasoning and justification difficult. The work seeks to verify semantically plausible subsumptions and generate explanations with missing axioms, unifying verification and abduction without a predefined candidate axiom set.

Method: End-to-end neuro-symbolic system NeurOWL that jointly performs subsumption verification and ontology abduction, integrating formal OWL semantics with textual semantics from Large Language Models and ontology embeddings to produce explanations of missing axioms.

Result: Empirical evaluation on real-world ontologies across multiple domains shows strong, robust performance and generalizability of NeurOWL.

Conclusion: NeurOWL effectively addresses incomplete ontology subsumption reasoning by combining verification and abductive explanation within a single neuro-symbolic framework, validated by cross-domain experiments.

Abstract: OWL ontologies provide a formal knowledge representation framework that enables semantic reasoning, and have been widely adopted across domains such as healthcare and bioinformatics. In practice, however, real-world ontologies are often incomplete, which pose challenges for reasoning. In this work, we focus on a fundamental subsumption reasoning problem: given an incomplete ontology and a candidate (non-entailed) subsumption, determine whether the subsumption is semantically plausible and, if so, providing a logically sound explanation containing potential missing axioms. This task unifies subsumption verification with ontology abduction, and generalizes the latter by removing the need for a predefined candidate set of missing axioms. To address this subsumption reasoning problem, we propose NeurOWL, an end-to-end neuro-symbolic framework that jointly performs verification and abduction, leveraging both formally defined semantics and textual semantics through Large Language Models and ontology embeddings. We evaluate NeurOWL on real-world ontologies across multiple domains, demonstrating strong and robust performance across different domains.

</details>


### [206] [AgentFAIR: A Multi-Agent Collaborative Framework for FAIRness Evaluation of Geospatial Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15781)
*Ming Chen,Pranav Pai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentFAIR is a multi-agent framework that uses 13 sub-principle LLM evaluators to assess FAIR compliance of geospatial datasets, revealing substantial disagreement across evaluators, partial alignment with baselines, and promising yet limited validation by experts and cost-efficient auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address inconsistent, non-auditable FAIR evaluations across geospatial repositories and to develop a reproducible, evidence-based auditing framework.

Method: Combine structured metadata extraction with 13 sub-principle-specific LLM evaluators, each producing a 0–3 maturity score, cited evidence, and recommendations; a critic checks evidence and consistency and can trigger re-evaluation; evaluate 50 datasets from 10 repositories; compare against four baseline tools; perform repeated runs to assess agreement; conduct a preliminary 15-dataset expert study; analyze API cost per dataset.

Result: Mean scores: Findability 79.7%, Accessibility 70.4%, Interoperability 45.3%, Reusability 72.0%; rank correlations with baselines 0.31–0.61; not statistically significant; sub-principle agreement 89% (SD 3 pp) with critic vs 71% without; Fleiss' kappa 0.71 and 82% alignment with expert consensus; API cost ≈ $0.054 per dataset; limitations include limited benchmark, incomplete ablations, and single-model-family validation.

Conclusion: AgentFAIR demonstrates auditability and feasibility for automated FAIR assessments but cautions that accuracy and generalization claims are constrained by the benchmark size, ablations, and validation scope.

Abstract: Geospatial datasets support applications from urban planning to climate modeling, yet consistent assessment of FAIR compliance is difficult. Existing evaluators use different rubrics and evidence sources and may fail on JavaScript-rendered pages or repository-specific identifiers. For 50 datasets from 10 repositories, the standard deviation of normalized scores across available tools averages 15.0 percentage points and reaches 30.3 for one dataset. Because these outputs are not equivalent measurements, we use them to characterize disagreement and failure modes, not comparative accuracy. We present AgentFAIR, a multi-agent framework combining structured metadata extraction with 13 sub-principle-specific LLM evaluators. Each produces a 0-3 maturity score, cited evidence, and recommendations; a critic checks evidence and consistency and can request targeted re-evaluation. Mean Findability, Accessibility, Interoperability, and Reusability scores are 79.7%, 70.4%, 45.3%, and 72.0%. Rank correlations with four baseline tools range from 0.31 to 0.61; the FAIR-enough comparison is not statistically significant. On a 10-dataset repeated-run subset, sub-principle agreement averages 89% (standard deviation: 3 percentage points), versus 71% without the critic. A preliminary 15-dataset expert study yields Fleiss' kappa of 0.71 and 82% alignment with expert consensus. API cost is approximately USD 0.054 per dataset. These results support auditability and feasibility, while the limited benchmark, incomplete ablations, and single-model-family validation constrain claims about accuracy and generalization.

</details>


### [207] [Knowledge-Centric Agents for Workflow Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15845)
*Zhendong Li,Lei Sun,Ruibo Ming,He Zhang,Danda Pani Paudel,Luc Van Gool,Jinjin Gu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a knowledge-centric framework for multi-level reasoning in workflow generation; uses inversion, injection, and reversible inference to produce more diverse and executable workflows than text-to-JSON baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based workflow generation with text-to-JSON tends to be structurally brittle and lacks the experiential, hierarchical knowledge needed for effective modular design.

Method: 1) Knowledge inversion to distill hierarchical representations from real workflows; 2) Knowledge injection via supervised fine-tuning to map task descriptions to strategies and executable structures; 3) Reversible inference with self-refinement to synthesize coherent, executable workflows.

Result: Generated workflows exhibit richer node diversity, more coherent structures, and higher execution success rates compared with existing systems.

Conclusion: Establishes a knowledge-driven foundation for agentic, interpretable workflow generation in modular visual systems.

Abstract: Workflow generation in visual creation systems such as ComfyUI demands not only syntactic accuracy but also expert-level reasoning over modular compositions. Existing large language model (LLM) approaches often treat this as a direct text-to-JSON generation task, struggling with structural brittleness and lacking the experiential knowledge required for effective design. We argue that successful workflow generation requires modeling knowledge itself, including its structure, hierarchy, and reasoning dynamics. To this end, we propose a knowledge-centric framework that learns to invert, inject, and infer with knowledge across multiple abstraction levels. We first perform knowledge inversion to distill hierarchical representations, ranging from full pseudo-codes and skeletons to high-level strategies, from large collections of real-world workflows. We then conduct knowledge injection through supervised fine-tuning, teaching the model to reason from task descriptions to strategies and from strategies to executable structures. During inference, the model performs reversible reasoning to synthesize executable workflows, augmented by self-refinement for structural coherence. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method produces workflows with richer node diversity, more coherent structures, and higher execution success rates than existing systems, establishing a new foundation for knowledge-driven, agentic workflow generation.

</details>


### [208] [DSWorld: A Data Science World Model for Efficient Autonomous Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15901)
*Zherui Yang,Fan Liu,Hao Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DSWorld introduces a Data Science World Model and a practical framework to predict environment transitions for data science workflows, reducing expensive computations; it enables faster RL-based training (~14x) and search-based inference (3–6x) while achieving strong transition-prediction performance (outperforming a strong LLM baseline by 35.6%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous data science agents rely on trial-and-error workflows that incur high computational costs. There is a need to anticipate the effects of data science operations before real execution to accelerate learning and inference.

Method: DSWorld constructs a structured representation of the environment state, employs cost-aware operation routing, enables lightweight real execution, and uses an LLM-based simulator for expensive operations. Training relies on an 8K-scale transition trajectory dataset. Reflective World Model Optimization (RWMO) is introduced as an error-aware reinforcement learning strategy to improve transition prediction.

Result: Empirical results show approximately 14x acceleration for RL-based agent training and 3–6x acceleration for search-based inference, while maintaining competitive performance. The DSWorld approach outperforms the strongest LLM baseline by 35.6% on transition prediction tasks.

Conclusion: DSWorld provides a practical, scalable framework for anticipating data science workflow outcomes, yielding substantial speedups without sacrificing performance. The authors provide code and dataset resources for reproducibility.

Abstract: Despite strong capabilities in data understanding and decision-making, autonomous data science agents still heavily rely on trial-and-error workflows that involve expensive computation. This bottleneck motivates models that can anticipate the effects of data science operations before real execution. In this paper, we introduce the concept of Data Science World Model, which model the data science execution environment by predicting environment state transitions conditioned on current workflow states and candidate operations. We further propose DSWorld, a practical framework that combines structured state construction, cost-aware routing, lightweight real execution, and an LLM-based simulator for expensive operations. To support training, we construct an 8K-scale transition trajectory dataset and introduce Reflective World Model Optimization, an error-aware reinforcement learning strategy for improving transition prediction. Experiments show that DSWorld accelerates RL-based agent training by approximately $14\times$ and search-based inference by approximately $3$-$6\times$ while maintaining competitive performance, and outperforms the strongest LLM baseline by 35.6% on transition prediction tasks. The code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DSWorld.

</details>


### [209] [A Formally Grounded ODRL Evaluator: Implementation and Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15987)
*Jaime Osvaldo Salas,Paolo Pareti,Adeel Aslam,Christopher Maidens,George Konstantinidis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Formal semantics for ODRL evaluation; introduces the first ODRL Evaluator with semantics, supporting all rule types, with static and streaming evaluation, performance analysis, and a comparative survey against existing evaluators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ODRL currently lacks a unified formal semantics for policy evaluation, leading to divergent implementations and interoperability gaps in data access policy enforcement and monitoring across European dataspaces.

Method: Grounded on an existing ODRL semantic model, formalizes evaluation problems for access control and monitoring in both static and streaming settings; develops a novel, efficient evaluation algorithm and implementation; builds the ODRL Evaluator with transparent semantics and comprehensive rule-type support; conducts performance experiments across policy complexity and data sizes; provides a comparative review of current evaluators to map feature support and evaluation modes.

Result: First ODRL Evaluator with transparent formal semantics that supports all rule types; provides an efficient algorithm and implementation; empirical performance measurements and scalability analysis; comparative review highlighting differences with state-of-the-art evaluators in features and evaluation modes.

Conclusion: The work delivers a formal, interoperable evaluation mechanism for ODRL, enabling consistent results across systems and setting a baseline for future evaluators; it enhances governance and policy enforcement capabilities in European dataspaces by improving interoperability and reliability of policy evaluation.

Abstract: The ODRL policy language is emerging as the de-facto standard for policy modelling data access and usage preferences, AI governance policies and data workflows in European dataspaces. The current standard has no mathematical formal semantics to describe how a system should implement policy evaluation. This has resulted in a variety of systems and tools that implement their own interpretation of the language, which limits interoperability and cannot guarantee consistent results. Based on an existing semantic model of ODRL, we formalise the problems of ODRL evaluation for the access control and monitoring scenarios, in both static and streaming settings, and we provide a novel, efficient algorithm and implementation. We present the first ODRL Evaluator with transparent formal semantics and supporting all rule types. We experimentally measure its performance, analysing different scalability dimensions related to policy complexity and size of the data on which a policy is evaluated. We compare our system with the state-of-the-art by providing a comparative review of existing ODRL evaluators, which highlights the differences in supported ODRL features and evaluation modes.

</details>


### [210] [Closing the AI Trust Gap: The Case for Independent Certification for Trustworthy AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15992)
*Trisevgeni Papakonstantinou,Cansu Canca,Farah Nanji,Waheedullah Pardess,Jen Weedon,Jasmijn Remmers,Eliza Krigman,Matthew Ball,Yalda Daryani,Kiran Iqbal,Francielle Vargas,María Llorente Sánchez,Joe Humphreys,Fendi Tsim,Kelly Fitzpatrick,Jeff Dunn,Catherine Feldman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Independent, outcome-based AI certification to signal trustworthy real-world performance and close the trust gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a 'trust gap': responsible AI (internal, process-focused) does not yield independently verifiable signals of trustworthy outcomes accessible to consumers, regulators, and shareholders. Market inefficiencies prevent recognition of trustworthy systems due to: (1) inability to distinguish trustworthy systems from imitators, (2) evaluation targets models/outputs rather than deployed sociotechnical systems and their outcomes, (3) measurement ecosystems favor avoiding harms over demonstrating benefits. A single framework that combines governance baseline, verifiable positive-outcome evidence, and market signaling is needed.

Method: Comparative review of existing AI governance instruments and certification regimes in healthcare, sustainability, and security; analysis to identify gaps; proposal of an independent, outcome-oriented certification as a connective layer linking governance, evidence, and market signals.

Result: Current governance tools do not integrate a governance baseline, independently verified positive-outcome evidence, and market signaling. This gap motivates a proposed independent, outcome-oriented certification framework intended to measure, compare, and commercially reward trustworthiness in deployed AI systems.

Conclusion: Independent, outcome-oriented certification can close the trust gap and complement regulation and internal governance by making trustworthiness measurable, comparable, and commercially rewarded.

Abstract: Over the past decade, responsible AI (RAI) has produced a substantial body of practice for identifying and mitigating the risks AI poses in high-stakes settings. Yet this work has not produced a market that rewards trustworthiness. Firms that invest seriously in safety, fairness, and oversight cannot consistently prove to consumers, regulators, and shareholders that their systems go beyond the bare minimum of compliance. What is missing is a way for society to recognize or compare the difference. The result is a trust gap: a structural condition in which responsible development efforts happen inside organizations but produce no external, independently recognized and verifiable signal of trustworthy outcomes. We argue this gap is sustained in part because of a focus on responsible AI (a matter of internal process) as opposed to trustworthy AI (a matter of independently verifiable real-world outcomes), and that it persists because of three compounding failures: (1) the market cannot distinguish trustworthy systems from their imitations; (2) evaluation targets models and outputs rather than deployed sociotechnical systems and their outcomes; (3) the measurement ecosystem is oriented toward avoiding harm rather than demonstrating benefit. Reviewing existing AI governance instruments and comparing them to certification regimes in healthcare, sustainability, and security, we show that none integrate a governance baseline, independently verified positive-outcome evidence, and market signaling in a single framework. We propose independent, outcome-oriented certification as the connective layer that can close the trust gap, complementing regulation and internal governance by making trustworthiness measurable, comparable, and commercially rewarded.

</details>


### [211] [SciForge: An AI-Native, Multimodal Workbench for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16038)
*SciForge Team,Zhangyang Gao,Minghao Fang,Yifei Liu,Hanhui Yang,Xinyu Gu,Shixiang Tang,Siqi Sun,Lei Bai,Cheng Tan,Mengdi Liu,Hao Wu,Shuizhou Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SciForge is a multimodal, research-native AI workbench that structures heterogeneous scientific artifacts into an auditable, goal-driven research state via modular agent services, with five pillars: governance, translate-then-reason, evidence governance, collaborative team science, and real-world end-to-end demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of coherent, auditable, and collaborative handling of heterogeneous scientific artifacts (papers, code, datasets, models, etc.) and to enable goal-oriented, auditable research through a modular agent-based platform.

Method: Architected around five pillars: (i) goal-scoped decision governance with review gates; (ii) translate-then-reason routing multimodal inputs through domain translators; (iii) evidence governance linking claims to provenance and audit findings; (iv) collaborative team science with multi-role governance and shared workspaces; (v) real-world end-to-end use cases. Implemented via an Agent Runtime, Workflow Engine, Evidence-DAG audit sidecar, and Scientific Model Router; desktop-first, open-source, with planned mobile and deeper collaboration features.

Result: Demonstrated eight end-to-end user cases, including multi-day agentic research sprints for gene discovery, AI-guided de novo protein design, molecular optimization, and genome-to-BGC discovery, illustrating practical impact and the feasibility of the architecture.

Conclusion: SciForge offers a comprehensive, auditable, multimodal AI workbench for goal-driven scientific research, with open-source availability and a clear roadmap for enhanced collaboration and real-world applications; future work emphasizes deeper team collaboration and broader deployment.

Abstract: Scientific work increasingly spans heterogeneous artifacts -- papers, code, datasets, scientific file formats, model outputs, figures, manuscripts, and team decisions -- yet general-purpose AI assistants rarely preserve these objects as a coherent, auditable research state. We present SciForge, a multimodal research-native AI workbench that reserves the graphical interface for human judgment while search, parsing, model routing, workflow execution, plotting, writing, and presentation generation run as modular agent-accessible services. SciForge is built around five pillars: (i) \emph{goal-scoped scientific decision governance} for \textbf{goal-oriented} research, with review gates and shared review surfaces; (ii) \emph{translate-then-reason} for \textbf{multimodal} input, routing scientific objects through domain translators before the agent reasons; (iii) \emph{evidence governance} for \textbf{auditable} traceability, linking claims to provenance chains and audit findings; (iv) \emph{collaborative team science} for \textbf{collaborative} research, enabling multi-role decision governance, with shared team workspaces planned for future releases; and (v) \emph{real-world application scenarios} for \textbf{practical} impact, demonstrated through eight end-to-end user cases, with flagship demonstrations including multi-day agentic research sprints for gene discovery, AI-guided de novo protein design, molecular optimization, and genome-to-BGC discovery. The system combines a thin interaction layer, contextual research capability patterns, an Agent Runtime and Workflow Engine, an Evidence-DAG audit sidecar and a Scientific Model Router. SciForge currently runs as a desktop application, with mobile supervision support; future releases will deepen team collaboration. The system is open-source and available at https://github.com/AGI4Sci/SciForge

</details>


### [212] [Harmonizing AI Safety Thresholds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16112)
*Wilber Sean Anterola,Matthew Ball,Luis F. Lafuerza,Markov Grey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a methodology to harmonize capability thresholds across frontier AI risk domains to enable verifiable Cross-company comparisons and consistent risk mitigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of common minimum thresholds among frontier AI companies, which hampers verification, comparison, and consistent safety risk mitigation, potentially enabling a race to the bottom in safety standards.

Method: Develops a harmonization methodology across three risk domains. For misuse risks (cyber and biological), uses expected harm as the key primitive with explicit risk-modeling that accounts for risk channels and model release conditions. For automated AI R&D, thresholds are based on the observed rate of AI progress. The abstract implies a third risk domain, not explicitly described.

Result: A harmonized-threshold derivation framework that integrates explicit risk modeling for misuse and progress-rate-based thresholds for AI R&D, extending prior work and outlining empirical gaps and limitations.

Conclusion: The work advances the literature by providing a method to derive harmonized thresholds and by highlighting empirical gaps and limitations in current data and modeling.

Abstract: Frontier AI companies have published capability thresholds that differ substantially, making it difficult for third parties to verify whether a threshold has been crossed or to compare requirements across companies. Moreover, without common minimum thresholds, risk mitigation may be inconsistent, creating a potential race to the bottom in safety standards. We develop a methodology for deriving harmonized thresholds across three risk domains. For misuse risks (cyber and biological), we take expected harm as the key primitive and use an explicit risk-modeling approach that accounts for risk channels and model release conditions. For automated AI R&D, we base our proposed threshold on the observed rate of AI progress rather than expected harm. Our analysis expands upon prior work and highlights existing empirical gaps and limitations.

</details>


### [213] [CRAFT: Clustering Rubrics to Diagnose Weak LLM Capabilities and Generate Targeted Fine-Tuning Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16122)
*Vipul Gupta,Zihao Wang,Razvan-Gabriel Dumitru,MohammadHossein Rezaei,Aakash Sabharwal,Yunzhong He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CRAFT converts rubric-based evaluations into model-specific capability diagnoses, enabling targeted finetuning through a hierarchical capability tree and outperforming prompt-based and random baselines in finance and legal domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations identify where models fail but not why, lacking guidance for targeted data generation; need diagnostics that map failures to underlying capabilities for systematic improvement.

Method: Treat each rubric criterion as a capability probe; extract capability description from each prompt-rubric pair; cluster descriptions into hierarchical capability tree; score target model at every node; dynamically select low-performing nodes across levels; generate targeted supervised finetuning data focusing on these capabilities; compare against EvalTree clustering and untargeted data across multiple models/domains; hold data/finetuning/evaluation constant; evaluate on held-out benchmarks.

Result: In finance, CRAFT achieves strongest domain-average performance across four open-source models under repeated temperature decoding; in legal, strongest for three of four models and within the variance band of best baseline for the fourth; demonstrates that diagnosing at rubric-criterion level yields sharper weaknesses and improved models after targeted finetuning.

Conclusion: Rubric-criterion level diagnosis yields clearer insights into limitations and leads to better-performing models via targeted data generation; CRAFT provides a generalizable framework and shows domain gains, particularly in finance, with good performance in law.

Abstract: Evaluations should do more than measure a models current performance. They should tell us what to fix for the next model iteration and provide a way to generate targeted post training data. Most evaluation pipelines identify weak examples, topics, or categories, but they leave the underlying capability failure implicit: they say where a model fails, not why. We introduce CRAFT, a method that converts any rubric based evaluation dataset into a model specific diagnosis of weak capabilities. CRAFT treats each grading criterion as a capability probe: it extracts a capability description from every prompt rubric pair, clusters these descriptions into a hierarchical capability tree, scores the target model at every node, and selects low performing nodes dynamically across tree levels, at the granularity where each failure is clearest. The selected weak capabilities then direct the generation of targeted supervised finetuning data. Holding the data generation, finetuning, and evaluation setup fixed, we compare CRAFT against prompt level EvalTree clustering and untargeted random generation on four open source models, two professional domains (finance and legal), and 13 held out benchmarks disjoint from the diagnostic data. CRAFT achieves the strongest finance domain average for all four models under repeated temperature decoding; on legal domain, it is strongest for three of four models and remains within the decoding variance bands of the best baseline on the fourth. Diagnosing weaknesses at the level of rubric criteria, rather than prompts or categories, thus yields both a sharper picture of what a model cannot do and measurably better models after finetuning on that diagnosis.

</details>


### [214] [Beyond a Single Direction: Chain-of-Thought Disrupts Simple Steering of Refusal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26772)
*Kia-Jüng Yang,Dominik Meier,Jiachen Zhao,Terry Ruas,Bela Gipp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Refusal in large reasoning models (LRMs) is encoded jointly in residual stream activations and their chain-of-thought (CoT); CoT can reinforce and reconstruct compliance signals, creating a surface-level vulnerability to CoT-targeted interventions while making the model robust to activation-only manipulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how refusal behavior arises in LRMs, especially the role of chain-of-thought (CoT) in reinforcing or transmitting compliance signals, and to assess robustness and potential attack surfaces.

Method: Experiments with DeepSeek-R1-Distill-LLaMA-8B applying activation steering; comparisons between fixed vs removed CoT; a two-stage intervention where the model regenerates CoT under steering; measurement of reversal rates of refusal and the CoT's independent effect.

Result: Refusal depends on CoT: 39% reversal with fixed CoT; 70% reversal when CoT is removed; 94% reversal when regenerating CoT under steering; CoT alone retains 48% effect after steering is removed.

Conclusion: Refusal in LRMs is a joint property of residual activations and CoT; LRMs resist activation-level interventions but expose CoT to surface-level attacks that can carry and reconstruct compliance signals.

Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) generate chain-of-thought (CoT) traces before producing final outputs, introducing a dynamic internal state that may complicate control mechanisms such as refusal. Unlike instruction-tuned LLMs, where refusal is mediated by a single directional subspace, refusal in large reasoning models (LRMs) additionally depends on the CoT. In DeepSeek-R1-Distill-LLaMA-8B, activation steering reverses refusal in only 39% of cases when the CoT is kept fixed, but removing the CoT entirely increases this to 70%, indicating that the CoT actively reinforces refusal. In a two-stage intervention where the model regenerates its CoT under activation steering, refusal is reversed in 94% of cases, while the resulting CoT alone retains 48% of this effect even after steering is removed. This suggests that the CoT can carry and reconstruct the compliance signal independently. These findings indicate that refusal in LRMs is jointly encoded in residual stream activations and CoT. This joint activation makes LRM more robust against activation-level interventions alone, but exposes CoT to a possible alternative surface attack.

</details>
